#3180. Want an iPhone Duo for the holidays? Apple’s latest stock update isn’t reassuring

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

The iPhone Duo likely won't make it into Santa's sleigh this year. Here's what Apple had to say about it.

Apple is unsure when it will have more of its foldable iPhone Duo in stock for customers to purchase ahead of Black Friday and the holiday shopping season.

During Apple’s earnings call on Monday, Nov. 2, Apple’s chief operating officer, Sabih Khan, told investors that the company couldn’t say when supply of its new iPhone Duo would catch up with current demand.

Also: iPhone Duo hands-on: Some magical touches

“iPhone Duo is constrained as of today … We’re working hard to get this revolutionary new product into the hands of our customers as quickly as possible. But at this time, it’s really hard to predict when supply and demand will be in balance,” Khan told investors.

The stock and shipping delays were among the broader concerns on an earnings call riddled with questions about rising memory chip costs driven by the AI industry, according to reports from TechCrunch.

Also: iPhone Duo shortage watch: Changing wait times, buying limits, launch-day odds

Last month, the iPhone Duo (Apple’s priciest smartphone to date) sold out shortly after its official launch on Oct. 23. Apple told the Financial Times that demand for the new Duo was “nothing short of extraordinary” after the smashing sell-outs, though veteran techies like Bloomberg’s Mark Gurman posited that Apple knew it would encounter supply chain issues, yet it was still the plan all along.

Those who didn’t initially jump to preorder the new device or wait in line at an Apple store are still facing shipping times that extend into 2027. Some outlets are even reporting that carriers like Verizon have offered gift cards as apologies for the extended delays.

Apple CEO John Ternus signaled the company’s effort to meet demand for the iPhone Duo, announcing that Apple will be “deepening” its partnership with Perryman, a titanium producer supplying pieces for the device.

Also: iPhone Duo screen cracks: Serious issue or overblown? What we know

“We’ve been thrilled with the early response from reviewers and customers, and we are working hard to get iPhone Duo into customers’ hands as quickly as we can,” Ternus said during his opening remarks on the earnings call.

While the iPhone Duo snafu is a rare event for Apple, which traditionally has a strong reputation for a smooth supply chain, rising demand for memory chips is raising hardware costs across the industry, and it’s something Apple can’t circumvent. On the call, Khan noted that Apple has had to increase prices on numerous products, though he said those decisions were made rather “reluctantly.”

Back in June, MacBooks and iPads saw price increases of up to 25% due to rising hardware and chip costs.

As Apple works to iron out its kinks, customers may be looking elsewhere. Reports from Korea indicate that Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8 sales jumped 20% in the week after the iPhone Duo became available, and recent reports of cracked displays are another strike for Apple.

Also: Want an iPhone Duo before 2027? Try this

So, Santa may not be able to deliver the iPhone Duo this year, but Samsung’s foldables may make it onto Christmas lists across the globe instead.

#3179. I’ve been tracking Garmin prices for years — here are 5 deals I’d be holding out for this Black Friday

The prices of the best Garmin watches have risen considerably in recent years, but if you wait for big sales periods like Black Friday, you can almost always get a great deal on the brand’s products.

Black Friday is now just a few weeks away, and I’m expecting widespread deals on Garmin watches to start appearing soon. There are a few good discounts available already, like the Garmin Fenix 8 dropping to $742 at Amazon at the time of writing — but I think the best is yet to come.

I’ve been testing Garmin watches for a decade and tracking their prices during sales like Black Friday, and I’ve picked five I expect to see heavily discounted in the coming weeks.

Garmin launched two new entry-level running watches this year: the Forerunner 70 and Forerunner 170; the latter is a direct replacement for the Forerunner 165.

That means the Forerunner 165 is set to be a star of upcoming sales, and indeed it’s already reduced from $249 to $202 at Best Buy.

However, the Forerunner 165 has dropped as low as $129 this year, and that’s the price I’m hoping it will be available for in the Black Friday sales, so I’ll hold off for now.

The Forerunner 265 is an attractive AMOLED multi-sport watch that offers a more affordable alternative to newer models like the Forerunner 570.

Its lowest price ever is $299, a deal that popped up in last year’s holiday sales period, so hopefully we’ll see that deal emerge again in the coming weeks.

No one is offering a really good deal on the Forerunner 265 right now, which for me suggests it’s primed for widespread discounts soon.

The Garmin Forerunner 965 is another older watch you can expect to see on sale, with $449 being its lowest price to date, and what I’d hope it drops to this year, at least.

It’s a fantastic triathlon watch with maps and a bright AMOLED display, and at $449 it would be $300 cheaper than the newer Garmin Forerunner 970’s full price.

If you want a watch right now, the black Forerunner 965 is currently $479 at Amazon, which is a great deal, but I think it could still drop lower.

The Garmin Epix Pro has now been superseded by several AMOLED Fenix models in the Fenix 8, Fenix 8 Pro and Fenix 9, but it’s still an excellent adventure watch and likely to be hundreds of dollars cheaper than those newer models on sale.

You can already save $360 on the Epix Pro, which is $639 at Amazon, but I’d be holding out for a deal that brings the watch down to at least $499, the lowest price it’s been in the past.

If you want a more up-to-date adventure watch, the Fenix 8 Pro has only recently been replaced by the Fenix 9 Pro as the flagship option in Garmin’s line-up, so it will hopefully be getting big discounts in Black Friday sales.

The lowest price the Fenix 8 Pro has been in the past is $849, and that’s the price I think it will drop to during the Black Friday sales.

You can get the Garmin Fenix 8 Pro for $1,049 at Amazon at the moment, a $50 saving, but I certainly think a better deal will come along if you wait a week or two.

#3178. A Wall-Mounted Smart Garden Is Peak Winter Decor (and a Great Gift)

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

I tested Modern Sprout's handsome Growframe for a pop of green during fall and winter.

My pursuit of good houseplant tools means I’m always looking for shops and resources. That led me to Modern Sprout, a Chicago-based online store that sells plants and plant-care accessories. When they reached out offering a chance to test some of its high-design indoor garden tech, I decided to see what, if anything, is worth considering for your houseplant needs or as a place to get gifts for the plant parents in your life. I found a few pleasant surprises.

Whether I’m tending to houseplants in winter or gardening in summer, I’m always looking for tools to improve plant care. For houseplants in particular, this means watering, trimming, fertilizing, occasionally repotting and providing as much real estate in sunny windows as possible. When that’s not enough, I harness the power of full-spectrum LED grow lights, so I can have greenery in practically every room of my apartment.

A quick look at Modern Sprout’s website shows it sells most of what you’d expect from a boutique plant nursery: live plants, potting soil, gardening accessories, grow lights, grow kits, candles, bird feeders and gift sets. While Modern Sprout carries plenty of its own products, it also curates items from other small businesses instead of relying on large mainstream gardening brands. This is a nice bonus for folks who prefer to support smart business owners. Modern Sprout also offers wholesale accounts, so you might even see it at a nursery near you.

For my tests, I stuck to the Modern Sprout brand, which is also available on Amazon. Since I review a lot of smart home gear, I wanted to try out the Smart Landscape Growframe ($229) and compare it with other plant light options I use. I also wanted to see if Modern Sprout’s tools are any good, so I rounded out my experience with three essentials behind effective houseplant care: Pruning Shears ($20), a Glass Watering Can ($48) and Dissolving Plant Food Sheets ($20).

I don’t think anything could’ve prepared me for the size of the Smart Landscape Growframe: 32.1 inches long, 11.5 inches tall and 5.3 inches deep. For some context, that’s wider than my kitchen table and large enough to require a careful survey of my home to find a suitable location. I found a nice spot above my stairs next to the front door.

Additionally, its durable powder-coated steel frame weighs an incredible 8 pounds. That’s roughly the weight of a gallon of milk, which makes wall mounting tricky. I agree with Modern Sprout’s recommendation to place the mounting screws in studs, so you’ll need a stud finder, a level, and a power drill if you want it on the wall. You can use drywall anchors, but they aren’t included.

Make sure you visually line up the Growframe’s mounting holes with your marks before driving screws into the wall. This is easier with a second person, but I managed it alone. However, I relied purely on my measurements and had to redo the screws after one was slightly off. I suggest marking one stud first, then holding the planter and using a level to see where the other should go. Similarly, keep in mind that the 9-foot power cord needs to reach an outlet.

The app isn’t complex and is really easy to use. I really like that there are preset schedules for shade, partial sun and full sun light levels — these options always start at 8 a.m., providing 8, 12 and 16 hours of lighting at different intensities, respectively. The presets aren’t customizable, so you must use one of the app’s two other scheduling options to adjust the timing and other settings. I noticed that toggling a preset works as intended, but closing and reopening the menu shows an inactive toggle — a minor visual bug that doesn’t seem to affect the feature. (It might even get patched by the time you read this review.)

You can also set a schedule using specific times of day in the Growframe device menu. While this particular scheduling menu lacks an option to tie the lighting controls to sunrise and sunset, you can find that on the Scenario tab. (Sunrise/sunset automation isn’t necessary for most tropical plants and herbs people put in the planter, but it’s nice to have a temperate lighting option that changes throughout the year.)

The Scenario tab offers a few automation options based on device status, scheduling and weather changes, plus a way to set up manual Tap-to-Run automations. Beyond that, there isn’t much else to the app, which makes sense and aligns with the company’s goal of keeping things simple. However, folks wanting smart home integrations with a platform like Home Assistant or Samsung SmartThings might be better off using a smart plug with a different grow light brand.

Speaking of grow lights, the full-spectrum unit inside the top of the Smart Landscape Growframe can reach up to 1,480 lumens, which is very bright considering most plants in the planter will be within 6 inches or less. Given the size limit on the plants you can put in the planter, I doubt you’ll have any issues with giving your plants enough light. I want to note that the adhesive holding one of the grow light strips in my sample unit came loose during shipping, but it was easy enough to reattach.

Planting in the Smart Landscape Growframe is fairly easy. It comfortably holds up to seven potted plants (2- to 3-inch pots), though I ended up putting five 4-inch potted herbs in my setup. One pot in particular is a little taller than the planter box lip, but it doesn’t bother me.

Be mindful of pot size in the planter, as rims that press up against the side can cause water to overflow onto the floor, as it did with mine. The best solution is to remove a little soil from overfilled pots and to add a small notch or hole in a side of the pot that drains into the basin.

The potted herbs I got from my local garden center needed a little trimming before I could put them in the Smart Landscape Growframe, so it was a good opportunity to try out the Pruning Shears. I’m a huge fan of the all-metal design, which is easily one of the most comfortable pairs of gardening shears I’ve used. The nifty locking latch at the end of the handle isn’t metal — it doesn’t need to be — but it’s easy to use and doesn’t require any fiddling.

I also love the needle-nose blades, which are easy to maneuver into tight spaces when trimming dead growth off plants — they are great for trimming all of my herbs. Although I’ll admit that the densely packed chives tested my patience, that’s hardly the shears’ fault.

Of the items I tested, the glass watering can was my least favorite. For instance, the borosilicate glass may be more durable than other types of glass, but it’s still glass and still fragile. I’m not sure if I trust my clumsy self with it when I water my plants. Similarly, the 1-quart size is great for folks caring for a handful of plants, but it can’t keep up with the gallon-size watering can that I sometimes empty completely just to water my dwarf lemon tree in a large container.

Another issue I had with the glass watering can was the upper rim. While I like that the flared rim makes it easy to fill from my bathroom sink, it can get in the way when watering the Growframe. This is especially tedious when you need to tilt the watering can nearly vertical to get the last drops out. Fortunately, you can see when the water is almost gone, but that’s the only real advantage it has over my normal watering can.

I often put off fertilizing houseplants because it feels like a hassle. My main reason is that most of the carnivorous plants I grow don’t like fertilizer, so I skip it on other houseplants. (I get around this with plant food spikes, but I’m hardly consistent at that either.) A secondary reason is that keeping the fertilizer out where I’ll use it isn’t the best use of space in my apartment. While Modern Sprout’s Dissolving Plant Food Sheets don’t really solve the former problem, they’re very convenient for the latter.

Just like when I tested dishwasher detergent sheets, I really like how easy it is to toss Modern Sprout’s fertilizer into the watering can. A full sheet treats a gallon and has perforations so you can tear it into segments to fit your watering can. Since the glass watering can holds one quart, I needed only one-quarter of a sheet. The sheets dissolved a little slowly, and I had to stir them for a minute or two before no fragments were left.

I’m a fan of the seaweed sheets, which are a great option for plant food that isn’t entirely a collection of processed chemicals. Despite the seaweed ingredient, the sheets don’t explicitly state they’re certified organic, so keep that in mind if you use them for herbs or microgreens.

All of the Modern Sprout products I tested have a premium design and build quality that makes them easy to recommend for people who value form as much as function. I consider the Pruning Shears the best value in the bunch. They feel incredibly sturdy and capable for $20 and are a huge step up from my $10 Vivosun Gardening Scissors in every way. I haven’t tested Modern Sprout Bypass Hand Pruners, but I suspect they’ll easily rival the Fiskars Shears I use for thicker growth.

While $229 is a lot to ask for the Smart Landscape Growframe, keep in mind that premium heavy-duty steel planters without smart lighting already cost more than those made from other materials. Likewise, more affordable — and more powerful — grow lights are on the market, but Modern Sprout’s intuitive app includes every scheduling and customization feature I consider essential. Even people who don’t use other smart home gear should find it easy to navigate.

I think the Dissolving Plant Food Sheets are a fairly nice option for seaweed-based fertilizers, but the 20-pack will treat only 20 gallons of water at the normal dilution. This makes the sheets generally more expensive per feeding than liquid kelp fertilizers from brands like TPS Nutrients and Grow Queen. That said, Modern Sprout is more convenient to measure, and it won’t cause a liquid or powdery mess if you knock the package over. It’s a great gift, but not necessarily for an enthusiast with a large collection of houseplants.

I wouldn’t buy the Glass Watering Can, but I don’t think it’s a bad product. It’s undeniably attractive, but $48 is more than most. I’ll stick with my cheaper, larger plastic watering can. Overall, Modern Sprout suits folks with higher budgets or as an attractive gift option for your favorite plant nerd.

#3177. Can Netflix’s new AI tool stop your TV-streaming doomscrolling? How to try it

Home & Office > Home Entertainment > Streaming & Services > Video Streaming Services

Netflix is adding a new Discover feature, which recommends tailored streaming selections. Here's how it works.

AI tools are all over the internet, in the workplace, in your apps — and now there’s one coming on Netflix. The streaming giant just announced a new AI feature for the TV app called AI Discover, which will help users find titles that match their moods and interests. It’s initially being rolled out to a small group of users in the US whose language is set to English. To find out if you have access, check your Netflix navigation bar for the word “Discover” with a red gem icon next to it and a “New” label.

Also: Roku is taking away a fan-favorite screensaver

The AI Discover tab will be centered around themes. You’ll be able to pick from a range of themes depending on what you feel like watching, or how long you want to watch. You’ll also see selections that match what you’ve been watching lately and what’s popular.

You can refresh the list of suggestions or dive deeper with more complex search prompts. If you’d like to start over, you can pick the “Something different” option. To support AI Discover, Netflix is also adding a new button next to Play and My List that’ll let you tune your recommendations. You can press this button to keep a recommendation or to request that you don’t be recommended a title again.

Most streaming apps already rely quite heavily on machine learning and AI algorithms to offer user recommendations, even if they’re not marketed as AI features. Though most rely on traditional AI algorithms, like Prime Video’s “Recommended for you” tab, they personalize title recommendations based on your viewing history, likes and dislikes, and rental behavior.

Other platforms offer generative AI and features more similar to Netflix’s AI Discover. For example, YouTube features Gemini-powered searches that let you ask complex questions or use open-ended prompts to get results you want.

Also: The best early Black Friday TV deals are all at Best Buy

Some smart TV interfaces — including including Roku, Fire TV, Google TV, and, coming soon, Apple TV — have also adopted generative AI in their operating systems to let you use voice search with more naturally worded requests, and the system generates cross-platform results.

While I am itching to test this feature to see just how accurate it can be, Netflix hasn’t made it widely available yet. The company will gradually grant users access over the coming weeks, so stay tuned.

#3176. nLIGHT: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

nLIGHT's exit from low-margin cutting and welding lifted non-GAAP product gross margins to a record 48.8%.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 5, 2026, nLIGHT (LASR +29.58%) reported top-line growth driven by military program execution despite optical supply bottlenecks. Long-term investors must weigh how effectively the laser manufacturer transitions from component vendor to prime defense contractor while navigating global supply chain shifts. Three takeaways from the call stand out.

Management secured a $627 million Joint Laser Weapon System (JLWS) agreement, marking a shift from supplying individual laser components to assembling complete military defense systems. This contract establishes nLIGHT as a direct system partner for armed forces aiming to field high-energy laser weapons by 2028.

Constraints in the Chinese optical supply chain forced nLIGHT to defer $15 million in commercial product shipments into the first half of 2027. Management fully exited its operations in China during the quarter, incurring restructuring costs while qualifying alternative suppliers to build a more resilient domestic footprint.

nLIGHT completed its strategic exit from low-margin industrial cutting and welding markets to concentrate resources on defense applications and advanced manufacturing. Eliminating commoditized product lines helped drive non-GAAP product gross margins -- the share of sales remaining after direct production expenses -- to a record 48.8%.

Optical component shortages continue to constrain immediate delivery schedules, but management is aggressively expanding domestic sourcing and carrying higher inventory to meet customer demand. The company relies on its balance sheet of over $300 million in cash to build manufacturing capacity.

As military demand for directed energy weapons accelerates, nLIGHT is expanding its role in next-generation defense programs while protecting profitability.

The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3175. You Should Update to iOS 27.2 Now to Patch Hundreds of Bugs

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems

The update fixes some bugs that could expose your sensitive iPhone data or worse.

Apple released iOS 27.2 on Monday, Oct. 26, just a few days after the launch of the iPhone Duo. While iOS 27.2 brings Siri AI to more languages and updates a few other features, it also patches 141 bugs and security issues, including some that could allow a third party to access data or run commands on your iPhone or leave your device inoperable.

You can download iOS 27.2 now by going to Settings > General > Software Update > Update Now and following the prompts on your screen.

According to Apple’s release notes, iOS 27.2 patches dozens of issues across your iPhone. Some of these issues can cause some serious problems for you and your device.

Nine of these bugs address issues that could lead to kernel corruption on your device. The kernel is the core of your device’s operating system, and kernel corruption can cause your device to freeze or crash. The blue screen of death, for example, could be caused by kernel corruption.

Apple also wrote that seven of these bugs could lead to arbitrary code execution. That means the bug could allow a third party to steal your data or use your device for other nefarious purposes.

The update addresses a lot of other bugs, including one that you might run into if you plug a malicious USB into your device. Apple didn’t write that any of these bugs were being actively exploited, but you should still update to iOS 27.2 now to give yourself the most protection.

For as many bugs as 27.2 fixes, it’s actually a slight decrease from earlier updates. Last month’s iOS 27 update patched even more bugs at 157. The number of bug fixes in both iOS updates are unusual based on data over the past few years. In iOS 26, the most bug fixes included in an update was 88 with iOS 26.6. The update with the next highest number of patches was iOS 18.4 with 75.

Apple didn’t say why there’s been an uptick in the number of bug fixes in iOS updates. Artificial intelligence has been used recently to find and address huge numbers of software bugs; however, that doesn’t appear to be the cause behind the surge in iOS 27.2 or iOS 27. Apple usually notes when a bug is found with the help of AI, yet OpenAI is mentioned four times in iOS 27.2’s release notes, and Claude’s Anthropic is only mentioned thrice.

Apple didn’t immediately respond to my request to comment.

For more iOS news, here’s what you should know about iOS 27.2 and iOS 27.1. You can also check out our iOS 27 cheat sheet.

#3174. CoreWeave credit default swaps hit distress levels as investors sour on AI debt

CoreWeave's credit stress continued to spiral on Thursday amid a broader sell-off in AI-related names.

CoreWeave — a so-called neocloud company — has taken on more than $51 billion of debt and obligations to finance its AI infrastructure build-out. It's now facing a rebellion from stock and bond investors as concerns mount about AI overspending and debt levels.

Yields on CoreWeave debt have continued to climb, with the yield to maturity on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 reaching 15.78%. Bond yields move inversely to prices. Meanwhile, the spread on its five-year credit default swaps, which are insurance against default, at one point soared above 1,140 basis points. It last stood at nearly 1,038 bps — a 16% week-over-week jump and 164% higher than a year ago.

Credit concerns are spreading into AI equities too. CoreWeave's stock dropped during trading Thursday before recovering and closing 5.94% higher. The moved followed Wednesday's 15.5% nosedive.

Other neocloud stocks were under similar pressure. Nebius also sank before climbing higher to close 1.29% and and Iren ultimately ended the day down less than 1%.

Anxiety about AI overspending is also spreading. For instance, shares of Micron Technology and Advanced Micro Devices sold off following a big sell-off in South Korean chip stocks. Micron closed up 1.87%, while AMD fell 1.5%.

While AI debt worries have been rising steadily, investors have suddenly grown skittish about the underlying finances of companies borrowing heavily to buy chips, build data centers, and rent out computing power.

Coreweave's credit default swaps are one place to see that stress.

Credit default swaps are contracts where buyers pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. The spread widening in CoreWeave's CDS means investors view the debt as riskier.

The market is "basically saying 'no mas' to AI debt without operating cash flow support," the Evercore ISI trading desk said.

A spread level of 1,000 basis points or above is generally a sign of credit distress, said Gavan Nolan, executive director of fixed income pricing at S&P Global Market Intelligence.

No one is questioning whether demand for CoreWeave's services is real, since the company has contracts with all the big hyperscalers, Nolan said. The question is whether the cash flows will ultimately arrive.

"It's obviously heavily negative free cash flow at the moment, and it needs that cash flow to arrive really to justify all the capex that it's spending," Nolan said.

CEO Mike Intrator dismissed the market moves on Wednesday, according to a memo obtained by CNBC.

"When you are building a new type of technology company there will be periods of time when the market misunderstands and acts irrationally," he wrote. "We are confident in our backlog which more than supports all of our debt obligations."

The backlog is over $100 billion, with around two-thirds of it with investment-grade counterparts including Microsoft, Meta, IBM and Caterpillar, Deutsche Bank said in a note Thursday. The firm reiterated its buy-rating on the stock Wednesday, calling the dislocation a buying opportunity.

"The problem is this kind of concern can prove to be a self-fulfilling prophecy, and risks triggering broader issues in other AI-related names," said Jim Reid, global head of macro and thematic research.

Domino effects may already be happening. Oracle's five-year CDS spread also spiked this week. It was last at 282 basis points, a roughly 10% increase in one week.

"A downgrade from its BBB rating would risk pressuring IG-only investors into forced sales," Reid said.

'iZombie' offers a unique take on the familiar topic of the undead

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

As much as I enjoy many zombie movies and TV shows, zombies have always struck me as slightly less versatile onscreen monsters, with a single primary function of shambling — or, sometimes, running — mindlessly after people in order to eat their brains and/or other organs. So it’s particularly refreshing when a show like “iZombie” comes along, finding a fresh take on the familiar beats of zombie storytelling. There are plenty of references to zombie classics, from “Night of the Living Dead” to “28 Days Later,” but the series forges its own unique path in the zombie subgenre.

The supernatural drama created by “Veronica Mars” veterans Rob Thomas and Diane Ruggiero-Wright aired for five seasons on The CW from 2015-2019, but never quite achieved the same level of fan following as other CW genre shows. It’s been available on various streaming platforms since then, and as of this month, all five seasons of “iZombie” are streaming on Prime Video.

One other advantage for the potential rediscovery of “iZombie” is star Rose McIver’s breakout role on another, much more popular supernatural series, CBS’ “Ghosts.” She brings the same likable presence and slightly snarky demeanor to her portrayal of assistant medical examiner Liv Moore, who uses her zombie abilities to help solve crimes in her Seattle hometown.

Although it’s based on a Vertigo comic book series by writer Chris Roberson and artist Mike Allred, “iZombie” takes very little from its source material, even changing the protagonist’s name to one of Thomas and Ruggiero-Wright’s many amusing pun-based monikers. Liv is a promising medical resident with a picture-perfect relationship with her hunky fiancé Major Lillywhite (Robert Buckley) when an ill-fated night out at a boat party ends in her becoming one of the undead.

Liv isn’t a grunting, stumbling corpse, though; she’s essentially still herself, just with paler skin, white hair and almost no resting heart rate. Oh, and she also has a craving to eat brains, without which she’ll degenerate further into a more stereotypical zombie. As is often the case for network TV shows, “iZombie” grafts its high-concept premise onto a crime procedural, as Liv quits her hospital job to go work in the police morgue alongside Dr. Ravi Chakrabarti (Rahul Kohli). She discovers that eating brains not only gives her sustenance, but also provides visions from the dead person’s life, which helps in finding out information about murder victims.

Liv teams up with police detective Clive Babineaux (Malcolm Goodwin) to aid in his investigations, at first claiming to be psychic before eventually letting him in on her zombie secret. Initially, Liv and fellow boat party victim Blaine DeBeers (David Anders) seem to be the only surviving zombies in Seattle, but “iZombie” eventually builds out an entire zombie subculture, with the effects of brain-eating as a central facet.

Along with the dead person’s memories, zombies also absorb some of the victim’s personality, giving McIver the chance to play everything from a hockey bro to a dominatrix over the course of the series.

Sign up to the weekly Tom's Streaming Guide to stay in the loop with the latest releases and top recommendations across your favorite streaming services. We’ve got you covered with a complete guide on what’s worth watching.

Blaine is just one of many memorable villains that Liv contends with across five seasons of “iZombie,” and guest stars including Steven Weber, Robert Knepper and Jason Dohring of “Veronica Mars” make strong impressions as devious antagonists. Liv also builds a makeshift team/family of allies as more characters learn about her zombie nature.

Although she breaks up with Major when she first becomes undead, he’s quickly drawn back into various zombie conspiracies, as is Liv’s best friend and roommate, lawyer Peyton Charles (Aly Michalka). Recurring characters like vain TV weatherman Johnny Frost (Daran Norris) and smarmy defense attorney Brandt Stone (Ken Marino) give the show a comfortable, lived-in feel as Liv navigates her new existence.

In its later seasons, “iZombie” gets a little too ambitious, turning Seattle into a dystopian zombie enclave ruled by the private military contractor Fillmore Graves, and the budget doesn’t always match up to the grand storytelling ambitions. Even so, the creators mostly maintain the episodic procedural structure amid the increasing serialization, giving Liv the chance to adopt more offbeat personalities and bring justice to more murdered people.

McIver takes on every new persona with enthusiasm and skill, showcasing her wide range as a comedic actor while also giving her chances for dramatic depth as the stakes rise for Liv and her friends. Throughout the series, Thomas, Ruggiero-Wright and their collaborators stretch the boundaries of the zombie concept, never falling back on the obvious clichés.

As someone who’s seen seemingly every type of zombie story, I find that consistently delightful, even more so as zombies retain their hold on horror pop culture, rarely doing more than what’s expected of them.

#3172. Safari finally gets 3 popular browser features – and I can’t wait to try them

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

New developer beta release notes point to deeper Siri AI integration and more Safari customization.

Developer betas for MacOS 27.3, iPadOS 27.3, and iOS 27.3 are here, with a few major upgrades to the Safari browser. Vertical tabs, adaptive dark mode, and deeper Siri AI integration are on the way to the public, but are first in beta for testing.

Also: Your Apple Watch has 2 new controversial listening features – but you have to opt in

Siri AI is expected to be further woven into Music, Messages, Photos, Calendar, and Safari. Adaptive dark mode will allow for any website to be viewed in dark mode, even if the site doesn’t support it. Finally, vertical tabs are pretty straightforward, enabling vertical navigation and drag-and-drop tab reorganization.

Here’s what you can expect when the software goes public in mid-December.

Also: 3 handy iPhone features that make my life easier – even doing laundry

Siri AI gains more access to your Apple devices, allowing more cross-app functionality and the ability to execute complex, multi-step tasks within your apps.

According to 9to5Mac, you can ask Siri AI to create a playlist in Music and save it to your library, request messages to be scheduled for a later send, organize and label photos, and create a detailed calendar invite — all with your voice.

The same reporting also states that Siri AI features will appear in Maps, Weather, Shortcuts, and Notes, signaling that an AI-supercharged Siri will live in every crevice of your Apple devices.

Also: iOS 27 will organize your open Safari tabs now

I’m not one to complete tasks like the ones mentioned above on my iPhone — I’m far more likely to do them on my MacBook Air, since I associate the laptop with productivity. Every blue moon I’ll rearrange my entire Apple Music catalog, every month I fill out my calendar, and sometimes I’ll go through Photos and try to organize it. I can see myself automating certain steps in these tasks, and I’m excited to try it.

A WebKit blog post details that adaptive dark mode is coming to Safari, how it will work, and what users can expect. According to the post, 40% of web page loads are from dark mode users, but only 10% of web pages natively support it.

I’m particularly excited about this feature, as I set my Apple devices to automatically enable dark mode at 7 p.m. to encourage a more relaxing evening scrolling vibe, especially while reading. I read a lot on my MacBook, and in a dark room, a bright page can feel too bright, but dimming my laptop’s display doesn’t quite do the trick.

Also: Your AirPods got better in iOS 27 – three new ways I’m using them

The WebKit blog post states that adaptive dark mode uses “a color matrix to invert the luminosity of colors while preserving hue” and distinguishes between when a page’s color is purely decorative and when it carries substantial meaning, to ensure the user experience remains consistent between light and dark modes.

Vertical browser tabs aren’t a new concept, but they’ll soon be new to Safari. I see the value in vertical tabs: a list view is easier to scan, web page titles aren’t smushed when several tabs are open, and they’re particularly useful when you’re working on a widescreen monitor.

Many people who spend hours using their Mac or MacBook may find this upcoming feature useful. I do believe that Safari is the superior browser for personal use, given its integration with your Apple devices, especially with features like Handoff.

Also: 7 AI-free iOS 27 features that make old iPhones exciting again

However, I do have one major gripe with vertical tabs: they’re usually locked to the left panel, and I prefer the right. If Safari allows this customization, I’ll seriously consider switching from horizontal tabs.

#3171. Intel, Arm, and Marvell Have Each Been Cut in Half Since June. Only 1 Is a Stock I'd Buy Now.

All three chip stocks are more than half off their peaks. Only one expects its sales growth to keep speeding up.

Intel (INTC +7.71%), Arm Holdings (ARM +4.74%), and Marvell Technology (MRVL +5.73%) all set record closes in June. Five months later, each stock is worth less than half as much.

Intel closed at $58.27 on Nov. 4, off around 59% from $140.94, its record close on June 22. Arm ended that day at $191.80, around 56% under its June 18 record close, and Marvell finished at $159.80, about 50% below where it closed on June 4.

All three fell between around 4% and 7% on Nov. 4 alone, then slid further on Nov. 5. Investors were weighing whether Democrats' midterm wins might slow the build-out of artificial intelligence data centers, especially after Texas elected a governor who campaigned for a moratorium on new ones.

Falls this steep can make all three look like bargains. But each stock is still up over 40% in 2026. And only one of them expects its sales growth to keep speeding up, even as its stock got cheaper.

Marvell is that one, and it's the only one of the three I'd buy right now.

Intel's third quarter was strong, with revenue up 21% year over year. The problem is the outlook it gave on Oct. 29. Intel sees non-GAAP (adjusted) earnings of $0.25 per share for the fourth quarter, under half the $0.52 it earned in the third quarter.

PCs are the biggest reason. Higher memory prices have pushed up what new PCs cost, and Intel expects revenue in its PC chip segment to drop over 20% from the third quarter. Its chief financial officer, David Zinsner, sees the weakness lasting until at least mid-2027.

Yes, demand for Intel's server chips still tops what it can supply. But at around $54, Intel stock trades at around 36 times the earnings expected for 2026 and about 30 times the earnings expected for 2027.

Arm reported its fiscal 2027 second quarter (the three months ended Sept. 30) on the evening of Nov. 4. Revenue climbed 27% year over year to $1.44 billion, and its adjusted earnings per share increased 33% to $0.52.

The figure I'd follow most closely, though, is royalty revenue (the fees Arm gets for every Arm-based chip its partners ship). Royalties grew just 8% from a year earlier to $670 million, a sharp slowdown from 22% growth in fiscal Q1. Arm said memory prices hit the smartphone market and offset some of its gains elsewhere.

Licensing revenue, however, jumped 50% to $773 million, and management called licensing an important leading indicator of future royalty growth.

Sure, that might prove true. But at around $170, Arm stock trades at around 56 times its expected earnings for next fiscal year (ending March 2028). Even after a drop of around 60%, investors paying that price are arguably relying on royalties to speed up again soon.

Marvell's numbers are going the other way. Its revenue growth has climbed each quarter of fiscal 2027 so far, from 28% year over year in the first quarter to 37% in the second (through Aug. 1). On Nov. 2, Marvell said fiscal third-quarter revenue is tracking with its $3.15 billion forecast, now within 2% either way. That'd be around 52% above the year-ago quarter. What's more, Marvell's August forecast called for $1.10 in adjusted earnings per share, up around 45% from $0.76 the year before.

The same day, Marvell agreed to buy optical parts maker Lumentum Holdings (LITE +5.67%) in an all-stock deal that values Lumentum at around $115 billion, including its debt. Lumentum shareholders would get 4.5 Marvell shares for every Lumentum share, leaving today's Marvell shareholders holding around two-thirds of the combined company. Closing is expected in the first half of 2028, pending shareholder and regulatory approval.

At around $141, Marvell stock trades at around 19 times the earnings expected for fiscal 2028 (ending January 2028), based on Marvell's business alone.

Intel's server chips and Arm's licensing business are each doing well. But Intel's profits are set to fall steeply this quarter, and Arm's royalty growth just slowed to 8%. At today's prices, I think both stocks still cost too much for these problems.

Marvell is the only one of the three whose latest forecast calls for its sales growth to speed up again. And based on next year's expected earnings, its price-to-earnings ratio is around a third of Arm's.

In the end, I think Marvell stock is worth buying here, maybe a bit at a time as the Lumentum deal moves toward closing.

#3169. Want an AI job? Deployment engineers are earning executive-level pay – up to $1.45M a year

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

AI has ushered in new jobs, and some of them are paying handsomely, according to a new study.

AI engineers are making well over six figures in salary — and plenty more in bonuses and equity, according to data from a new report by executive search firm Christian & Timbers.

Enterprises now pay “elite” AI deployment engineers between $586,000 and $1.45 million annually in total compensation, with senior leaders in the field earning up to $2.25 million, according to the report. This figure approaches the median compensation of executive officers of public companies, the authors note.

Also: ‘So perfect, they are literally identical’ – Your AI-polished resumes are recruiters’ new top stressor

Christian & Timbers is an AI executive search and leadership advisory firm, recruiting CEOs, board members, technology executives, and AI engineers for enterprises. The new report draws on more than 550 interviews with engineers, deployment leaders, and executives conducted from January through June 2026, along with closed and current open offers. The compensation bands are Christian & Timbers’ estimates and represent ranges rather than statistical averages.

“Enterprise CEOs are under pressure to deliver returns for their AI investments. The most important decision is how to build the team that delivers on that goal,” said Jeff Christian, founder and CEO of Christian & Timbers, in a press release.

Also: What workers are really using AI for in 2026 – and what they aren’t

The report noted that the staggering salary range includes base salary, bonus, and annualized equity. Christian & Timbers said it defines an “elite” forward deployment engineer (FDE) as “a senior AI-native deployment builder, typically with 7 to 15 years of experience, including at least two years building and deploying agentic automated workflows that deliver measurable ROI with a documented enterprise impact of $10M or more,” who has also led a team of other forward deployment engineers.

Also: 50% of companies generate value from AI now – here’s how

The large salary ranges reflect increasing enterprise investments in AI talent and employee development. Microsoft, AWS, and OpenAI have announced more than $7.5 billion in corporate and partner investment in AI deployment over the past six months, Christian & Timbers noted in the release.

Also: Nearly 70% of workers use AI regularly now – but many get no time to upskill

There’s an increasing need to fill roles such as Senior Principal AI Engineer, AI Solutions Engineer, and Head of Deployment — but there is a limited pool of qualified candidates. The firm estimates that only 2,000 of the most senior deployment leaders exist in the US market, according to a previous Christian & Timbers study, which does not meet demand — so companies are competing for the same small group of professionals, hence the increasing salaries.

While the report underscores lucrative yields for certain engineering roles, the firm said that public job titles make comparisons more challenging.

“Christian & Timbers’ elite category spans several titles, while advertised FDE roles cover a broader range of experience and responsibilities. Experienced deployment engineers may be recruited directly or promoted into the work under titles such as staff software engineer or principal AI engineer,” the study said.

Plus, equity and bonus make up 52% to 66% of senior engineer pay, and most job postings don’t include that value in the salary, the report noted.

#3168. 1 Stat That Makes Amazon Hard to Ignore Right Now

The biggest company in the world sees huge opportunities.

Amazon (AMZN -0.35%) just reported outstanding third-quarter results, but its stock sank after earnings. There were clear worries about how much Amazon is spending, and it reported negative free cash flow for the third quarter in a row.

However, there was a lot to like about the report, and the stock is already back up. Consider this one stat that's hard to ignore right now.

Amazon's sales increased 12% year over year, or 13% excluding the impact of unfavorable foreign exchange. The e-commerce growth was middling, and the biggest growth came from Amazon Web Services (AWS), the company's cloud division.

In fact, AWS sales increased 39% year over year, back to levels from four years ago, but on a base that's more than double the size. Some examples of the incredible demand include Fortune 500 companies increasing their spend on Amazon Bedrock sevenfold from last year, and total token volume on Bedrock increasing almost 17-fold.

Even more exciting, Amazon is already providing guidance that AWS sales will increase 40% in both 2027 and 2028. CEO Andy Jassy said, "There has never been a trillion-dollar revenue business, and we may have two of them in the next 5-10 years."

That's a compelling reason to buy Amazon stock today.

#3167. The Step-by-Step Guide to Shutting Off Apple Intelligence Features in iOS 27

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

With the master Apple Intelligence toggle gone, opting out in iOS 27 means turning off Siri AI, Writing Tools, Image Playground and other features separately.

Screen Time, the iPhone feature most people know for limiting app use, has become the most direct way to block Apple Intelligence in iOS 27. That’s because Apple removed the single switch that used to turn every AI feature off at once. The Settings section once called Apple Intelligence & Siri is now just Siri, centered on the new Siri AI, and the remaining AI tools are scattered across several menus. Screen Time’s restrictions can shut down Writing Tools and image creation together, and a few other features need their own toggles. Here’s the full map.

Individual features can still be disabled, but they’re scattered across several menus in Settings and Screen Time rather than consolidated in one place. Here’s the current map of what can be turned off and where to find each control.

If your device is not running the newest software, turning off Apple Intelligence all at once only takes a few taps in Settings and doesn’t affect any core iPhone functionality. Here’s how to do it on iPhone, iPad and Mac.

You can still disable some of Apple’s AI features if your iPhone is running iOS 27. It just won’t be as easy as tapping the main toggle.

To disable Siri AI, go to Settings > Siri and toggle off the control. Beware, though, that this will disable your voice assistant entirely, as well as disable conversations in the new dedicated Siri app and block you from using Apple’s Visual Intelligence. Additionally, your HomePod speakers will not be able to recognize your voice or help with personal content if they’re on the same iCloud account as your iPhone.

Keep reading for how to disable some of the individual Apple AI features.

Since the first Apple Intelligence features rolled out in iOS 18.1, some people have opted out of the new technology. Tools such as Image Playground, Genmoji and message summaries can use up to 7GB of your device’s internal storage — a sizable chunk, especially if you tend to run against the upper limit of its free space.

Even if storage is not an issue, you just may not be interested in Apple’s set of AI features. A recent CNET survey finds that just 19% of US smartphone owners are choosing to upgrade their devices to take advantage of new AI features. And a recent ZDNET/Aberdeen survey reveals that the majority of US adults say they will not use most AI features, and just 8% would consider paying extra for them. Some AI features are more welcome than others, with 58% of respondents saying using AI for photo editing would be valuable.

Or, honestly, you may not want to deal with features that are creating inaccurate summaries or that can be replicated without Apple Intelligence.

(Earlier iOS 18 updates re-enabled Apple Intelligence automatically even if you had turned it off, but as of iOS 18.4, the setting is honored.)

Read more: Did a well-meaning friend send you a message about Apple Intelligence possibly reading your private information? Here’s what’s actually going on.

I’m not an AI crank — I appreciate features such as notification summaries and the Clean Up tool in the Photos app. And yet, Apple Intelligence is also a work in progress, an evolving set of features that Apple is heavily hyping while gradually developing. I wouldn’t hold it against you if you wanted to not be distracted or feel like you’re doing Apple’s testing for them (that’s what the developer and public betas should be for).

Remember that Apple Intelligence works only on the new iPhone 18 lineup, the iPhone Duo, iPhone 17, iPhone 17 Pro, iPhone Air, iPhone 17E, iPhone 16, iPhone 16E, iPhone 16 Pro, iPhone 15 Pro or M-series Macs and iPads, plus the newest iPad mini.

If you’re concerned about the storage requirements of Apple Intelligence, you can find out how much the system is using by going to Settings > General > iPhone (or iPad) Storage > iOS (or iPadOS).

See how much space Apple Intelligence is using in Settings. This is what it looks like running iOS 26, but the settings flow is the same.

Note that even after turning off Apple Intelligence, the storage still shows up in the iOS Storage list. However, I was able to confirm that if the phone’s storage fills up and the system needs that now-inactive space, iOS reclaims what it needs.

Maybe you’re finding some Apple Intelligence features helpful while others are annoying (or just highly amusing). You can turn off specific features in the settings for apps to which they apply.

For example, to disable text summaries in Messages notifications, go to Settings > Apps > Messages and switch off the Summarize Messages option.

Some features are not as app-specific. Writing Tools, which use Apple Intelligence to proof or rewrite text, appear as an option when you select text, regardless of the app. To turn that off, you need to disable Apple Intelligence system-wide.

Also note that nearly every app includes an “Apple Intelligence & Siri” option in Settings, with a Learn from this App option (turned on by default). That applies just to whether Apple Intelligence and Siri can monitor how you use it to make suggestions; it doesn’t affect any particular Apple Intelligence features.

Another option for restricting what Apple Intelligence can do is buried in the Screen Time settings. Screen Time is designed to control which apps and features can run on another device, such as the iPhone used by a child in your household. But it’s also helpful for tracking how much time you’re spending on the device and, in this case, turning off some components of Apple Intelligence.

Turn off selected Apple Intelligence features in the Screen Time settings. This is what the settings look like with iOS 26. It may look different if you are using iOS 27. The settings flow is the same.

If you haven’t upgraded to iOS 27 yet, make a good backup first.

If you aren’t on iOS 27, you can turn off Apple Intelligence with one tap. (You can see what software your iPhone is running by going to Settings > General > About and seeing what iOS version you’re running.)

If you’re on iOS 26 or earlier, here’s how to disable Apple Intelligence. Open Settings (iPhone or iPad) or System Settings (Mac) and choose Apple Intelligence & Siri. Then turn off the Apple Intelligence option. Confirm your choice in the dialog that appears by tapping Turn Off Apple Intelligence.

Turn off Apple Intelligence entirely with one switch if you are running iOS 26 or an earlier software version.

Curiously, the Clean Up tool in Photos sticks around even when Apple Intelligence is turned off, perhaps because the first time you use it the app downloads resources for it and holds onto them.

#3166. Researchers asked people to use AI chatbots for election info – results were mixed

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Chatbots can skew election-related information and act as an intervening layer in a voter's search.

Most search tools now put a layer of AI between you and the information you’re looking for, especially Google.

Also: Tired of AI Overviews? I found 9 Google Search alternatives

In a new study, Yale researchers found that a third of users asked a chatbot to help them pick a candidate. Grok often recommended candidates on its own without any information from users, while ChatGPT recommended candidates most often overall, and twice as much as Claude.

The researchers asked nearly 2,500 people in 35 states with Senate races to query ChatGPT, Claude, Grok, or Gemini about the election. Participants in the study, which ran for a few weeks in October leading up to the Nov. 3 midterm elections, were adults who already use chatbots weekly.

Most users asked chatbots for basic facts about the election, including who was on the ballot. But 36% asked a chatbot to help them pick a candidate to vote for, often by describing their priorities and asking the chatbot to assess which candidates aligned with them.

The former query has slightly less room for error, because the internet has a ton of data on the basic facts of an election, such as who is running where. Chatbots interpreting candidate positions, on the other hand, is a bit more dicey, and relies significantly on the sources a chatbot uses (a statement from a candidate’s own website, for example, is more reliable than a social media clip of an opponent characterizing them in a certain way).

Also: How to keep your chatbot conversations as private as possible

In one example, a user asked ChatGPT to give them the “pros and cons” of each candidate in Virginia, an inherently subjective question.

When it came to recommending candidates, chatbots varied widely.

“When users asked who to vote for but had not yet said anything about themselves, only Grok regularly recommended a candidate, in 14% of chats,” the researchers wrote. “Once users shared their views, ChatGPT recommended a candidate in 79% of chats and Claude in 40%, with Grok and Gemini in between.”

Pew Research found that 42% of US adults surveyed use chatbots to search for information. That’s a broad umbrella that can include election information — but Pew also found that only 8% of US adults reported using chatbots to find election-related information in this year’s election.

What those users are looking for matters, though. Pew found that of that group, 76% were inquiring about where candidates stand on certain issues, and 71% were fact-checking information they’d heard about candidates or issues.

Also: OpenAI will merge ChatGPT’s chat and work tabs

Given that chatbots are still not reliable summarizers, the nature of these questions poses a misinformation risk if users don’t also check a chatbot’s linked sources. An overwhelming majority of users don’t, at least when it comes to Google’s AI Overviews. At least one study found AI platforms gave users “outdated or inaccurate” information that could mislead voters in some cases by missing a deadline or failing to mention a step about voting logistics.

This election cycle was particularly plagued by AI-generated deepfakes and misinformation as tools have become much more sophisticated since the 2024 election. OpenAI and Anthropic reportedly set up election “war rooms” to monitor misuse and information accuracy, according to exclusive reporting by Politico and Semafor.

While an August study from the Brennan Center for Justice found that chatbots did push back surprisingly well against certain falsehoods related to election disinformation campaigns, it also found that AI models easily generated “misleading election-related images and video” when asked.

“Many of their safeguards for preventing this were ineffective, inconsistently applied, or functionally absent,” noted the study, which tested ChatGPT, Gemini, Grok, Meta AI, Runway, and Flux.2.

Also: 50 AI agents tried to build a thriving Minecraft civilization – it didn’t end well

Apart from these factors, however, there is a broader theme in AI use that is equally consequential for civic engagement: cognitive offloading. The political media landscape can already be dense and hard to parse for a voter trying to stay informed. Layering a chatbot — which has opaque training data sets, filters, and other settings determined at the discretion of AI labs — on top of that information search process can further muddy the waters while appearing like a convenient and neutral summarizing tool.

While some studies test how AI chatbots respond to election questions by using standardized questions, this approach let users themselves engage with chatbots as they normally would. While the Yale researchers’ queries and standardized study queries show a lot of similarities, the distinction is valuable for evaluations.

“This is a small step toward evaluations that measure AI’s political influence on the questions users actually ask,” the researchers noted.

Outside of users self-reporting what they asked a chatbot, only AI labs have records of what users are really asking about elections. In October, Anthropic analyzed 250,000 conversations with Claude and determined people “rarely ask Claude whom or what to vote for,” finding that “0.51% of all conversations are specifically about elections or voting,” though it expected election-related queries to increase closer to Nov. 3.

Also: 6 AI agent workflows I use to hand off everyday tasks

“More often, we observed people asking Claude questions that could help them reach their own conclusions, like where candidates stand on a particular issue. Requests that violated our election integrity policies were even less common, appearing in about 0.002% of conversations,” Anthropic’s report said.

The Yale researchers said the next step for a study like theirs is determining how accurate and unbiased models are in their responses, as well as what models learn about users, and whether their recommendations actually match users’ needs.

“In multi-turn chats, users often answered the chatbot’s questions, and those answers led to its recommendations. The questions a model asks shape what users tell it and what it recommends,” the researchers explained.

#3164. Why Aurora Innovation Stock Lagged the Market Last Month

An analyst's move at the start of the month and quarterly earnings near the end didn't improve sentiment on the self-driving truck specialist.

Self-driving truck specialist Aurora Innovation (AUR +9.43%) had an October to forget, as far as its stock was concerned. A lackluster quarterly earnings report drained investor sentiment on the company, as did a change by an analyst tracking its fortunes. With these roadblocks, Aurora's shares skidded to an almost 21% loss across the Halloween month.

On Oct. 1, Evercore ISI's Chris McNally updated his take on Aurora's equity. He continues to be bullish on the company, although, according to reports, he pointed out that it didn't disclose any notable new clients during its annual analyst day. More impactfully, he removed Aurora stock from his company's tactical action positioning (TAP) list of hot stocks to buy on short-term catalysts.

In a slightly more positive development on the analyst front, one week later Oppenheimer (OPY +2.92%) pundit Colin Rusch raised his price target on Aurora's shares. Granted, this wasn't a major hike -- from $15 per share to $16 -- but Rusch did maintain his outperform (read: buy) recommendation.

Aurora cruised through the rest of the month without much news to report until Oct. 28, when it opened the hood on those quarterly results.

Since autonomous vehicles are still very much the exception rather than the rule on the world's roads, Aurora's revenue remained modest in the third quarter. It booked $3 million on the top line, which was notably better than the $1 million it earned in the same period of 2025. Due to an uptick in costs, particularly a 13% rise in crucial research and development spending, the company's headline net loss deepened. It was $234 million, or $0.12 per share, against the year-ago deficit of $201 million. Both headline metrics were basically in line with analyst estimates.

This wasn't an especially eventful quarter for Aurora. It signed a memorandum of understanding with transportation company Charger Logistics, under which the latter will buy and operate 500 trucks equipped with the auto tech company's assisted-driving software. Deliveries should occur next year and in 2028. Aurora didn't provide any financial details of the arrangement.

Investors in companies that specialize in a cutting-edge technology expect their investments to make tangible progress quarter by quarter. I think this is what dinged Aurora in October, more than the continuing nine-figure losses and persistently thin revenue. The Charger Logistics deal will provide some welcome coin for the company's coffers, but as it currently exists only as a non-binding MOU, we'd hardly be able to count on it, even if we knew what dollar figure we were looking at.

I should emphasize that this is a highly speculative stock, although Aurora has made significant strides with its technology, and a small fleet of its trucks runs key commercial routes in the U.S. Southwest. Still, it needs to show some greater progress for investors to get more excited about its stock; until then, the shares could very well languish or drop further.

Eric Volkman has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3163. Will Democrats Rein in Big Tech? It Starts With Privacy and Data Centers

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Tech critics say that real change will depend on newly elected officials keeping their campaign promises.

In this week’s midterm elections, Democrats won back control of both chambers of Congress, with stunning victories in battleground states that have voters and tech critics asking: Was this the AI election?

Artificial intelligence was central to the national conversation during the midterms, alongside concerns about affordability and the Iran war. From data centers needed to power AI to how it’s used in surveillance tech like Flock cameras, many Democratic (and a few Republican) candidates campaigned against AI.

Despite tech leaders like Elon Musk pouring hundreds of millions of dollars into Republican campaigns, tech critics say voters’ choices show a strong distrust in Big Tech and give the newly elected leaders the necessary momentum for change. But how, exactly, they deliver on those goals remains to be seen — and will have real consequences for tech consumers and companies.

“It is time for lawmakers to get real about reining in unchecked tech power and to hold these companies accountable to the people,” Butler says.

More than any other tech issue, data centers took center stage in the midterm elections. It isn’t surprising; these facilities are popping up everywhere and require vast amounts of water and electricity at the expense of nearby communities’ health and environments.

Voters showed their frustration at the ballot box. Across the country, voters rejected more than 40 ballot measures that would’ve supported data centers. It was a rare national consensus on an issue that is usually debated in city councils and statehouses. As such, “action is more likely to come on the local and state levels, at least in the short term,” says Paris Marx, tech critic and host of the Tech Won’t Save Us podcast.

Governments may continue to adopt moratoriums on new data center construction, like New York’s first-in-the-nation pause implemented earlier this year, as they try to figure out how to mitigate the harms. State and local legislators need to continue setting environmental rules, managing electricity demand and finding real community benefits, Marx says. (OpenAI, for its part, is tackling the issue by renaming data centers as “compute campuses.”)

In many ways, “data centers are a symptom of the larger problem,” says Samir Jain, vice president of policy at the Center for Democracy and Technology. Tech companies are so focused on “winning” the global AI race that they forget (or didn’t prioritize) the impact on everyday people, he says.

But neighbors living next to data centers cannot forget. In that sense, the overwhelming public backlash to data centers and the midterm election results are “a real repudiation of that kind of approach to technology,” Jain says.

But we’ve seen very clearly over the past decade that when left unchecked, tech companies can collect and use our data in concerning ways. Despite stalled progress on a national bill in the past, there may be a new push for it, thanks to recent tech headlines. Flock cameras and other automatic license plate readers have sparked immense public concern as many cases show law enforcement officers misusing the tech to stalk and harass people.

It isn’t just major tech platforms; it’s also data brokers that sell data to companies you don’t interact with. That industry is worth more than $277 billion, and it’s the engine behind much of how Big Tech operates.

“If you are mad at this whole surveillance pricing situation where we’re going to charge you more if we feel like you can afford it, that is because of data brokers,” says McKinney. “If you are mad that the federal government can just buy information from data brokers that [it] would otherwise have to get a warrant to get, that’s a data broker problem.”

“We can’t lead the world in AI and support transformative AI innovation with a patchwork of conflicting state laws that make life harder for startups and small businesses,” Fabrizio says.

While the Democrats will have a firm majority in both chambers of Congress, many of the most contentious tech issues have bipartisan support, tech critics told me. And we’ve already seen how that can result in actionable legislation, such as the Take It Down Act, which passed nearly unanimously in 2025.

This fleeting moment of bipartisan agreement on reining in Big Tech could help lawmakers achieve the elusive task of actually fulfilling their campaign promises. But part of the challenge will be getting lawmakers to focus on real-world harm and basic protections, not whatever dystopian tech prediction is trending that day.

“It’s very likely we’ll see companies continue to push lawmakers to focus AI regulation on so-called ‘existential risks,’ meaning their claims that more advanced AI might kill humanity or end the world,” Marx says, “rather than addressing the impacts of data centers and the present-day harms.”

Those existential risks only serve to make tech companies more powerful. “These elected representatives don’t just have the power to rein in these powerful corporations; they have the obligation to do so to promote the interests of the constituents they serve,” Butler says.

We saw this election cycle how much money Big Tech is willing to spend to try to get its preferred candidates and policies. Elon Musk alone reportedly spent nearly half a billion dollars backing Republican candidates such as Ken Paxton in Texas, who lost by 12 percentage points to Democrat James Talarico. Despite that, most of the tech experts I spoke to said the results showed a real desire from voters to rein in tech companies and the harms they wrought.

But that will depend on how willing the new Congress is to buck Big Tech. “If we have a new electorate that is newly emboldened to stand up to bullies,” McKinney says, “let’s start there.”

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, CNET’s parent company, in 2025 filed a lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

Chrome might receive a much-needed addition.

I’m a longtime Chrome user, but I’ve never been a fan of the browser’s user settings menu, which has grown into a dense collection of nesting submenus that can be difficult to navigate. That may be changing soon.

Also: How Chrome warns you about suspicious websites now

X (formerly Twitter) user, Leopeva64, posted a short video and several still images showcasing a potential new feature in Chrome Canary, which Google uses to test new Chrome web tools and features. The posts show the search bar pinned to the top of the settings menu for instant access. By entering the name of the submenu or feature you want to access, the new search bar could save a lot of time normally spent clicking around, trying to remember exactly where it’s located.

In Chrome Canary, Google is testing removing the scroll arrows that let you automatically scroll to reveal hidden items when you hover over them in the 3-dot menu. They also added a scrollbar, and the search box at the top and the footer are now pinned so they are always visible: https://t.co/Plx6BgKfJN pic.twitter.com/D7TxDkCtmP

According to Leopeva64, other possible new features include a visual update that groups similar menu options into tiles for faster navigation of your most-accessed settings, as well as pinning the menu footer to keep it visible as you click through.

Chrome Extensions is also up for a possible refresh with a move to a dedicated submenu called Manage Skills to help keep all of your installed extensions organized and make it easier to remove ones that are no longer needed.

Google Lens may get a new submenu, called “Ask Google about this page,” for searching for items you see across an entire page, not just in a single image.

There’s no official word yet on when these features will leave Canary’s testing environment and become available in the regular Chrome menu. I’ve reached out to Google for a comment, but at the time of writing, I have not received a response.

#3161. Brian Sullivan: Why the 'real' price of oil is likely $30-$40 higher than where it is now

Oil, in reality, is $125 per barrel or higher.

That's the real cost of a barrel of oil because buying the physical commodity is a very different story from a number on traders' screens.

Oil futures are just that: paper contracts indicating a price today, tomorrow, next month or further out.  If you want to buy the physical commodity, it may be a totally different story — and price.

As I've reported, prices for oil delivery have skyrocketed. Last week, a VLCC (aka a "supertanker" in non-shipper parlance) was chartered from the U.S. Gulf Coast to China for $76 million. Another very large crude carrier went for $77 million from the U.S. to South Korea. That's 10 times more than the price of the same trip less than one year ago.

Assuming these ships hold about 2 million barrels of oil, that charter price means each barrel on the ship costs about $38 over the price of the oil itself.

I'm certainly not saying to avoid looking at oil futures prices, but you need to know that the price on the CNBC screen or CNBC.com isn't always the full price being paid. These days, any company that needs oil shipped by sea, rail or truck will pay a hefty premium over the price of the oil itself. So, while the volume of oil moving through Hormuz is closing in on pre-war levels, costs are not. And there's no sign that they're going anywhere but up.

You know what else is up? The stocks of the shipowners. Look at their moves this month and this year.

On a more serious note, I want to express my condolences for the awful attacks on the airport in Riyadh, Saudi Arabia. The cowardly strike on civilians—some of whom were religious pilgrims—took the lives of at least 14 people and injured more than 300.

The airport was attacked at least three times as travelers and energy industry professionals from around the world were flying into Riyadh for the World Petroleum Council conference. I was scheduled to fly in on Saturday, but the trip was canceled because of the strikes. The WPC went on as planned — here's one panel that took place Tuesday — and I look forward to seeing all my global energy friends again soon.

Thanks for reading—and watching. Be safe in these crazy and often scary times.

The supercap oil and gas stock to watch right now is Shell (SHEL). Shell is not a shell of its former self. It's back to being what energy investors wanted: a major player in global energy, focused on what it does best. Shareholders are being rewarded.

Shell hit a new high this week. While analysts remain bullish, the stock is getting close to its target price.

Shell may be underappreciated for its huge liquefied natural gas business. RBC analyst Biraj Borkhataria writes: "Without a sustained resumption in Qatari volumes and with European storage levels below seasonal lows heading into winter, European gas prices continued on an upward trajectory in 3Q26. With HH broadly unchanged throughout the summer, export margins have increased to the widest levels seen since 2022, on our estimates nearly double the level in 2Q26 (~$13/mmbtu vs. $7/mmbtu)."

Borkhataria expects Shell and other companies with huge U.S. LNG positions — TotalEnergies (TTE) and BP (BP) — to benefit from wider margins on exports to Europe.

Speaking of RBC and energy, the firm lists ConocoPhillips as one of its top 30 global stock ideas. The company is covered by analyst Scott Hanold, who believes it has a "returns-focused value proposition, a strong balance sheet, and peer-leading distributions." He loves its "global and diverse" asset base and management's clear "investment propositions." Hanold has a $152 target on COP. That's more than 20% upside.

In oil and gas, refiners and big energy stocks are generating cash for investors, and Morgan Stanley is optimistic more is coming.

The firm says the diesel shortage is now spilling over into gasoline, potentially marking another leg of the "golden age" for refiners. Morgan Stanley has been revising its earnings estimates higher for companies including ExxonMobil (XOM), Valero (VLO), Phillips 66 (PSX) and PBF (PBF).

Fun Fact →  While nearly every other company calls Texas home, refiner PBF is headquartered in a suburban office park just off a highway in New Jersey.

Two big calls to quickly highlight in power production.

First, Truist says you have to buy First Solar (FSLR). The analyst upgrades it to Buy with a $270 target. Tariffs should help the Phoenix-based company, giving it pricing power for at least four more years. The Section 232 tariffs—which are too complicated to detail here—should help First Solar raise solar module prices and boost earnings. We agree, having written about the company and stock back in July.

Another call on the Power Insider radar: TD Cowen is starting coverage of TeraWulf (WULF) with a Buy rating and a $21 target. That indicates about 50% upside for the stock, but it's actually one of the Street's lower price targets. TeraWulf and other AI power companies have seen their stocks hit hard following months of big gains, but Cowen analyst Michael Elias says the valuation is now attractive and a likely lease in the second half of 2027 at a Kentucky location could be a catalyst.

In the wake of the terror attacks on Saudi Arabia, we spoke with RBC Capital Markets' Helima Croft about the attacks, energy and more.

The entire energy world is watching Europe's natural gas storage levels. The question is whether the continent will have enough gas to make it safely through the winter. Power Insider wrote about the topic in the last issue.

The fact that JPMorgan highlights expected weather patterns should tell you how much temperature matters when it comes to gas storage. The early forecast calls for October and November to be average or slightly warmer than the 10-year average. The red line represents the 10-year average.

Europe has been largely "saved" by mild temperatures over the past few years. Natural gas storage levels are lower this year than in years past, so the thermometer will matter a lot when it comes to how well countries can, ahem, weather the winter. Random, but interesting.
