#3159. Donald Trump Claims He'll Become "Mr. Veto" After Midterm Elections, but the Results Put the President and Wall Street in a Precarious Situation

A surprise from the midterm elections may fundamentally alter the artificial intelligence (AI) regulatory landscape.

One of the most pivotal days of the year for America and Wall Street has come and gone. Election Day, Nov. 3, yielded a "blue wave," with Democrats retaking both the House of Representatives and the Senate by a wide margin. Several prediction markets had forecast a strong likelihood of a Democratic sweep in advance of Nov. 3.

Wall Street's initial reaction to midterm elections was positive, with the timeless Dow Jones Industrial Average (^DJI -0.46%), benchmark S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.31%), and innovation-driven Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +1.09%) advancing 3%, 1.4%, and 1.6%, respectively, on Nov. 4. But these gains may prove fleeting.

As Election Night unfolded, President Donald Trump took to social media and Fox News to lay out his game plan for the final two years of his term. This included digging in his heels and proclaiming, "I guess I'll become Mr. Veto," with regard to his future dealings with a Democrat-led Congress.

President Trump's efforts to minimize AI regulation will face a tough test. Image source: Official White House Photo by Daniel Torok.

While the prospect of political gridlock has investors somewhat on edge, it's another subset of Election Night results that's put President Trump and Wall Street's rip-roaring bull market in a precarious situation.

Political gridlock isn't an end-all for the Dow Jones Industrial Average, S&P 500, and Nasdaq Composite. In fact, some aspects of political gridlock can be beneficial for the stock market.

For example, a Democrat-led Congress, coupled with a Republican in the White House, dramatically lowers the odds of significant tax and spending legislation being signed into law between January 2027 and January 2029. This suggests that the peak marginal corporate income tax rate of 21% will remain at its lowest level since 1939 through at least the end of Trump's final term. A historically low corporate income tax rate and some degree of continuity are both good news for Wall Street.

But other aspects of this political gridlock can create pain points for the stock market.

"I guess I'll become Mr. Veto." President Trump lays out his strategy for dealing with a potential Democratic-controlled Congress in an exclusive interview with Miranda Devine. Trump says he would veto legislation before eventually negotiating compromises, while expressing hope Republicans maintain control after Tuesday's midterms.

For instance, the ideological differences between Democrats and Republicans will likely make debt-ceiling negotiations far more challenging. The debt ceiling (i.e., the upper limit on what the federal government can borrow to meet its obligations) is forecast to be reached in the first half of 2027. A split government greatly increases the odds of a federal government shutdown next year, which could adversely affect the U.S. economy and corporate earnings.

Furthermore, a Democrat-led Congress is more likely to spar with President Trump over artificial intelligence (AI) regulation. Whereas the president has favored as little AI regulation as possible to encourage innovation, spur economic growth, and send the Dow, S&P 500, and Nasdaq Composite to new heights, most Democratic lawmakers want to beef up the testing and vetting of AI solutions, including large language models, and limit data center expansion.

This is an area where President Trump believes his veto power can be especially powerful. Unless the House and Senate both reach a two-thirds vote, Trump would be able to override AI legislation with a presidential veto.

At first glance, it would appear that growth for the stock market's No. 1 catalyst will continue unabated for the foreseeable future, thanks in part to Trump becoming "Mr. Veto." But a surprising Election Night result may end up negating the president's veto power.

While the bulk of Election Night coverage focused on Democrats retaking the House and Senate, the real surprise was that five governorships flipped from Republican to Democratic control. According to the Associated Press, as of 11 p.m. ET on Nov. 4, Democrats now hold 28 governorships, with two races left uncalled. It marks the first time since 2010 that Democrats have a majority of governorships.

It's not this majority that puts the president and Wall Street in a precarious situation. Rather, it's the states Democrats flipped that present a serious challenge to the stock market's No. 1 catalyst and President Trump's veto power.

As of this writing, the five governorships that flipped are in Texas, Ohio, Georgia, Nevada, and Iowa. Texas, Ohio, and Georgia rank among the top states for AI data center planning and construction.

Greg Abbott is still approving data centers in Texas despite his election year “pause.” That’s Abbott’s playbook: he sells us out and then lies about it. https://t.co/ozFasjNs3W

Democratic Governor-Elect Gina Hinojosa of Texas has proposed a ban on data center approvals until her state's legislature passes laws governing electricity, water, air, and property rights. Most Democratic lawmakers believe in limiting the pace of data center expansion and beefing up the oversight of AI innovations and large language models.

Although President Trump retains the ability to veto AI legislation passed by Congress, Democratic governors can slow the pace of the AI infrastructure build-out at the state level, effectively steering clear of the president's veto.

This subtle shift in gubernatorial control can have massive implications for Wall Street. For instance, the stock market entered 2026 at its second-highest valuation since January 1871. History has conclusively shown that premiums of this magnitude aren't well-tolerated on Wall Street and often lead to significant bear markets. In short, Wall Street is ill-equipped to deal with a slowdown in the AI data center build-out.

Credit markets are flashing WARNING SIGNS across the AI trade: Oracle, $ORCL, 5-year credit default swap (CDS) spread has surged to ~263 basis points, while SpaceX's has risen to ~203 basis points, both at record highs. At the same time, CoreWeave's $CRWV, 5-year CDS is up to a record 982 basis points. This comes as tech companies have issued nearly $500 billion in new debt this year to finance AI infrastructure, with 9 US investment-grade bond deals worth at least $25 billion each. The growing debt burden is colliding with uncertainty over whether AI revenues will justify the enormous spending. As a result, investors are increasingly demanding higher compensation to take on credit risk. Oracle's market-implied 5-year default risk has risen above 20%, compared with around 16% for SpaceX and more than 7% for Nvidia. The pressure could intensify further, with Goldman Sachs projecting hyperscaler debt issuance to jump from $250 billion in 2026 to a massive $420 billion in 2027, as 35% of next year's capital spending is expected to be debt-funded. The AI boom is now testing credit markets, as investors begin repricing the risks behind Big Tech's unprecedented borrowing spree.

We're also seeing evidence from the bond market that this shift may be problematic for some hyperscalers, neoclouds, and select AI hardware companies. Whereas corporate bonds should, ideally, trade near par value, CoreWeave's (CRWV +6.28%) 2031 notes, Oracle's (ORCL +0.82%) 2065 notes, and Nvidia's (NVDA +2.77%) 2060 notes are trading at $0.79, $0.73, and $0.63 on the dollar, respectively.

These sizable discounts to par value show that bond traders want considerably higher yields (bond prices and yields are inversely related) to offset the risk of holding debt tied to the AI data center build-out.

Even if President Trump becomes Mr. Veto, the gubernatorial blue wave can fundamentally alter the AI regulatory landscape.

#3158. Linux Mint 27 arrives next month, and I like it already: New features and other upgrades to expect

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Linux

It starts with a surprise version number: Linux Mint 27 instead of 24. But the next release brings far more changes than that.

Linux Mint, my favorite Linux desktop distribution, is ready for a fresh start.

Its lead developer, Clement “Clem” Lefebvre, announced earlier this year that the team behind it was considering slowing Mint’s release cadence because the old twice-a-year release cycle was time-consuming and constrained their ability to make major changes. So it is that the next release, Linux Mint 27, will appear in your Christmas stocking.

Also: 5 premium Linux distros absolutely worth paying for in 2026

The developers officially changed the designation on Oct. 13th when Mint changed both its release cadence and its numbering. Clem’s happy about the change: it “was a tough decision to make, but we’re really starting to see it pay off. We’re taking on projects and implementing changes which would previously have been considered too large or ambitious.”

The new Mint will use Ubuntu 26.04 Long Term Support (LTS) as its base. As before, it will offer Cinnamon, Xfce, and MATE desktops.

Earlier development snapshots had been labeled Mint 23 “Alfa.” That title was explicitly a placeholder while the team decided its release strategy. Its final name is Adrien. Moving ahead, release numbers will be based on the upcoming year. Clem said, “We’ll no longer use point releases, so the release after that will be 28. You’ll upgrade from 27 to 28 to 29, and so on.” Instead of girls’ names, Mint will use boys’ names for distro titles.

On Halloween, Clem announced the top changes in Mint 27. It starts with Mint bringing hibernation back into its supported desktop configuration through a dedicated Sleep Modes page in its System Administration tool. The page is intended to make enabling and configuring hibernation easier, including addressing its swap-space requirements.

It may not sound like much, but Clem explained it’s actually rather a big deal. “When you close the lid of your laptop, it goes into a sleep mode called “Suspend”. This mode is very useful… but it has a major limitation: It uses power.” People often forget it’s not really off and that your laptop will eventually run out of power. When that happens, “the outcome is worse than a normal shutdown. The session is gone, and the battery is empty.”

Also: Linux Mint vs. RefreshOS: I compared the two best distros for new users

That’s where “Hibernation comes into play. It saves the session to disk and completely shuts down the computer. No power is used. When you reopen the lid, the session is as it was, and so is the battery.”

Another new feature is Superlight. This fresh system-monitoring application replaces the older GNOME System Monitor. It comes with CPU, RAM, swap, GPU, network, disk I/O, temperature, and fan-speed graphs. It also includes per-application and per-process resource views. Battery-equipped systems also get charge and power-flow history.

Its storage analysis is particularly interesting: it can break down disk usage into files and folders, Steam games, and Flatpak applications. That brings several common troubleshooting tasks — “What’s using the CPU?” and “Where did my disk space go?” — into one interface. I’ve been checking it out on a Mint virtual machine (VM), and I like it already.

The Nemo file manager is getting a media preview pane, toggled with F7 or a status-bar button. It supports pictures, audio, and video, and displays associated information — for photographs, that can include the capture date and camera model. I like this feature a lot. I’ve always done a lot of work from file managers going all the way back to the classic Norton Commander and its Linux successor, Midnight Commander.

I’ve said it before, I’ll say it again. You don’t need to ever touch a Linux shell via a terminal to use Mint. But if, like me, you’re an old-school Linux user and you like using the terminal, Velocitty, Mint’s new terminal application, looks interesting. Its announced features include:

The point is to make recurring projects and SSH sessions easier to find and reopen. If you like other terminal programs, my latest fave is the Rust-based WexTerm; you don’t have to use Velocitty.

Unfold is a new archive manager for viewing, creating, and extracting archives. It uses 7-Zip and 7zip-RAR and supports the encryption formats those backends handle, including password-protected archives. Its design goal is simplicity rather than a sprawling feature set.

The new Mint uses Ubuntu 26.04 LTS as its foundation. Mint is also moving from Ubiquity to the Linux Mint Debian Edition live installer for installing the operating system. This fresh installer comes with support for OEM bulk-produced installations, installation on both BIOS and EFI PCs, support for Secure Boot, and LVM/LUKS configurations.

Also: Linux Mint vs. Zorin OS: I’ve tried both Windows alternatives, and here’s my winner

For the Linux kernel, Mint 27 will use version 7.0. Which one exactly? Good question. We’ll see closer to Christmas.

Wayland compatibility remains the goal, but X11 isn’t disappearing. As Clem said in June, “In an ideal scenario, we manage to make Wayland work just as well as X11.” But they’re not there yet.

The central change is a cleanup of existing code. Mint’s longer cycle gives developers room to overhaul several parts of the desktop together, rather than squeezing individual improvements into six-month windows. As Clem put it in the September newsletter, “I don’t think I’ve ever seen so many changes and new features implemented for a single release.”

What that means for you and me will be better laptop power management, more capable built-in utilities, and a more complete collection of everyday desktop applications. For example, I expect the updated Mint will ship with GIMP 3.2.2 as its default photo editor, instead of Mint 22.3’s years-old GIMP 2.10.

#3157. Bluetooth 7 Speeds Are Set to Quadruple in 2027, Especially for Audio

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

The Bluetooth Group has announced a new standard. Here's how it works and when you can expect to benefit from it.

Senior Editor / Home Security and Smart Home

Senior Editor / Home Security and Smart Home

Wireless tech is about to get a whole lot snappier. On Thursday, the Bluetooth Special Interest Group, the trade association that oversees Bluetooth, officially unveiled Bluetooth High Data Throughput, a flagship upgrade set to arrive as part of the Bluetooth Core 7.0 standard. The revision supercharges Bluetooth Low Energy by pushing maximum bit rates to 7.5Mbps, effectively quadrupling the throughput of the existing LE 2M technology. Compatible devices are slated to hit store shelves starting in 2027.

That’s good news for phone users, tablet havers, laptop groupies, smart speaker stans and more. Bluetooth is still the most popular medium-to-short-range wireless standard for connecting devices. But this is a big rollout, and the changes will start with premium wireless, such as earbuds, with other applications following.

Those other applications could include spatial computing, exchanging AI-related info with local devices, connecting to health wearables and a host of smart home applications, like playing Spotify through an Amazon Echo Studio or dimming a smart bulb.

“In practical terms, a smartwatch could sync hours of sensor-rich training data to a phone in seconds. A smart home product could complete a firmware update with little visible downtime,” Damon Barnes, Director of Technical Marketing at the Bluetooth Special Interest Group, told CNET. “Smart glasses and other AI-enabled devices could exchange more data with a companion device or the cloud. And in audio, the additional capacity can support richer experiences, including high-resolution, lossless, surround, and spatial audio.”

A representative from the Bluetooth Special Interest Group didn’t respond to a request for comment.

The biggest change in this update is a new physical layer for Bluetooth. Layers are like different sections of the Bluetooth protocol, and the physical layer is the bedrock of the standard, the foundational layer involved in transmitting and receiving data signals.

Improvements in the physical layer can have very significant effects on all Bluetooth communications. In this case, new modulation schemes let the physical layer handle data more efficiently and transmit more data at once, which is why the speed increases are so significant.

These improvements also make the new standard more efficient, saving battery life in many applications. So your smart lock may last longer before you need to exchange the AA batteries, earbuds won’t need to go back in the case as fast, and so on.

We know the first flagship devices are expected to appear with the technology in early 2027, but don’t have an exact date.

Barnes also told use that since the update is starting with wireless audio, the first devices to see it will be headphones, earbuds and speakers.

With protocol changes like this, it can take some time for new products to reach the market, and manufacturers may take a long time, even years, to adopt it.

#3155. Got $5,000? 2 Growth Stocks Building the Software Backbone of Artificial Intelligence (AI).

Chips get the headlines, but Microsoft and CrowdStrike sell the software that puts AI to work and keeps it secure.

Artificial intelligence (AI) has been a gold mine for companies selling chips and building data centers. But without software, those AI chips don't really have anything to do.

That's why I think software stocks are as important as chipmakers and hyperscalers when you consider how to invest in the AI build-out. The more companies depend on AI, the more important those software services become.

Two companies that stand to benefit are Microsoft (MSFT -0.99%) and CrowdStrike (CRWD -0.14%). They approach AI from different directions, but both are already seeing growing demand for their products. With $5,000 to invest, I'd consider splitting my money equally between these two outstanding software stocks.

You likely already use Microsoft software, though you may not realize it. Microsoft makes some of the most popular software programs, such as Word, Excel, PowerPoint, and Outlook. All of them are available in the company's popular Microsoft 365 suite.

In addition, its Azure cloud platform supplies the infrastructure needed to develop and run AI applications. Azure AI Foundry provides tools for developers to build those applications, while GitHub Copilot helps them write code.

The strategy is paying off. In Microsoft's fiscal first quarter of 2027, which ended Sept. 30, Azure and other cloud services revenue increased 48% year over year to $33.16 billion.

Microsoft has also incorporated Copilot in its 365 software suite of office tools. During the quarter, Microsoft reported that daily Copilot users increased nearly 50% sequentially and more than fivefold year over year. The number of customers with more than 50,000 Copilot seats also increased more than eightfold year over year. That's particularly encouraging because it demonstrates that businesses are willing to pay for AI capabilities within software they already use.

Microsoft reported $677 billion in commercial remaining performance obligations, representing contracted revenue that hasn't yet been recognized. That figure increased 73% year over year, although commitments from OpenAI contributed substantially to the growth.

If there's a downside to Microsoft stock now, however, it's in spending. Microsoft is dropping billions of dollars every quarter to satisfy AI demand. Capital expenditures reached $45.2 billion in the quarter, and management expects spending to increase to $50 billion in Q2.

Those investments are putting pressure on margins, and there's no guarantee they'll produce the returns investors expect.

Still, Microsoft generated $18.6 billion in quarterly free cash flow despite its spending. That gives the company considerable financial flexibility as it expands its AI business.

While AI offers businesses new opportunities to develop new products and work more efficiently, it also creates new opportunities for cybercriminals. And unfortunately, there are always bad actors looking for an opportunity to break the rules and make a quick buck.

Companies that deploy AI agents capable of accessing sensitive information and performing tasks without constant human supervision are susceptible to cyberattacks, and CrowdStrike addresses these risks through its Falcon cybersecurity platform.

During the company's fiscal second-quarter earnings call, CEO George Kurtz discussed the emerging market for AI detection and response (AIDR). He suggested that protecting AI agents could eventually become a larger business than traditional endpoint detection and response.

That's an ambitious prediction, but CrowdStrike's existing business is already producing impressive results. In the quarter ended July 31, revenue increased 26% year over year to $1.47 billion. Annual recurring revenue (ARR) reached $5.84 billion, up 25%, while net new ARR jumped 51% to a record $333 million.

And there's evidence that customers are expanding their relationships with CrowdStrike. The company reported that ARR from accounts adopting its Falcon Flex subscription model exceeded $2.29 billion, up 101% from the previous year. Falcon Flex allows customers to adopt additional security products under a flexible purchasing arrangement.

So, CrowdStrike doesn't have to rely exclusively on finding new customers. It can generate additional revenue by selling more services to businesses already using its platform.

As businesses adopt AI and introduce new security vulnerabilities, CrowdStrike has an opportunity to expand well beyond its original endpoint protection business.

Microsoft and CrowdStrike aren't competing for the same AI opportunity. Microsoft provides the infrastructure and applications that businesses use to develop and deploy AI. CrowdStrike provides security services that could become increasingly necessary as those applications gain access to more sensitive systems and information.

Both have demonstrated that customers are willing to pay for their services. For long-term investors, that's a more compelling argument than simply betting that AI will continue to grow.

Patrick Sanders has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends CrowdStrike and Microsoft. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3153. 3 ways Bluetooth 7 upgrades your devices beyond lossless audio

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Bluetooth Core 7.0 could make your smart home devices easier to set up, for one. Here's what we know about the new core specification.

Bluetooth Core 7.0 is here, and each new specification from the Bluetooth Special Interest Group (SIG) offers a glimpse of what we can expect from our devices in the future. The major feature incorporated into the new core standard is High Data Throughput (HDT), which can enable lossless wireless audio and improved multi-channel spatial audio on more Bluetooth devices.

Also: Apple has a Bluetooth problem, and Android is taking advantage

Bluetooth Core 7.0’s standout feature is that it can achieve wireless lossless audio. However, the improvements to the Bluetooth stack architecture — particularly, expanding the Bluetooth spectrum — allow for faster data transfer, improved energy efficiency, more resilient connections, and create more room for devices and their data-intensive applications.

HDT means good news for audiophiles, smart home enthusiasts, VR/XR fans, wellness wearable aficionados, AI companion devotees, and much more. I explained applications of HDT in a wireless audio context earlier this year. Now that it’s officially adopted, here’s what you can expect.

A major milestone in Bluetooth Core 7.0 is the ability to stream lossless audio over a wireless connection. Bluetooth is lossy, requiring data compression that diminishes audio quality. HDT’s faster data transfer and greater wireless data capacity enable it to handle the massive volume of data required to achieve lossless quality.

Also: Your Bose headphones are getting a major Bluetooth upgrade – what to expect

Audiophiles typically avoid wireless headphones for intensive listening due to the necessary data compression, opting for wired headphones that can accommodate larger lossless files. Bluetooth’s innovation here is widely regarded as a major leap in wireless technology.

HDT also promises to standardize spatial audio, with expanded data transfers allowing for multi-channel audio setups. Standardizing this form of audio transmission could mean consumers can expect more affordable brands and products to adopt this premium feature.

Every consumer-friendly feature possible within Bluetooth Core 7.0 can be traced back to its ability to accommodate more devices and their ever-expanding data needs. According to a Bluetooth blog post, Bluetooth HDT delivers nearly four times the previous maximum Bluetooth throughput.

Data throughput is the amount of data received, transmitted, and processed over the Bluetooth network. With nearly four times the processing power, HDT can move more data, faster. HDT increases Bluetooth’s maximum supported bitrate to 7.5 Mbps, up from the current 2 Mbps.

Also: Beware of Bluetooth sniffers: How your favorite gadgets could be exposing your data

The result is shorter download times for a device’s firmware update, faster device location when Find My Device is activated, or faster transfer of a large photo album via Android’s QuickShare or Apple’s AirDrop.

In the same blog post, Øyvind Strøm, executive vice president of the short-range business unit at Nordic Semiconductor, gives examples of a smartwatch syncing hours’ worth of workout data to your phone in seconds, or a pair of headphones, a smart speaker, or a security camera that can download a firmware update with nearly zero interruption to the user experience.

In a smart home context, Bluetooth Low Energy (LE) is the wireless technology that works in the background, connecting your devices. Bluetooth LE is the baseline technology for quick device discovery and connection. The most recent example of this is Apple’s HomeView, which uses Bluetooth LE to discover a nearby, trusted iPhone to unlock that user’s personalized home hub display.

The less you feel its presence through lags, wait times, and dropouts, the better it’s working. HDT’s goal is to make Bluetooth connectivity frictionless, which is especially valuable when your smart lights, shades, speakers, TVs, refrigerators, and more rely on the technology.

Also: Forget Wi-Fi: Apple is betting on Bluetooth with its new home devices

For example, HDT’s improved data throughput could allow doorbells, cameras, and robot vacuums to quickly offload large amounts of data to your smart home hub. It could also enable near-instantaneous setup of a new device onto your smart home network and firmware updates.

Finally, HDT also addressed network stability and reliability, especially in high-traffic areas such as apartment complexes and dense cities, where a busy network can cause delays and backups. Alongside improvements to Wi-Fi and Thread, a truly seamless smart home is on the horizon.

Bluetooth’s Human Interface Device (HID) profile refers to the wireless communication used by peripheral devices, such as mice, keyboards, and game controllers, when connecting to a host device. Smart glasses and controllers connected to XR/VR headsets also use this profile, and their data-intensive computing will benefit greatly from HDT.

Devices using the HID profile can expect HDT to lay the foundation for making cloud gaming more reliable, spatial computing more responsive, and offloading audio/visual data from smart glasses less power-intensive.

Also: Frustrated with your Bluetooth? How multipoint works – and why it sometimes won’t

Additionally, gaming keyboards and mice can expect HDT’s expanded data processing to deliver shorter device communication intervals, reducing latency and lag.

In 2026, we’re starting to see more Bluetooth Core 6.0-equipped devices, with Samsung’s recently announced Galaxy Buds On featuring Bluetooth 6.2. Though Bluetooth’s blog post states that the first application of HDT will be for premium wireless audio, many audio manufacturers are behind, still releasing products with Bluetooth 5.4.

Smartphones, smartwatches, and smart home products tend to move a little faster, with the majority of current Bluetooth Core 6.0 products falling into these categories.

The Bluetooth SIG announced Auracast in 2022, and audio manufacturers are now beginning to adopt this technology more widely. Channel Sounding was released with Bluetooth Core 6.0 in 2024, with some early adopters releasing compatible products this year, including Google’s Pixel Tag and Motorola’s Moto Tag 2; more are expected next year and into 2028. With these adoption cycles in mind, we may be well into the next decade before HDT becomes standard.

#3152. The Austin Powers steamroller: How to trade CoreWeave's slow moving crisis

In Austin Powers: International Man of Mystery, a Virtuocon guard famously stands in front of a commandeered steamroller, screaming "No!!" three times before being run over. The joke, of course, is that the hazard moved so slowly he had ample time to step out of the way.

For months, equity traders holding CoreWeave (CRWV) have played that guard. Despite credit markets flashing warnings, equity holders essentially just stood there. Now, after a shocking 27.5% drop from last Friday's close to this Thursday morning's 52-week lows, followed by a whiplash 17% rebound, equity investors, if not squashed like Dr. Evil's henchman, are dazed and confused. Shares are down another 5% today.

With earnings just a few weeks away, massive leverage, a heavily split sell-side, and 15.4% short interest, the stock remains primed for explosive volatility.

The hazard approaching CRWV equity wasn't hidden. The AI cloud infrastructure platform essentially didn't exist five years ago and has accumulated $51.6 billion in debt (~$46 billion net of cash) against a backdrop of zero historical profitability. That was well known, but the CEO's comments that the company aspired to be investment grade so it could borrow "unlimited sums", even as investors already anticipated nearly another $60 billion in debt in the next 18months, spooked folks. (Trick or Treat!) Especially since the debt issued only in June had already fallen 20% since.

From an options trader's point of view, it was hard to reconcile deteriorating credit and rising leverage with falling options premia; as recently as last week, they were in the 6th percentile, 1.5 standard deviations below the one-year mean. At 72%, options weren't cheap in absolute terms, but given everything going on, they should have been higher. The stock also looked somewhat complacent, with the equity chopping around historical support levels dating back to May 2025. The credit side was on tilt:

Because CRWV is levered by more than 2:1, a 10% move in enterprise value translates to a nearly 22% swing in equity value.

Despite the dire credit picture, equity sentiment remains fiercely divided, driving this week's 17% bounce from the lows. For example, HSBC maintains a Sell rating with a $39 price target, essentially calling for the stock to be cut in half. Conversely, banks like BNP and Cantor Fitzgerald (my former employer) suggest these fears are overblown and the stock should more than double from here.

It's not just bankers defending the company either. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang downplayed systemic concerns, and ARK Investment stepped in to buy 192,000 shares. BofA argues that CRWV's ability to bring active power online at scale, combined with higher pricing from Nvidia's upcoming Vera Rubin deployments, will drive faster-than-expected revenue growth. They described execution risk as a bigger concern than financing; I'm not sure those who bought the 2032 8 1/2s at par would agree.

With CRWV's credit heavily distressed but its equity heavily defended by high-profile dip-buyers, going outright long or short into earnings carries excessive directional risk. A November/January Call Diagonal, currently trading for just under $6 per spread. Almost 9% of the current stock price. Normally, that's a lot, but given this stock has fallen 27.5% and then gained 17% in the past 5 sessions, it isn't.

#3151. OpenAI and Anthropic Drop New AI Tools for Cybersecurity, Images and Finance

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Here are three AI product drops you might've missed this week.

Every week is a busy week in the artificial intelligence industry, and this week is no exception. As voters expressed serious concerns about AI and data centers at the ballot box, OpenAI and Anthropic — makers of ChatGPT and Claude, respectively — each dropped new updates to make their chatbots a little more capable.

All these changes come amid rapidly changing government and financial environments. The midterm elections, in which Democrats handily beat Republican candidates across the country, showed that anti-AI and anti-Big Tech campaigns resonated with voters. Tech critics told CNET this week that there is a clear desire for government officials at every level to change how they interact with technology companies — to keep their campaign promises. The election results are also shaking up Wall Street, as investors’ fears about AI’s long-term financial viability grow.

Smaller updates like these are helpful for everyday AI users, and there’s a benefit for AI companies, too. They can prove they’re competitive in a market that is losing confidence in AI. Here are the three biggest AI updates to ChatGPT and Claude.

Gone are the days when every new AI tool got an immediate general release — and that’s probably for the best. Now, when a major AI lab has a new frontier AI model, it slow-rolls its access, and it starts with cybersecurity defenders. That’s exactly what OpenAI did with its newest model variant, GPT-6 Cyber.

Introducing GPT‑6 Cyber: a major advance in security research that pairs stronger capabilities with better model alignment.The model powered the Linux kernel research we announced yesterday, and security teams at companies like Mozilla, Palo Alto Networks, Calif, and… pic.twitter.com/34uAeNnhwP

GPT-6 Cyber is a version of OpenAI’s newest flagship model that’s specifically made for security research and work. OpenAI says it’s best at finding vulnerabilities and staying in its secure container. Those two things matter a lot. Earlier this fall, an OpenAI model was partially responsible for helping researchers identify a major vulnerability in PHP, a programming language that’s used in the majority of websites. And any improvements to keep AI secure are necessary. We saw many instances this summer of AI agents breaking out of their testing environments and making cybersecurity experts fear future autonomous hacks.

You won’t be able to use GPT-6 Cyber unless you’re a cybersecurity expert who’s part of OpenAI’s Daybreak program. Cisco and Mozilla teams are already using the model, according to OpenAI.

OpenAI also added a minor model update to its AI image model, called ChatGPT Images 2.6. The biggest improvement is its speed; OpenAI says the new model is 50% faster than before. That matters for AI enthusiasts who are generating multiple variations of a prompt. The new model also lets you animate your AI images into short GIFs. It’s available globally now for all ChatGPT users.

This isn’t the return of the long-dead Sora AI video model, but it is a sign that OpenAI isn’t giving up on the creative AI space entirely. Anthropic, by comparison, has never had a traditional AI image or video generator and opted for a design tool that’s better suited for mocking up websites and apps.

Anthropic is teaming up with fintech platform Plaid to help you bring your financial information into its Claude chatbot.

Both companies say they have strong security measures in place to keep your information safe. But CNET always recommends a cautious approach when it comes to sharing your financial information (or any sensitive information) with an AI product.

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, CNET’s parent company, in 2025 filed a lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

#3150. Billionaires Warren Buffett and Bill Ackman Deliver a Similar, Shocking Warning to Wall Street. Here’s 1 Thing Investors Should Do Now.

The billionaires have observed a particular type of behavior among certain investors.

The S&P 500 has advanced about 100% since the start of 2023, buoyed by investors' enthusiasm about artificial intelligence (AI) stocks. This movement wasn't based only on potential earnings growth but on compelling gains that were already beginning to unfold. Companies offering AI chips and cloud services saw revenue take off, and investors were optimistic that this was just the beginning.

Though various headwinds -- from rising inflation to concerns about AI spending -- have weighed on the picture from time to time, the overall momentum has continued this year. Against this backdrop, however, certain investing experts have stepped forward to pronounce important words of caution.

In fact, billionaires Warren Buffett and Bill Ackman delivered a similar, shocking warning to Wall Street. Let's consider their comments and check out the one thing you, as an investor, should do now.

First, let's start by taking a closer look at the stock market's performance so far this year. The S&P 500 has climbed, extending its three-year gain, and AI and other growth stocks continue to rise. That said, investors have also broadened their portfolios to include AI stocks that didn't soar in the earliest stages of the boom, as well as other industries.

Certain headwinds have interrupted the momentum here and there, from concerns about rising energy costs to worries about the depth of spending on the AI infrastructure build-out. Investors also questioned how the results of U.S. midterm elections would impact the market immediately afterward and in the months to follow.

Still, the overall positive momentum this year has reigned, and AI stocks have generally continued their march higher, helping the S&P 500 reach new record highs.

Now, let's turn to the warnings from investors Bill Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, and Warren Buffett, chairman emeritus of Berkshire Hathaway. Both of these billionaires used the word "casino" to describe the behavior of certain investors these days. This is shocking, as most investors turn to the stock market to invest and benefit over the long term -- not to gamble.

Just this week, Ackman told CNBC that speculation in the market was resulting in enormous price swings. And this is driven by leverage, or borrowed money poured into a variety of bets on or against particular assets.

"We're back to the casino-type markets when there was 95% leverage," Ackman said in the interview. "Every number of days, I feel like markets are broken."

This echoes something Buffett told CNBC a few months earlier, during the Berkshire Hathaway shareholders' meeting. He compared long-term investing to speculation, likening them to a church and a casino, respectively. He said the casino had become attractive for some, and "We've never had people in a more gambling mood than now."

So the message from these two billionaires is clear. A certain level of speculation is driving volatility these days, and quality stocks as well as major benchmarks have felt the impact.

Meanwhile, with the S&P 500 Shiller CAPE ratio trading at historically high levels, indicating that stocks are expensive, history shows us that declines may be ahead.

The Shiller CAPE ratio is an inflation-adjusted measure of stock price in relation to earnings per share. And over time, after it reaches a peak, the index generally slips. In certain cases, this may be brief and short-lived, while in others it may result in longer and deeper declines.

Considering the billionaires' warnings and the idea that a drop may be on the horizon, here's a key move investors should make: Seize the opportunity to buy quality stocks at bargain prices.

In recent times, investors have worried about AI spending, and that's left certain quality tech stocks trading at very reasonable valuations, such as Nvidia and Amazon, for example, trading at 21x and 24x forward earnings estimates, respectively. Even if these and other companies slip in the near future, their long-term outlooks are bright. It's impossible to get in on a stock at its very lowest, so it's important to make the move when valuation is reasonable -- and then sit back and hold on for a few years.

So, today, Ackman and Buffett are warning Wall Street about a certain amount of gambling behavior in the market. But instead of panicking, investors can use the dips as buying opportunities and potentially win big over time.

Adria Cimino has positions in Amazon. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Amazon, Berkshire Hathaway, and Nvidia. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3149. Claude’s new Personal Finance tool aims to help analyze your spending – read-only, of course

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Anthropic has released what appears to be its answer to ChatGPT Finances.

You can now share your financial information with Claude and ask the chatbot for insights on your spending.

Also: Researchers asked people to use AI chatbots for election info

Personal Finance in Claude, which Anthropic announced Thursday, reviews your connected accounts through read-only access via Plaid. That security measure follows OpenAI’s equivalent feature, ChatGPT Finances, which launched in May. Here’s what to know.

Functionally, this feature appears similar to ChatGPT Finances: It lets you link as many bank and credit card accounts as you’d like to Claude via Plaid with read-only access. You can then ask Claude where you spent your money this month or whether you can afford to take a vacation in six months, for example.

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, ZDNET’s parent company, filed an April 2025 lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

Also: Stop sharing your passwords with AI agents

According to Anthropic, the feature is a response to existing use patterns: the company’s April usage report found that financial advice was one of the main topics users ask Claude for help with.

“For the accounts you choose to connect (and only those), Claude sees the bank’s name, each account’s name, type, and last four digits, balances and credit limits, and up to two years of transactions. When you chat with Claude in the Personal Finance hub, those conversations aren’t used to train our models.”

The idea behind the feature is that you can ask follow-up questions and request more insights about spending habits, trends over time, and future goals based on what Claude finds, similar to any other Claude conversation. The tool will also generate tables and charts to visualize your financial information.

“You can ask for a weekly spending chart, a calendar of what’s due, or a dashboard of the numbers you choose, and Claude builds it as an artifact you can keep adjusting and coming back to,” an Anthropic representative told ZDNET via email.

Also: I connected ChatGPT to my bank, and it’s my go-to finance app now

You can also expect an ad-free experience in the feature.

“Like the rest of Claude, Personal Finance has no ads or sponsored recommendations, and it doesn’t offer credit cards, loans, or savings accounts of its own, so your money questions get a straight answer,” the company said in the announcement.

The company added that because Claude is using actual transaction data and not memory, the feature shouldn’t hallucinate.

“It can list the exact transactions behind any number so you can check its math,” Anthropic said via email. “Like any AI, it can make mistakes, so for anything important, we recommend confirming with your bank.”

Still, the difference — and for many, the appeal — here is the natural language interface. Other finance apps don’t necessarily have an easy, accessible chat function where users can shoot off quick questions like they would to a human financial planner. These assistants also present historical budget analysis in plain English, rather than requiring users to interpret dashboards themselves, which users may see as a perk.

Also: 5 ways to use AI when your budget is tight

On Reddit, many users find ChatGPT Finances to be accurate and reliable. One Reddit user said ChatGPT Finance seemed to have Mint or Monarch potential, but noted it was still “weaker” than those apps in some areas, such as historical syncing, high-quality dashboards, and generating full statement PDFs.

“ChatGPT has genuinely helped me make better financial decisions,” she wrote.

Anthropic noted that users shouldn’t look to Personal Finance for Claude as a total authority. “Personal Finance is a first step. For life’s bigger money decisions, we think people are best served by Claude working alongside the licensed professionals and regulated firms they trust,” the company said in the announcement.

“We’re exploring how to make that possible in Claude, and we’d like to hear from banks, wealth managers, and financial services companies who want to help shape it” — indicating a more integrated financial planning system within Claude may be on the way.

#3148. Pet Insurance on Your Mind? Buy It Now, Not Later

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

If your pet is young and healthy, the cost of coverage may not seem worth it. But that math can change fast.

Senior Writer & Licensed Insurance Specialist

It feels natural to protect your pet’s health with insurance in the same way you protect your loved ones with health insurance. After all, fur babies are part of the family.

But if you’ve shopped for pet insurance recently, you might have cringed at the cost. Should you really pay hundreds of dollars every year for something you might not use? For some pet parents, the answer is yes, even if their pet is young and healthy. And that’s partly because of how pet insurance handles pre-existing conditions.

A pre-existing condition is any illness or injury that shows symptoms before your pet insurance policy takes effect. Pet insurers usually won’t cover these conditions.

Dr. Rebecca Greenstein, chief veterinarian at Kleinburg Veterinary Hospital and veterinary expert for rover.com, says she’s seeing an increase in claim denials based on pre-existing conditions. “The whole point of insurance is you want broad spectrum coverage for a variety of things,” she says. “By the time they're excluding major organ systems and anything related to that, you may be paying monthly for coverage that doesn't actually help you when you need to go make a claim.”

If you’re considering pet insurance, it’s best to buy a policy before your pet needs it.

Your pet’s pre-existing conditions will determine which treatments and procedures insurers cover down the road.

Say your dog has a history of allergies. If you buy a policy after your four-footed friend has shown symptoms, pet insurers typically won’t pay for allergy shots or other treatments. The exclusion might also apply to related problems like itchiness or ear infections, says Dr. Michael Goldstein, medical director at Billerica Veterinary Hospital and Thrive Pet Healthcare.

He offers the example of a dog who goes to the vet for a hotspot one year and an ear infection the next. If you got pet insurance for your dog between those visits, the insurer might deny your claim because both issues could be caused by underlying allergies.

The best way around this type of scenario is to buy coverage when your pet is young, before any symptoms develop.

» MORE: The best pet insurance for pre-existing conditions

It’s tricky to figure out if pet insurance will actually save you money in the long run. The most common type of plan covers accidents and illness. It costs an average of $836 for dogs and $435 for cats each year, according to the North American Pet Health Insurance Association (NAPHIA). You could pay these premiums for years before an emergency surgery or chronic illness makes its way to your financial doorstep.

It’s impossible to know for sure whether your pet will stay healthy or run up sky-high vet bills. But you can assess the potential value of pet insurance by looking at common chronic conditions that pets develop with age and how much it costs to treat them.

For instance, “cats generally, as younger animals, don't have a lot of problems,” Goldstein says. “But as they get older, there are definitely common issues. Older cats in particular get chronic kidney disease.”

In cats over age 10, the Cornell Feline Health Center estimates that as many as 40% have chronic kidney disease. The annual cost to treat this condition can top $1,200.

Another example is atopic dermatitis from skin allergies, a common ailment affecting as many as 15% of dogs, according to the Cornell Richard P. Riney Canine Health Center. A single vet visit to address these itchy, persistent symptoms can cost $250 or more. And while that’s a fraction of what you might pay for pet insurance, costs of chronic conditions can add up over your pet’s lifetime.

Vet bills can also balloon quickly if chronic conditions require surgery. One example of this is hip dysplasia, a genetic disorder that can cause arthritis and pain in large-breed dogs. Depending on the procedure, you could pay up to $10,000 for hip surgery.

Many pet insurance plans cover treatments for hip dysplasia, including surgical costs, as long as it's not a pre-existing condition. However, some policies have waiting periods of six to 12 months for orthopedic issues like hip dysplasia. If symptoms develop during that waiting period, the condition will be considered pre-existing. That’s another argument for buying your pet’s coverage earlier rather than later.

Most importantly, having insurance means you can choose the best treatment for your pet no matter the cost. “It's really tragic when it's a life and death decision that is needed and it’s based on someone's financial constraints,” Greenstein says. “What is best and what's affordable often are not on the same side of the spectrum.”

One option to keep pet insurance affordable is to buy an accident-only plan. This type of coverage is usually much cheaper, costing about $190 annually for dogs and $112 for cats, according to NAPHIA. These plans cover injuries resulting from accidents, which include not just wounds and broken bones but also objects or toxins your pet might swallow.

However, an accident-only plan won’t help with some of the most common vet expenses. Illnesses, not injuries, are behind most claims submitted to pet insurer Lemonade, the company said in an email. In the past year, more than 60% of Lemonade's pet insurance claims stemmed from illnesses. That’s twice as many as the next-largest claim category, which was preventive care. A similar trend can be found in data from Nationwide, where chronic diseases make up a clear majority of pet insurance claims submitted to the company in 2025.

If you opt for an accident-only plan or no coverage at all, it’s still smart to set aside some savings for those inevitable pet emergencies and illnesses. This pool of funds can keep you from having to make difficult decisions about your pet’s care based solely on the cost of treatment.

#3146. 6 default Pixel 11 settings I changed that made a big difference – and why

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

If you’re a new Pixel 11 user, here are six things I recommend changing right away to get the most from your phone.

I’ve been a dedicated Pixel user since the beginning, so I was naturally excited to get my hands on Google’s latest iteration, the Pixel 11 Pro XL. I’ve been impressed with it overall, but I noticed that, as Google has done before, the device isn’t set up perfectly out of the box.

Also: Pixel 11 is $200 off in this early Black Friday deal

After spending a few weeks with the device, I found that several features are either turned off by default or need optimizing to work in the best way possible.

If you’re a new Pixel 11 user, here are six things I recommend changing right away to get the most from your phone.

The camera was the biggest reason I first joined team Pixel back in 2016, so it’s one of the first places I looked on the Pixel 11 Pro XL. New this year is Camera Looks, a setting that changes how images will be processed before you take them. You can change a number of settings, including contrast, shadows, tint, sharpness, and warmth.

Also: A week with the Pixel 11 changed my mind about Gemini

I’ve always loved the camera on any Pixel I’ve had, but I’ll admit that images can look a little over-processed at times. If picture quality is important to you at all, it’s vital to set this feature right out of the box because it applies to every image you take from that point on. You can find Camera Look by opening the camera app, tapping the settings icon in the bottom-right corner, and making your changes.

Pixel’s “At a Glance” lock screen widgets are one of the most sneakily useful features, and you’re missing out if you’re not using them. These widgets are off by default, but when you enable them, you can see real-time information without unlocking your phone. You can see weather info, sports updates, flight information, rideshare and food delivery updates, fitness information, and more.

Also: These tech gifts topped our experts’ 2026 holiday wish lists

To use these widgets, long-press on an open space on your home screen. To customize your widgets, go to Settings > Wallpaper & style > More lock screen settings > Widgets on lock screen. To access your widgets, you just swipe left from your lock screen.

Out of the box, your Pixel 11’s display isn’t set to utilize its full capability. The Pixel 11 series comes with a fantastic low-temperature polycrystalline oxide (LTPO) display, but it’s slightly throttled in the name of battery life. You’ll notice the increased resolution most on text and in fine details. It won’t make a huge difference to your streaming or large display elements, though. If you read consistently, either articles or e-books, you’ll want to make the change.

To change this, head to Settings > Display > Screen Resolution. Bump the setting from “High resolution,” which is 1080 x 2404, to “Max resolution,” which is 1344 x 2992.

If you’re anything like me, your phone usually needs a quick charge some evenings. The Pixel 11 series introduced Extreme Charging Mode, which accelerates charging by pausing background activity and non-essential apps. Google says the mode should charge the Pixel 11 and 11 Pro to over 50% in 30 minutes, and the Pixel 11 Pro XL to 75% in 30 minutes. From my testing, that estimate was pretty accurate.

Also: After testing the Pixel 11, I feel fine about skipping the Pro models

There are a few caveats: this feature is only available when your phone is at or below 50% battery, and you do need to turn it on every time you use it. But after the first time you do it, it’s available in your quick settings. Head to Settings > Battery & charging > Extreme Charging to turn it on the first time.

If you have the newest Pixel, you might as well take advantage of one of its biggest selling points. I felt like this feature was gimmicky at first (and I’m still a little grumpy it replaced my beloved thermometer), but the more I use it, the more I like it. The multicolor LED on the phone’s rear camera bump can indicate who’s calling, serve as a status indicator for Gemini, and more — and it’s almost certain that more usability will arrive over time.

Where I found the most use was when I had my phone on silent. After assigning specific colors to contacts (you can do this from the contact information screen), I could easily see whether a call was worth picking up without reaching for my phone.

To make sure you’re getting the most out of your new device, head in and change your contact HiLight colors right away. It only takes a few seconds and makes deciding whether to answer a call a lot easier.

We’ve all accidentally swiped away an important notification or missed a notification in a sea of updates. The Pixel 11 offers two notification features to help you stay on top of things.

Notification History is a feature that logs every alert from the last 24 hours, including those you swipe away. This is off by default, meaning when you dismiss a notification, it’s gone forever. With this on, you get a searchable, chronological backup.

Also: Google’s big flagship is more Pro than ever

AI Notification Summaries, as the name implies, takes long multi-notification threads and stacked notifications and turns them into bullet points. This feature is powered by on-device Gemini Intelligence, and you can choose which apps to summarize.

You can find both of these under Settings > Notifications > Notification history & Notification Summaries.

#3145. The Trade Desk Stock Has Lost About 89% Since the End of 2024. Its Nov. 9 Report Is the Next Test.

After missing its own forecast last quarter, can the ad tech company clear a lower bar?

The Trade Desk (TTD +0.94%) posts its third-quarter results after the market closes on Monday, Nov. 9. And the stock goes into the report about where the last one left it.

Shares dropped about 22% the day after the company's August forecast, finishing at $13.80. As I write, they trade near $13, after three months of closes between about $12 and $15.

Pull back further, and the damage is much bigger. The ad tech company's stock ended 2024 at $117.53, so it has lost nearly 89% since then.

That August forecast called for third-quarter revenue of at least $650 million, down around 12% year over year from $739 million. Management also guided for non-GAAP (adjusted) earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) of about $160 million, about half the $317 million it made on that measure in the third quarter of 2025.

I think Monday's report matters less for that third-quarter number than for what it says about later quarters.

The Trade Desk forecasts revenue as a minimum ("at least" a certain amount), and recently its results have been near that minimum, or under it. Fourth-quarter 2025 revenue of $847 million was $7 million over the company's floor. First-quarter 2026 revenue of $689 million cleared it by $11 million. Then second-quarter revenue grew just 3% year over year to $715 million, about $35 million below the "at least $750 million" management had called for. Adjusted EBITDA of $241 million also came in under the forecast of about $260 million.

That wasn't the first miss, either. The company called for at least $756 million of revenue in the fourth quarter of 2024 but brought in $741 million.

"In general, we don't think that guidance should really be considered conservative or aggressive. The goal really is to be credible and I think grounded in the data," said Nate Olmstead, The Trade Desk's new chief financial officer, on the second-quarter earnings call in August.

This makes $650 million the first number to watch on Monday. Clearing it could be an early sign the company's forecasts can be trusted again. Another shortfall would mean The Trade Desk missed its own floor for two straight quarters.

In its second-quarter 10-Q filing, The Trade Desk said gross spend (the total ad dollars clients put through its platform) climbed mostly because of campaigns from new clients, partly offset by lower spending from existing clients. In other words, the customers the company already had spent less with it.

Video (which includes connected TV, meaning ads on streaming services watched on internet-connected TVs) is the biggest channel, at a low-50s percentage of the business in the second quarter. Mobile followed at a high-20s percentage. Olmstead said connected TV and audio both grew at double-digit rates. But with total revenue up just 3%, the slowdown probably sat in mobile, display, or the rest of video.

The U.S. lagged, too. Its share of revenue was around 83% in the second quarter, down from 86% in the same quarter of 2025, which implies U.S. revenue was roughly flat while international markets did almost all the growing.

A quarter that hits $650 million mainly from new clients and overseas growth might still leave the biggest part of the business under pressure.

Last year's fourth quarter was the company's largest, with revenue of $847 million, up 14% from $741 million the year before. Another 12% drop would put this year's fourth-quarter floor around $745 million. A forecast over that might signal the declines are easing, while one under it may mean they're still getting steeper.

Earnings could also look worse on paper soon. For the four quarters through June, The Trade Desk earned $0.84 per share, putting the stock at around 16 times earnings. The total includes the third quarter of 2025, when the business earned $0.23 per share. Monday's report swaps it for a quarter guided at around half the adjusted EBITDA, plus severance charges of $39 million to $51 million linked to September's plan to shed about 15% of the staff. So the price-to-earnings ratio might rise even if the stock stays flat.

Ultimately, The Trade Desk doesn't need to return to growth on Monday. But it does need revenue of at least $650 million and a fourth-quarter forecast that drops less than 12%. Without that, I don't think the step-downs are over.

Daniel Sparks and his clients do not have positions in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends The Trade Desk. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.
