#3128. Alpine Linux and OpenSSH patch flaw that’s as bad as it can get

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

This dangerous Linux security hole must be patched as soon as possible.

The perfect nightmare scenario for a Linux server is when all an attacker needs to do is reach the server’s Secure Shell (SSH) port, waltz right in, and take it over. That’s what happened to Alpine Linux, the popular, lightweight Linux server. Adding insult to injury, this happened only days after Alpine Linux dodged a security hole-sized bullet to its GitLab implementation.

Also: Linux Mint 27 arrives next month, and I like it already: What to expect

How bad is it? As bad as it can get. Alpine Linux explained in its security advisory that if an attacker can reach a vulnerable server’s SSH port, they can get in “with one connection,” and the attack “works every time, and needs no retries.”

As security engineer Gregory Bryant explained in his LinkedIn post:

Most authentication bypasses come from complex logic chains, but yesterday’s Alpine Linux OpenSSH vulnerability (CVE-2026-123158) comes down to a single character: *.”

If you run Alpine Linux (or another musl-based distro) with the default openssh-server package (no Pluggable Authentication Modules (PAM)) and PasswordAuthentication enabled, an attacker can log into password-disabled, or key-only accounts simply by sending a password between 257 and 1024 bytes long.

Alpine Linux isn’t the only operating system that uses musl, a lightweight version of the standard C library (libc). Other operating systems that use musl and are potentially vulnerable include Adélie Linux; Void Linux; Sabotage Linux; OpenWrt, an Android variant,  and the Yocto Project, which is used to build embedded Linux distributions.

Also: This clever banking scam keeps phishing by faking its own demise

Wind River, which produces the commercial embedded Linux distro, Wind River Linux, is also looking into the issue. A company representative told me, “Wind River takes security seriously. We are actively reviewing the reported vulnerability and assessing its potential impact. We will take appropriate action as needed.”

Behind the scenes, when OpenSSH is built without PAM, it verifies passwords by hashing the password attempt using the C library’s crypt() function. It then compares the result to the account’s stored password field. So far, so good.

But when a key is longer than 256 bytes, musl’s crypt() function returns the string * as an error indicator. That is also what the password field holds for any account with no password, such as a key-only account. Thus, the “hash” matches and the login succeeds, and the attacker walks in.

Also: The new Cutefish Linux distro might be the best MacOS clone around

To exploit it, an attacker only needs the name of such an account. The attacker also needs at least one other account on the machine with a 5 or 6 password hash, and then to send a password of 257 to 1,024 bytes. According to Alpine, one user with a password is enough to make every passwordless account accessible.

In Linux systems that use glibc’s crypt(), which is almost all of them, the same attempt would return NULL on error, which sshd already rejects, so Debian, Ubuntu, Fedora, etc., aren’t affected.

According to *sshole, the group that uncovered the problem, this is a side effect that has been hiding since 2016.

Alpine said every release it checked is affected: 3.21 through 3.24, edge, and the older 3.16 to 3.20 branches. Exposure requires the following:

Root is not directly exposed in most cases. It has shipped as root:* in the base image since about Alpine 3.16, but OpenSSH’s default PermitRootLogin prohibit-password blocks it. Another account that can become root, for example through doas, still gives an attacker a route to root.

Alpine singled out its official tiny-cloud images, versions 3.18.11 through 3.24.3, as especially vulnerable. These ship with an alpine user with a * password field and passwordless doas. They are safe on first boot but become exploitable once any 6 hash exists on the machine. That can happen when an administrator sets a console password or when user data adds a user with a hashed password, which means, essentially, all of them.

Alpine released 3.21.10, 3.22.8, 3.23.8 and 3.24.4 on Thursday, with the OpenSSH fix among the security changes. It also published fixed packages for each release back to 3.16. Alpine 3.21 reached end of support on Nov. 1.

The just-released OpenSSH 10.6p2 avoids the problem in sshd. Alpine’s stable branches carry a patch for the same problem. You should run apk upgrade -U -a and then restart the daemon with rc-service sshd restart. Alpine stressed that upgrading the package does not replace the running daemon. Anyone who cannot update should set PasswordAuthentication no and restart sshd, after confirming that key login works.

Also: Maze Linux is the best security-focused distro I’ve ever tested

To check for an attack, Alpine told administrators to search /var/log/messages for “Accepted password for” lines that name an account with no password. It also advised reviewing dedicated OpenBSD application subexecutor (doas) use and recently created users, files, and authorized keys. “If you cannot rule out a login, treat the machine as compromised.” Just what you wanted for your weekend, I know, but here we are.

→ Baca artikel asli

#3127. This Janus Henderson fund manager owns CoreWeave debt — here's why he's sticking with it

CoreWeave can overcome the market's skepticism about its debt.

That isn't the consensus view — some Coreweave debt remains deeply distressed with yields above 15%. But Coreweave should power through this tough stretch, according to John Lloyd, global head of multi-sector and corporate credit at Janus Henderson.

"It's a little bit of overreacting," Lloyd tells CNBC, referring to the sell-off in Coreweave bonds, which have tanked along with its equity.

Lloyd owns Coreweave 9.75% bonds due in 2031 in the firm's Multi-Sector Income Fund. The yield to maturity on the debt is now 15.97%, a more than 70% jump since its issuance in April. Bond prices and yields move in opposite directions.

"Bond prices are lower, spreads are wider, yields are wider, and I don't see a huge fundamental change in the ecosystem right now that would warrant that, other than their higher cost of financing, which I think they can get creative with," Lloyd added.

CoreWeave has taken on a massive amount of debt to fund its data center buildout. The company has roughly $51.6 billion in total debt, including about $35 billion in notes, bonds and loans.

The market is getting nervous about how CoreWeave will be able to keep financing its data centers, and at what cost. Its stock has tumbled 20% over the past week, while the spread on its five-year credit default swaps is nearly 1,081 basis points — lower than earlier in the session but up 13% week over week. Spreads of 1,000 basis points and above are generally a sign of credit distress.

CoreWeave shares moved roughly 2% higher on Tuesday, after tumbling over the past several days and hitting a 52-week low earlier in the session. Its bond yields and spreads remain highly elevated.

Lloyd believes the selloff is a bump in the road for the neocloud company.

The market has been spooked by the jump in Treasury rates, which pushes up funding costs for companies, he explained. The focus largely turned to companies like CoreWeave that have refinance risk when rates move higher, he said.

CoreWeave leverage is high with a debt-to-equity ratio of 702%, up from 290% a year ago. Moreover, CoreWeave will spend an estimated $36.4 billion on capital expenditures this year against Ebitda of $7.5 billion, according to consensus estimates.

Revenue and Ebitda will ramp up next year, hitting $26.5 billion and $16.7 billion respectively, analysts estimate. But capex will hit $47.9 billion, indicating the company may need to finance its data center buildout with more equity issuance and/or debt.

Lloyd isn't necessarily worried about CoreWeave's ability to finance its capital expenditures, despite the fact that its capex outweighs its free-cash flow.

"Next year … they're going to be generating operating free-cash flow that'll support some of that capex need," he said.

The company can also turn to other methods of funding, like more GPU financing, which will allow it to borrow against its graphics processing units or rental contracts, he noted. In addition, CoreWeave could ask its biggest customers — including Microsoft, Meta Platforms and OpenAI — to increase their prepayments, he added.

"They can probably get creative enough where they don't even need to come to the high-yield market," Lloyd said.

The company also doesn't have any near-term maturities, which means it isn't facing a refinancing risk anytime soon, he added.

Meanwhile the supply/demand dynamic should remain tight across the AI compute landscape. Overall adoption rates within the ecosystem are running above consensus demand curves, he said. Add to that a bottleneck in supply, with chips and memory power sold out, and it's a nice set up for CoreWeave.

"All data point to the business model functioning very, very well," Lloyd said. "CoreWeave is one of the best-in-class at getting data centers up and actually running them," he added, noting that CoreWeave's financials have been improving throughout the year.

Another turning point could come if Anthropic goes forth with an IPO, expected in November, followed by OpenAI. Successful IPOs for the frontier labs could soothe market nerves and help support the neocloud operators.

"You just need more of those data points where people become more comfortable with the AI demand side," Lloyd said.

CoreWeave still needs to convince markets it can handle higher market rates and get through the financing bottlenecks.

Lloyd sees the company making it, albeit with some bumps along the way. The bonds should recover, he says, "just not in that straight line."

#3125. AMD Stock Has More Than Doubled in 2026. Here's How Its Last 8 Doubles Played Out the Next Year.

The chipmaker has seen eight other years like this one since 1981. Did the year after usually reward buyers?

Advanced Micro Devices (AMD +11.04%) shareholders are having a huge year. Trading around $486 as of this writing, the shares are up about 127% in 2026, on top of a 77% gain in 2025.

The company has kept up, too. AMD's third-quarter revenue grew to a record $13.4 billion, a 45% rise from the year-ago quarter, as data center sales almost doubled.

But a year like this isn't new to AMD. Going by year-end closing prices since 1981, the shares at least doubled in eight other calendar years. And the year after split evenly with four gains and four losses.

What does that record tell someone thinking about buying the shares now?

AMD's eight earlier double years are in the table, each beside the stock's price change in the following year. AMD has never paid a cash dividend, so every price change is the total return.

Data source: Yahoo Finance. Calculations by author.

Put simply, investors learned nearly nothing about the next year's direction from a double. AMD rose in 21 of the 45 full calendar years from 1981 through 2025, or around 47% of the time. After a double, it rose in four of eight. Either way, the odds were near a coin flip.

And 2020 almost made the list itself. The shares climbed just under 100% that year, then added another 57% in 2021.

The average follow-up year was far better than the split suggests, with a gain of around 33%. But that average leans on two years, 1983 and 2020, with gains of about 168% and 100%. The other six follow-up years averaged a small loss.

I think the more useful pattern is in the size of the losses. All four of AMD's down years after a double were fairly modest, ranging from a 9% decline in 2017 to an 18% drop in 2024.

A bad year for this growth stock normally looks far worse. AMD fell in 24 of its 45 full calendar years, and its typical decline in those years was around 31%. In 14 of the 24, it lost over 20%, including a 71% drop in 2008 and a 55% slide in 2022.

So, an investor who owned AMD heading into any of the double years kept most of the gain through the next year. The worst case was 2023 to 2024, when a 128% gain was followed by an 18% drop. Someone who held through both years still had around 86% more money than at the start.

Yes, four losing years is a small sample, and the next one might be worse. But the record does argue against the idea that a stock that doubles is due to give it all back. In none of those eight cases did the next year undo the double.

The year isn't over, of course. To finish with a double, AMD has to end December above about $428, or around 12% below where it trades as I write.

Calendar returns can also hide a lot of the ride in a year. AMD's highest close ever, $649.42, came on Oct. 6, when the stock was up around 203% for the year. By its Nov. 2 close of $466.84, it had slid around 28%.

Measured from that peak, AMD has already given back more than a third of its 2026 gain.

The pullback has at least made the price easier to defend. At around $486, the stock has a forward price-to-earnings ratio of about 30, using analysts' consensus estimate for 2027 earnings per share. At the October peak, that ratio would have been about 40.

But paying 30 times the earnings analysts expect for 2027 arguably assumes that sales of AMD's data center chips, including its artificial intelligence processors, keep growing fast.

So, how much of this year's rally should a new buyer expect to keep? Going by AMD's own record, a double alone isn't a reason to steer clear. The year after one was a coin flip, and the bad results were milder than the stock's typical losing years.

But eight years is a thin record. What a buyer should pay today depends on whether AMD's earnings keep up with the valuation. If I were buying now, I'd start with a small stake and expect something closer to the tamer follow-up years than 1983 or 2020.

#3124. What Does a ‘Blue Wave’ Mean for Mortgage Rates?

Democrats won big in the midterms, but mortgage rates have bigger forces to reckon with.

Mortgage rates fell again this week, extending a gradual slide that began well before Election Day.

The average rate on a 30-year fixed-rate mortgage fell 13 basis points to 7.05% APR in the week ending Nov. 5, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.) We calculate our weekly average using daily APRs recorded over the past five business days.

Homebuyers’ budgets are feeling the pinch from high mortgage rates, steep home prices and a shortage of listings. On the campaign trail, candidates from both parties promised to make homeownership more affordable. But solving that problem is like untangling 20 years’ worth of Christmas lights from your attic. Yank on one strand, and three more tighten.

“Jumpstarting the housing market relies on a calmer geopolitical environment and bond market in the short run and more housing supply plus a more dynamic labor market in the long run,” Jake Krimmel, senior economist for Realtor.com, said in an email.

A shift in political power won’t erase those challenges overnight. Here's what's driving mortgage rates right now — and where a Democratic Congress might have some pull.

How that affects mortgage rates: The relief of one less uncertainty is giving rates some breathing room.

Election results have cleared up one big unknown for investors. With Democrats controlling Congress and Republicans holding the White House, markets are bracing for the reality that any major spending bills could face roadblocks.

Political gridlock isn't always popular, but weirdly, investors sometimes welcome it. When a divided government limits new spending, the government may need to borrow less than expected — which could turn down the heat on mortgage rates.

But there’s a catch. That same gridlock could make it harder to pass laws to help bring down housing costs.

How that affects mortgage rates: Heavy borrowing from the government and tech companies adds upward pressure to the bond market, where mortgage rates take their cues.

When the government spends more than it collects in taxes, it borrows the difference by selling Treasury bonds. When there's lots of debt for sale, investors may demand higher returns, called yields. Mortgage rates tend to follow the yield on the 10-year Treasury note.

The new Congress still faces a mountain of government debt. What they choose to do about it can ripple through the economy to affect your monthly mortgage payment.

“Sound fiscal policy, rather than deficit-financed spending and standoffs over budgets and the debt limit, can help keep rates in check,” Andy Winkler, managing director for housing and infrastructure policy at the Bipartisan Policy Center, said in an email.

Meanwhile, Big Tech companies are borrowing a boatload of money to build AI data centers. To get the cash, they issue corporate bonds, giving investors another place to put their money. More bonds flooding the market can push Treasury yields higher, taking mortgage rates along for the ride.

But how long can the AI building boom last? Voters across party lines pushed back against data centers in the midterms. Even if lawmakers pump the brakes on new construction, a slowdown in tech spending doesn't guarantee cheaper mortgages.

How that affects mortgage rates: Wartime disruptions to global oil supplies can fuel inflation, pushing borrowing costs higher.

The unpopular war in Iran likely added to the voter frustration that helped fuel Democrats' midterm victories. Higher energy costs can hit household budgets in multiple ways: at the pump, through pricier goods that need to be shipped, and through higher interest rates on loans (including mortgages) as inflation heats up.

“A resolution would likely improve oil supply, helping to ease price pressures and potentially providing relief for mortgage rates,” Krimmel said.

A Democratic Congress could push for a different approach to ending the war, but it can't make Iran agree to a deal.

Meanwhile, stubborn inflation from the Iran war has raised the prospect of another Federal Reserve rate hike at its Dec. 8-9 meeting. Futures traders now put the odds of a quarter-point increase at more than 80%, according to CME FedWatch. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates directly, but investors anticipate its decisions — so mortgage rates often move before the Fed does.

If buying a home makes sense for your budget now, there's little reason to wait just because Congress changed hands. Buyers can sometimes score lower prices in winter, when sellers are more willing to negotiate. But with today’s mortgage rates hovering above 7%, higher borrowing costs can eat into those savings, so it’s important to compare mortgage lenders to get the best rate.

For longer-term hope, look to the bipartisan 21st Century ROAD to Housing Act, which became law this summer. This legislation shows lawmakers can, in fact, work together to make meaningful progress on housing affordability.

“Even with divided government, lawmakers and the administration can build on that common ground to further boost housing production, preserve existing affordable homes, and help families struggling with high housing and utility costs,” Winkler said.

But new homes take time to build, and your house hunt doesn't have to follow Congress's calendar. Your homebuying budget and local market conditions matter more than the next political headline.

#3123. Wendy’s Just Released a Video Game Where You Can Win Fast Food

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

I won a medium fries by defeating a sinister robot that looked suspiciously similar to a McDonald’s french fry container.

Wendy’s has turned its red-headed mascot into a wisecracking warrior who battles evil french fry robots in a free mobile game called WendyQuest. The company announced the retro-style role-playing game on Nov. 4. The announcement came with a promotion where you can win codes in the game, which are redeemable for menu items like fries and burgers at Wendy’s locations.

WendyQuest is available to download on iOS and Android. Desktop users can get it through the Steam platform. Anyone can play the game, but the promotion is restricted to people who are 18 and up living in the US.

You need to sign up for a Wendy’s account to redeem a code. While the food you can win is free, it requires you to buy something when you go.

“Wendy’s has been a part of gaming culture for years, including on Twitch, Fortnite and Feast of Legends, but WendyQuest is the brand’s first fully owned video game,” a spokesperson for Wendy’s said.

In 2019, Wendy’s partnered with the online multiplayer video game Fortnite, as well as the video-streaming platform Twitch, for various promotions featuring the company’s mascot. The company even released a rule book for a traditional tabletop roleplaying game the same year, called Feast of Legends, as a free download.

I downloaded WendyQuest, and my daughter and I played it for a while. The blocky graphics are reminiscent of the home-console video games of the late 1980s and early 1990s. It plays a lot like a simplified version of the DragonQuest, Final Fantasy and Breath of Fire games from the era, only stuffed with fast-food branding.

Rather than the regular turn-based battles that come standard in the early RPGs, this game adds a timing element, where you need to press a button at the right time like you do in old golfing video games.

It’s not a bad game. The light humor is suitable for younger players, and it’s fun to play. Like many RPGs, you battle monsters to gain levels, search around the maps for hidden areas, buy items and talk to people in towns to figure out what’s going on.

It took us over an hour to complete the first region, Fryshire, and get the Fry Stone, which unlocks a promo code for medium fries, free with a purchase. But again, you have to sign up for a Wendy’s account on their website or on the company’s mobile app.

Video games have a long history of offering real-world prizes to players as promotions. Swordquest, a series of video games released in the early 1980s for the Atari 2600, famously held a contest where players tried to win actual treasure made of gold and jewels.

In 2015, a promotion for the video game Just Cause 3 offered players the chance to win a private island. Developer Square Enix clarified that it couldn’t guarantee that the island would be livable and that the winner would have to pay all the taxes involved.

The reward codes from WendyQuest expire seven days after you get them, and Wendy’s says the codes are first come, first served.

#3122. Is Dutch Bros Stock a Buy as Shares Plunge?

Dutch Bros shares plummeted as same-store sales growth slows.

For the second time in two quarters, Dutch Bros (BROS -2.31%) shares collapsed following its earnings report, as investors fear that increasing competition is starting to eat into its growth. However, the company's overall same-store sales growth is still solid in a difficult consumer environment, and it continues to have one of the best expansion opportunities in the restaurant space.

The stock is now down more than 40% on the year, reversing the strong momentum the stock saw earlier in 2026. This looks like a golden opportunity to buy Dutch Bros in my view, as the stock's long-term underlying investment thesis remains unchanged.

Let's take a closer look at the coffee shop operator's third-quarter results and prospects and why I think it is a buy on this dip.

The biggest reason to own Dutch Bros is that it is one of the best expansion stories in the consumer space. The company opened 48 shops in Q3, of which 42 were company-opened, and plans to open at least 185 this year. It ended the quarter with 1,273 locations, of which 960 were company-owned shops. Meanwhile, it forecasted that it will open around 250 locations in 2027, which would be about 19% growth.

The company said it now has full visibility with its real estate pipeline to reach its goal of 2,029 shops in 2029. Its longer-term goal is to have 7,000 shops in the U.S., and it said it's being deliberate about how and where it grows, and it's not just simply about opening new locations and entering new states. In 2027, it plans to open most of its new shops in existing markets to benefit from increased density and to build brand awareness in these markets.

Dutch Bros shops typically have smaller footprints with two drive-thrus and no indoor seating, and are cost-efficient to build. This lets the company fund expansion fully with its operating cash flow. And while its shops are small, they generate a lot of revenue per location, with average unit volume (AUV) of more than $2.2 million. That helps the company get quick paybacks on new stores, increasing cash flow, and letting it funnel that back into new shops.

Dutch Bros also continues to deliver solid same-store sales. For Q3, comparable-store sales rose by 4.4%, as traffic increased by 0.4%. Company-owned stores yet again outperformed, with comparable-shop sales jumping 5.4% on a 1% increase in visits. The company credited its brand marketing and its rewards program for the growth.

CEO Cristine Barone said the company saw increased choppiness in Q3, but didn't feel that came from any increased competition. Instead, she blamed rising gas prices and said that its mix from energy drinks grew during the quarter despite a big competitor's launch in Q3. The company plans to continue to lean into menu innovation, including launching a matcha platform in 2027.

Dutch Bros' overall revenue climbed 29.8% to $549.8 million, while earnings per share (EPS) jumped 43% to $0.20. Adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) climbed 30% to $101.1 million.

Looking ahead, Dutch Bros projected 2026 revenue to be at least $2.1 billion compared to prior guidance for revenue of between $2.1 billion to $2.13 billion. Same-store sales are expected to be up at least 5%, which is below the 5.5% level the company said it was trending toward last quarter. It now expects adjusted EBITDA of roughly $380 million, down from its previous forecast of $385 million to $390 million.

Same-store growth in the restaurant industry is never a linear path, and there will be ups and downs based on things like the strength of the consumer and the lapping of popular new menu items. I would not be overly concerned with slightly slower same-store growth from Dutch Bros in what has been a tough consumer environment this year due to high gas prices stemming from the war in Iran and elevated overall inflation because of tariffs.

The recent sell-off, meanwhile, has put the stock squarely in the bargain bin. It now trades at a forward price-to-sales (P/S) ratio of 1.9, which is well below Starbucks' 3.2 multiple, despite Dutch Bros having the much larger growth runway ahead. Meanwhile, earnings growth will continue to climb at a faster pace than revenue as the company scales and spreads out fixed corporate costs over a larger base.

BROS PS Ratio (Forward 1y) data by YCharts

Dutch Bros is a top growth stock trading at a big discount, making the stock a buy following this sell-off.

#3121. Marriott Bonvoy Boundless vs. Bevy: Which One Comes Out On Top?

The right card for you may depend on where you travel, how often, and your level of elite status with Bonvoy.

Two of the popular credit cards in the Marriott Bonvoy portfolio deliver various benefits to their users. The right one for you may depend on your typical travel patterns and history, such as if you already have a high level of elite status with Marriott or you travel only to certain regions.

The Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card and the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card both come with valuable perks, but there are quite a few considerations that are worth a thorough review. Many travelers prefer not to have two cards from the same loyalty program, and these two cards are actually quite different from each other (including being issued by different banks).

Let’s take a look at these two Marriott co-branded cards to see if one may serve your needs over the other.

Here's how the cards stack up on the basics.

Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card

While the “winning” card for some may not apply to everyone, we wanted to analyze the card that provides exceptional benefits for travelers looking for a Marriott card in exchange for the cost of the annual fee. Those new to Marriott will find this to be a solid entry-level card, but it also works for Marriott Bonvoy fans looking for some extra perks without a huge outlay of money for an annual fee. These are the benefits that handed this card the win.

The annual fee on the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card is notably less at $95 compared with $250 on the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card. While the earning rates when using the card to pay for Marriott stays are nearly the same, and both have bonus categories where you can earn multiples of points based on everyday spending, the lower-annual-fee card offers a lot of points earning for its low annual fee.

On top of the lower annual fee, it comes with an automatic annual free night award, Silver Elite status and a fast path to achieve Gold if you use the card often. Add to that an annual $100 in airline credits and DoorDash DashPass membership, and you have more than covered the annual fee without considering the other perks.

The Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card card’s $250 annual fee is higher, and its annual free night award requires spending $15,000 in eligible purchases. But the card comes with automatic Gold Elite status and more bonus points earned.

There are other cards (like American Express Platinum Card®) that also include free Gold Elite status with Marriott that can provide decent value for their annual fee (enrollment required). From a pure cash maximization perspective, the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card wins because it provides more bang for your buck.

The Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card is the only one of the two cards to include an annual free night award, and it has a lower annual fee. It can be used at properties up to 35,000 points a night. This can render great value and is more than worth the $95 annual fee. Hands down, it is the winner in this category.

If you spend more than $15,000 on the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card in a calendar year, you can unlock a free night certificate valid at up to 50,000 points. This is even more valuable than the included certificate that comes with the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card, but it also requires you to spend more on this card to get it.

It is important to consider the opportunity cost of not putting certain types of spend on other cards where you could be earning greater multiples of points for the same type of spending. Would losing those points be worth the value of a one-night-free certificate? Since Marriott relies on dynamic pricing, which means that points rates for a redemption night can fluctuate regularly, in many cases the value may not reach the level of the missed points opportunity.

While both cards offer decent travel protection benefits, only the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card comes with a flight credit. You can earn up to $100 in statement credits when making purchases directly with airlines by spending $250 on the card before the end of 2026 for a $50 statement credit and another $50 credit by spending $50 before June 30, 2027. That gives another point to the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card Boundless card.

» Learn more: Should I be loyal to a single airline?

If you don’t already have a Marriott co-branded credit card and you want to diversify what’s in your wallet, this could be a great option for big spenders. And if you are looking for one of the fastest paths to high Marriott elite status, this card can help you get there.

Both the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card and Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card cards offer bonus points on spending, but the more expensive annual fee Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card card offers more points on more spending opportunities, including the below:

18.5X points on Marriott Bonvoy property spending (1.5X more than the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card card).

4X points at global restaurants, including U.S. takeout and delivery plus U.S. Supermarkets on up to $15,000 in combined purchases, then 2X points (that’s 1X more than what Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card offers and available on $9,000 more in spending).

1,000 bonus points when staying at a Marriott Bonvoy property.

Depending on how much you spend in these categories, you have the ability to earn more points with this card than the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card, including when you are not traveling. And then when you are, you earn 1,000 Marriott bonus points for each paid, eligible stay booked directly with Marriott. This gives it an edge, even though the annual fee is higher.

» Learn more: Hilton versus Marriott — Which one is right for you?

When you get the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card, you’ll receive 15 elite night credits deposited into your Marriott account. This is on top of the instant Gold Elite status you earn with the card, which normally requires staying 25 nights. To reach the next tier, Platinum, you only need an additional 10 nights to reach its 50-night minimum.

This means not only do you start out at a higher elite tier with Marriott just for having this card, but also you can progress more quickly to Platinum. For frequent travelers, the best value of this card is the opportunity to reach the next status tier faster. And if you wanted to keep going, you would only need 35 more nights to get to Titanium, which is rich with on-property perks like lounge access, welcome gifts and room upgrades.

Earning these nights within a calendar year is not difficult for Marriott loyalists, especially if you hit the jackpot during a promotion that offers double elite nights.

The status perks don’t stop there. Once you hit Marriott’s Titanium status, you can then link your account with United Airlines to earn United’s Premier Silver elite status for more benefits when you fly United, including greater baggage allowance and free extra-legroom economy seats at check-in.

» Learn more: Marriott elite status: what you need to know

Both of these cards provide Marriott Bonvoy points earning and elite status, and they both offer a great way to expedite points earning or receive travel and purchase protections when using the card. But the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card packs a greater punch in its benefits for a lower annual fee. It becomes a great additional card to others you may have (like the American Express Platinum Card®, which has its own impressive travel perk lineup).

If you are a Marriott frequent guest, the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card offers higher-tier Gold status and a faster path to even better tiers for perks. But, it costs more each year and does not offer a free annual night.

Deciding which perks are more important for you, as well as looking at what benefits come from other cards in your wallet, will help you make an informed decision on which of the two best suits your needs.

For rates and fees of the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card, please visit American Express' rates and fees page.

#3120. Nike Is Down 78% From Its Peak. Here's How Much $10,000 Invested Pays in Dividends Now.

The silver lining in its decline is the stock's considerable dividend yield.

The last five years have been among the most difficult in the history of Nike (NKE -2.45%) stock. Over that time, the stock has steadily slid and recently was down 78% from its all-time high.

The silver lining in this scenario is that it raises the dividend yield considerably. Thus, investors might be surprised to learn how much a $10,000 investment in Nike now earns in dividends.

Today, Nike pays its shareholders $1.64 per share annually, and it has a 24-year streak of payout hikes, a record that admittedly makes Nike stock hard to ignore. Amid the falling share price, the dividend yield is just under 4.3%.

As of the time of this writing, $10,005 buys 260 shares, meaning a shareholder buying today will earn just over $426 in annual dividend income.

Although that seems like a strong return, investors may have to worry about how Nike will pay for it. In fiscal 2026 (ended May 31), Nike's $2.2 billion in free cash was less than the $2.4 billion cost of the dividend over the same time frame. The situation worsened further in the first quarter of fiscal 2027 (ended Aug. 31), when free cash flow was negative.

Since Nike has $8.4 billion in liquidity, the dividend is probably not in immediate danger. Still, if Nike cannot increase its free cash flow, it could bode poorly for its payout.

Unfortunately, analysts forecast an 8% revenue decline in fiscal 2027, followed by a 1% increase the next year. Those forecasts strongly indicate that Nike's performance is not going to improve anytime soon.

Ultimately, income investors tend to be conservative, which will likely discourage taking a chance on a possible recovery. Unless Nike can rejuvenate its growth, this consumer discretionary stock may not be a great choice for a $10,000 dividend investment.

#3119. BlackRock's Rick Rieder sees 'historic' opportunity in high-quality bonds

Investors may be wary of the volatility hitting parts of the bond market right now, but they have an "historic" opportunity to grab attractive yields in high-quality credit, Rick Rieder, BlackRock's chief investment officer for global fixed income, told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Hypothetically, it's now possible for investors to build a diversified portfolio that yields more than 7% with less than three years of duration and an average credit quality of A-, he said. Duration is a measurement of a bond's price sensitivity to fluctuations in rates, and issues with shorter maturities tend to have lower duration.

"Not only is it historic in that these yields are where they are, but the only time these yields are ever here is when there's some problem, like an economic problem or default problem," Rieder said in an interview about his fourth-quarter outlook, released Wednesday.

That isn't the case now, where the economy remains strong, he said. "So you don't really have to worry about defaults of any significance."

While Rieder didn't specifically identify the credits in the aforementioned portfolio, the iShares Aaa - A Rated Corporate Bond ETF (QLTA) has a 30-day SEC yield of 5.68% and an effective duration of 6.48 years — higher than Rieder's low duration focus.

The iShares Flexible Income Active ETF (BINC), which is managed by Rieder, has a 5.66% 30-day SEC yield and three-year effective duration, but its portfolio extends beyond investment-grade credit.

Rieder sees the best opportunity right now in investment-grade corporate bonds — which he pointed out he hasn't said "in a really long time." Investment grade bonds are rated AAA through BBB- by S&P and Fitch, while Moody's rates them Aaa through Baa3.

That includes hyperscaler bonds, which have flooded the market with issuance this year as they ramp up their AI infrastructure. Alphabet, Amazon, Meta Platforms and Oracle have issued a total of about $228.4 billion in bonds year to date, as of Oct. 20, according to LSEG.

"They're very low leverage. They have tremendous amounts of cash flow coming in," Rieder said of the hyperscalers in general. "You're now getting to buy these entities which are higher quality, paying you a very attractive yield to get it."

Rieder's iShares Flexible Income Active ETF has upped its allocation to investment-grade corporates to 14.3% of its portfolio. In July, that stood at 8.5%.

That said, investors need to be patient and sit tight since rates could still move higher from here, he advised.

"Your starting point is by far your best barometer of what your return is going to be," he noted. "So if you get seven and a quarter, maybe you get eight, maybe you get six, but ... if your holding period is a year or longer, you're going to get that yield."

Caution should also be exercised outside of investment-grade credit, which requires more selectivity and deep research, he warned. That includes AI names.

"There's parts of the data center supply that's not hyperscaler guaranteed or hyperscaler backed," he said. "That we've been more cautious around, and we'll continue to be."

The bond market, particularly high-yield credit, has been rocked as investor concern grows over the massive amount of debt some companies are taking out to fund their artificial intelligence build-out.

Junk-rated CoreWeave has become the poster child, seeing its stock drop, bond yields jump and credit-default spreads widen. Oracle, which is sitting in the lower end of investment grade and is seen as a bellwether on the broader AI market, has also taken some hits.

Rieder is also keeping an eye on the Federal Reserve, which is expected to raise its federal funds rate again in December to combat inflation. The market is currently pricing in a nearly 90% probability that the central bank will hike at that time, according to the CME Group FedWatch tool.

The money manager expects that to happen, although he does not agree with it. Higher rates are hurting parts of the economy, like housing, while not having much of an impact on other areas — such as tech companies' capital expenditures, which have become a big driver of growth, he said.

"They're not borrowing off the overnight funds rate," Rieder noted. "There's a lot of floating rate finance in housing, credit card, auto loan finance, and you're seeing those areas already under pressure."

Instead, the market is and will continue to price the AI supply coming into the market, he added.

Rieder sees a number of possible risks ahead, including rising levels of government debt across the globe. In the United States, the budget deficit has grown to $2 trillion for fiscal 2026.

"The more the central banks raise the rates, the cost of debt to these to already indebted governments is something we've got to be careful about over time," he said.

Bond supply coming from AI companies should also be watched.

"With the amount of supply that's coming — data center, hyperscaler — there's some crowding-out effect that's taking place," he said. "With government rates moving higher, that is something that we just got to keep an eye on."

In the meantime, Rieder would just continue to reap the rewards of high investment-grade yields "and smile a lot," he said.

#3117. Physicists listened to electrical noise to crack a superconductor mystery

Tuning in to electronic noise can tell us whether electrons have paired up or not.

Superconductors carry electricity without resistance, but most only work a few degrees above absolute zero. In the late 1980s, copper-based materials called cuprates were found to superconduct at higher temperatures—reachable with affordable liquid nitrogen. Yet nearly 40 years later, physicists still don’t agree on why.

In a recent Science study, a team of physicists led by Milan Allan of Ludwig Maximilian University of Munich and Ingmar Swart of Utrecht University in the Netherlands has found some answers by listening to the faint electrical crackle of current flowing into a cuprate.

Central to the mystery of cuprates’ high-temperature superconductivity is a strange state called the pseudogap. “The pseudogap is a phenomenon of missing electrons,” Allan said. Above the temperature at which cuprates become superconducting, some of their electrons seem to vanish—measurements find far fewer of them at the energies where they should be. “Everybody knows that from different probes, you see that there’s these vanishing electrons, but nobody knows why,” Allan said.

Over the decades, physicists have put forward two main competing explanations. The first hypothesizes that the electrons get stuck in some sort of structure. In this view, electrons freeze into patterns, such as charge density waves, and superconductivity develops separately. The alternative explanation tightly links this phenomenon to superconductivity. “Other people think they’re missing because they form pairs,” Allan said. Pairs of electrons, called Cooper pairs, are what carry current in a superconductor. If, as the material cools, these pairs appear before the material becomes superconducting, they would be a precondition of superconductivity, a waiting room of sorts, where pairs exist but can’t yet move together in lockstep.

Settling this proved extremely hard. “Lots of different experimental signatures are kind of lumped inside this,” Allan said. Different instruments all see something, but their readings, Allan said, are never conclusive. “It’s always something kind of incoherent, hard to grasp,” he said.  What the researchers needed was a way to see pairs directly, and Allan and his colleagues figured it out.

The team decided to listen to shot noise—tiny fluctuations caused by the arrival of individual charges that form the stream of an electric current. Allan says it resembles rain drumming on the roof.  “If you imagine that you have raindrops and then all of a sudden you make the drops twice as heavy, there’ll be more noise,” Allan said. “With the electrons it’s similar. If you have two electrons coming always together, because they’re associated with pairing, then it will be more noise.”

The researchers did this measurement with a scanning tunneling microscope, which passed current between a needle-sharp tip and a cuprate sample, letting them listen to the noise at specific energy levels and different locations on the surface. In conventional superconductors, pairing produces a clean doubling of the noise. In cuprates, the signal is much weaker, because single electrons keep flowing alongside the pairs.

Making the instrument sensitive enough to discern the pairs took years. “What it took is very talented and persistent students who worked for, I don’t know, four years on this to get it working,” Allan said. “There was not a single trick. It was electronics craftsmanship.”

The team examined crystals of a cuprate known as BSCCO, cooled to a few degrees above absolute zero, and compared the noise to the energies at which electrons go missing. By adjusting the voltage between the tip of the needle and the sample, the team could choose the energy of the electrons they were listening to, a bit like turning a radio dial. If the pseudogap came purely from frozen patterns, the team reasoned, the extra noise of pairing should only kick in at energy levels associated with superconductivity. Instead, pairing showed up right at the pseudogap energy.

“The fact that there is more noise indicates that pairing is associated with this large gap,” Allan said. The pseudogap’s energy varies from spot to spot on the surface, but wherever the team listened, the extra noise always started rising at the local pseudogap energy. The same pattern held in samples with different chemical compositions.

Ruling out other sources of noise, such as atoms shifting on the surface, took a lot of effort. “Noise is super hard to interpret, and that was basically the thing that we had the most sleepless nights about,” Allan said. The decisive check involved placing the samples in magnetic fields of up to six tesla, which suppresses pairing while leaving the overall pattern of missing electrons largely intact. As the magnetic field grew, the noise dropped back toward the single-electron level, exactly as expected if pairs were behind it.

The strength of electron pairing can be expressed as the energy required to pull a pair apart. Lowering the temperature helps boost pairing because the warmer a material becomes, the more violently its particles jostle and the more pairs get broken. In conventional superconductors, two ingredients needed for superconductivity—pair formation and phase coherence—occur simultaneously at the critical temperature. (Phase coherence is where the electrons start moving together as a fluid.) In cuprates, Allan and Swart’s study points to a different sequence.

The pairing energy the team measured is well above the temperature at which the material superconducts. “So the picture that emerges is that you first have pre-formed pairs, and then only at lower temperatures, phase coherence sets in,” Swart said. That suggests the superconductivity bottleneck is not getting electrons to pair, but getting the pairs to move together.

“If you have pairing at such high energies, that means that you might be able to push Tc [superconducting temperature] higher,” Allan said. “This is something people hoped for in the very early days of cuprates, and now everybody’s afraid to even mention that. But to me it gives hope.”

An optimistic reading of the results is that cuprate electrons could theoretically pair at room temperature, and we only need to make them coherent to achieve a room-temperature superconductor. The team, though, thinks that’s a bit naive. “I think Ingmar and I both believe that it’s not going to be that simple, because you have all kinds of other effects that are coupled there,” Allan said.

The researchers also stress that their experiment does not fully answer the central question. It rules out the idea that the pseudogap comes from frozen patterns alone, but not that such patterns are intertwined with pairing. “Whatever the detailed explanation of the pseudogap is, pairing should be part of it,” Swart said.

Because the technique used in the study loses sensitivity as temperature rises, the team couldn’t yet listen above the superconducting temperature, where the pseudogap is usually defined. “Hopefully other groups will do that,” Allan said. “What limits Tc, especially if the energy of pairing is high? What happens at Tc? That’s for me the most interesting question.”

Swart wants to make noise maps of the surface with higher resolution and, eventually, an instrument that can detect coherence directly, which he hopes should give us separate markers for the onset of phase coherence and electron pairing. Allan, meanwhile, increasingly thinks the route to better superconductors runs through chemistry, aided by AI, rather than pure physics. “When it comes to trying to make the superconducting material better and increase Tc, I have more hope in direct trial and error,” he said.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#3116. Which of These 5 Cannabis Stocks Has the Strongest Balance Sheet?

Debt and cash flow reveal some significant differences.

Tilray Brands (TLRY +3.91%), Canopy Growth (CGC -3.58%), Cronos Group (CRON -2.96%), SNDL (SNDL +2.54%), and Aurora Cannabis (ACB +0.96%) have all had their share of financial challenges. But some are in much better shape than others. In an industry where marijuana companies have spent years burning through cash, issuing new shares, and struggling to turn a profit, a strong balance sheet matters.

So which of these five cannabis stocks is in the best financial position? Let's look at the numbers.

Tilray reported approximately $221.4 million in cash, restricted cash, and marketable securities at the end of first-quarter 2027. It also had $376.9 million in working capital.

The company has been cleaning up its balance sheet, too. In its first fiscal quarter, Tilray reduced its long-term convertible debt by about $79.5 million. Indeed, this was encouraging news, but the company still carries roughly $118 million in long-term debt. While quarterly revenue jumped 23% to $257.1 million, Tilray still reported a $40 million net loss and negative free cash flow of $27.4 million.

The company does have plenty of opportunities across cannabis, beverages, and pharmaceutical distribution. But until it can consistently generate positive cash flow, its balance sheet remains vulnerable.

Canopy Growth has spent years trying to get its finances under control. As of June 30, 2026, the company had $241.4 million in cash and cash equivalents, down from $261.6 million just three months earlier. It also carried approximately $172.3 million in debt. Granted, there has been some improvement. Fiscal first-quarter revenue increased 13% to $58.2 million, while its net loss narrowed to $10.5 million from $32.2 million a year earlier.

To be sure, Canopy isn't facing an immediate cash crisis. But with that much debt still on the books, it has less financial flexibility than some of its competitors.

As of Sept. 30, 2026, SNDL reported $440.1 million in unrestricted cash, marketable securities, and investments, including investments in other businesses. That's a significant financial cushion. Also worth noting: SNDL generated a record $14.2 million in free cash flow in the third quarter, up 19.5% from a year earlier. Adjusted operating income also turned positive at $3.7 million.Those are definitely encouraging numbers, but there are also some weaknesses.

Quarterly revenue declined 2.5%, and SNDL recorded a $37 million noncash goodwill impairment. Nevertheless, SNDL is debt-free, and its improving cash flow makes it one of the industry's financially stronger companies.

At the end of June 2026, the company reported $49.4 million in cash and $21.9 million in short-term investments. It also held $35 million in restricted cash while working capital stood at approximately $228.2 million.That's a reasonable cushion, particularly for a company that's been focusing more heavily on medical cannabis and international markets. But Aurora's latest quarterly results weren't particularly impressive.

Revenue declined to $48.2 million from $52.8 million a year earlier. Adjusted EBITDA remained positive at $2.5 million, but free cash flow was negative $4.1 million. Don't get me wrong. Aurora isn't in terrible financial shape, but its readily available cash reserves are considerably smaller than those of Cronos.

Cronos Group still has the strongest balance sheet of these five cannabis companies. As of Sept. 30, 2026, the company reported approximately $841 million in combined cash, restricted cash, short-term investments, and longer-term interest-bearing deposits, with no significant debt burden. That's up from approximately $827 million at the end of June, giving Cronos a lot of financial flexibility to expand its operations, pursue acquisitions, and weather challenges in the cannabis industry.

Its operating performance is also improving. According to its Nov. 5 earnings release, Q3 revenue jumped 75% year over year to $63.5 million, while adjusted EBITDA surged 201% to $17.1 million. Gross profit also increased 76% to $32.3 million, reflecting stronger cannabis sales in Canada and international markets, particularly Germany.

Of these five cannabis companies, Cronos Group has the strongest balance sheet. Its approximately $841 million in cash and interest-bearing investments, limited debt exposure, and improving operating performance put it ahead of the competition.

SNDL comes in a close second, thanks to its debt-free balance sheet and positive free cash flow. Tilray and Canopy still have work to do, and Aurora has a respectable but smaller liquidity cushion.

Now, does that mean Cronos is automatically the best cannabis stock to buy? Not necessarily. A strong balance sheet doesn't guarantee strong revenue growth, profitability, or attractive shareholder returns. But it does give a company more room to maneuver when business conditions deteriorate. Considering how much money cannabis companies have burned through over the years, that's an advantage I wouldn't underestimate.

Jeff Siegel has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Cronos Group. The Motley Fool recommends SNDL and Tilray Brands. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3114. 2027 Tax Brackets and Federal Income Tax Rates

Each year, the IRS adjusts tax brackets to keep pace with inflation. The 2027 tax brackets were announced on Nov. 5, 2026.

The IRS has released updated tax brackets for 2027, which apply to the taxes we file in 2028. You may notice that the seven tax rates generally stay the same year-to-year — so why do tax brackets get adjusted?

Well, due to inflation, our money might not stretch as far as it did the previous year. To combat that, the IRS adjusts tax brackets so taxpayers can keep a little bit more of their money in a lower tax bracket compared with the previous year.

For example, a single filer could make up to $50,400 in 2026 and pay a top tax rate of 12%. But in 2027, they could make up to $52,025 and still only pay a top tax rate of 12% thanks to these inflation adjustments.

Not sure how to read the tax bracket tables below?

Find your total taxable income in the "tax bracket" column. The corresponding tax rate is the highest rate you'll pay — it doesn’t apply to all your income; it applies only to the portion that falls within that bracket. The income in the lower brackets is taxed at those lower rates.

For example, let's say you’re a single filer and you expect to earn $150,000 this year. The top rate you’d pay on a portion of your income is 24%. The rest would be taxed at 10%, 12% and 22%.

#3113. 3 AI Chip Stocks to Buy in November After the October Sell-Off

AI stocks are trading at some attractive price tags right now.

October was a rough month for many stocks. Some of the biggest names in artificial intelligence (AI) sold off. Big names in the industry, like Micron (MU +5.12%), sold off around 20%. Furthermore, stalwarts like Nvidia (NVDA +2.77%) declined more than 10% while Broadcom (AVGO +0.74%) lost 5%.

However, I think November could be the month that these three turn it around, and it's very easy to see why: 2027 projections.

Unless you're investing with earplugs in, these companies have repeatedly said that demand for 2027 AI computing units will be great. This benefits all three stocks, and as investors start to realize that, I think there will be a large rally in this trio. As a result, I think investors should consider taking positions in these stocks now, as they look primed to soar.

Whether you're listening to an AI hyperscaler discuss 2027 plans or listening to one of the trio listed above, it's pretty clear that 2027 will bring about greater spending than 2026, which is almost hard to fathom.

Nvidia noted that the big five AI hyperscalers plan to spend around $1.3 trillion next year, up from nearly $800 billion this year. It's so confident in this that it told investors to expect a 70% growth rate next year. Nvidia has this confidence because orders must be placed well in advance to secure computing units during a supply-constrained period like the one the industry is in now.

Nvidia isn't the only computing unit provider seeing huge growth. Broadcom designs custom computing units tailored to specific AI workloads. These are becoming increasingly popular among AI hyperscalers as a way to maximize efficiency, and Broadcom expects to double its AI semiconductor revenue in 2027.

Those are strong growth projections from both of them, but having a strong market for computing units means strong demand for memory chips, which Micron makes.

The memory chip market has been in a tight spot in 2026, with demand far exceeding supply. This caused memory chip prices to skyrocket, benefiting companies like Micron. 2027 will be no different, as its management team informed investors that they believe 2027 and 2028 will be worse than 2026. That's almost hard to believe, but it's likely what will happen with great demand for AI computing.

All three of these stocks have positive catalysts coming up in 2027, but none of them are priced into their stocks.

Valuing these stocks based on next year's earnings estimates is a smart idea, as it indicates where the companies are headed. All three of these companies operate on non-calendar-year quarterly cycles, and Micron and Broadcom have already entered fiscal year 2027. Broadcom trades at a cheap 17 times fiscal year 2027's earnings, while Micron is super cheap at less than 5 times forward earnings.

Nvidia's next fiscal year doesn't start until the end of January, but it trades for just 13 times next year's earnings.

NVDA PE Ratio (Forward 1y) data by YCharts.

For comparison, the S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.31%) trades for about 19 times forward earnings when the next 12 months' estimates are used. So, it's pretty easy to realize that all three of these stocks are cheaper than the broader market yet are expected to have fantastic growth years in 2027. I think that the market will realize this in November and send the stocks rallying in preparation for a strong 2027. Buying the stocks before this happens will be key, and now is the perfect time to strike.

#3112. IRS Announces 2027 Income Tax Brackets, Updated Standard Deduction

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Here's a rundown of changes to some well-known tax provisions for the 2027 tax year.

Each year, the IRS updates common tax provisions, such as income tax brackets and the standard deduction, to ensure the tax code is keeping pace with the rising cost of living.

These tweaks — formally known as inflation adjustments — help prevent “bracket creep,” where taxpayers who received a cost-of-living raise can pay more in taxes even though their purchasing power remains unchanged.

Inflation adjustments for the 2027 tax year were set at 3.2% — larger than the previous two years as inflation heated up. This could mean lower tax bills for some people when they file in 2028.

In the U.S., there are seven marginal rates — 10%, 12%, 22%, 24%, 32%, 35% and 37% — which will remain unchanged for 2027.

Contrary to a popular myth, it’s uncommon for people to pay a single flat federal tax rate on their taxable income. Instead, portions of a person’s income can be taxed at different rates depending on which tax brackets they fall into. For example, some of a person’s income could be taxed at 10%, another chunk at 12%, and so on.

For 2027, the IRS is adjusting those tax brackets — the windows of income that dictate where a tax rate ends and begins — for each filing status. The adjustments mean that some people may be able to keep some of their income in lower tax brackets, and those who received a cost-of-living raise may avoid getting a portion of their income pushed into a higher bracket.

Married filers, for example, can make up to $104,050 to remain with a top tax rate of 12%, compared with $100,800 in 2026. This increase could allow some couples to shelter an additional $3,250 from a higher tax rate in 2027.

2026 vs. 2027 tax brackets: Single filers

2026 vs. 2027 tax brackets: Heads of household

2026 vs. 2027 tax brackets: Married filing separately

» MORE: See the full 2027 tax brackets for each filing status

The IRS lets most filers lower their taxable income by taking the standard deduction or itemizing their returns. The standard deduction, a flat amount based on filing status and age, is popular, as it requires less work to claim and is often more beneficial for those who might not have deductible expenses.

For 2027, the standard deduction will be $16,600 for single filers and those married filing separately, a $500 increase from 2026. Joint filers will be eligible to take $33,200 and heads of household can take $24,925, a $1,000 and $775 increase over 2026, respectively.

» Need help deciding whether to itemize or take the standard deduction? Learn about the differences

Also included in the IRS' 32-page inflation release are various updates to several tax provisions beyond the federal tax brackets. Plus, a brand-new tax credit will go into effect in 2027.

Starting in 2027, taxpayers who contribute to a health flexible spending account (FSA) can contribute up to $3,500 and, if their plan permits, carry over up to $700 into the next tax year.

For those with health savings accounts, the 2027 limit for contributions will rise to $4,500 for self-only coverage and $9,000 for family coverage.

The annual exclusion for gifts, which limits how much taxpayers can give an individual without filing a gift tax return on certain gifts, will be $20,000 per person in 2027, an increase of $1,000 compared with 2026.

The lifetime estate tax exclusion establishes a threshold for the taxation of estates upon a person's death. In 2027, estates valued at or below $15.48 million will not be subject to estate tax, up from $15 million in 2026.

The Education Freedom Tax Credit will launch on Jan. 1. According to the IRS, it aims to support private contributions for K-12 scholarships. Taxpayers who donate to a "scholarship granting organization" in one of the 30 participating states can receive a nonrefundable tax credit of up to $1,700 ($3,400 for couples filing jointly).

#3110. A Top Silicon Valley Investor Predicts a Bitcoin Crash to $57,000 Within the Next 12 Months. Here's Why He's Wrong.

Longtime Bitcoin investors have seen this story before: a crash, and then a spectacular rally.

Several high-profile investors have already given up on Bitcoin (BTC +0.09%). Earlier this year, for example, billionaire Mark Cuban said Bitcoin had "lost the plot," announcing that he was selling nearly all his Bitcoin.

Now comes Silicon Valley investor Jason Calacanis, co-host of the All-In Podcast, who is predicting a Bitcoin crash all the way to the $57,000 price level. And he didn't stop there. He also predicted that Bitcoin would never come close to reaching its current all-time high of $126,000.

Yes, Bitcoin is still down 38% from its October 2025 highs, but all signs are now pointing to a bottom. This summer, Coinbase Global CEO Brian Armstrong called a Bitcoin bottom at the $60,000 price level. And many top analysts now say Bitcoin is in an accumulation phase. In other words, it's not yet a full-fledged Bitcoin bull market rally, but it's getting close.

Typical Bitcoin bear markets last around 12 months, and it has now been more than 12 months since Bitcoin last soared into nosebleed territory. Bitcoin has been through similar bear market cycles in the past, and each time, it has bounced back stronger than before. So the current price pullback, while unpleasant, is not unexpected. I'm fully expecting Bitcoin to head higher next year.

That said, Calacanis makes several valid points. For example, he points out that Bitcoin ETF outflows, if they persist, will make it much harder for Bitcoin to regain its former highs. At the very least, they point to weakening institutional demand for Bitcoin.

And Calacanis also suggests that new opportunities in AI (aka super intelligence) are far more attractive than those available in crypto right now. AI is growing quickly and is full of dynamism. The hottest companies are now in artificial intelligence.

By way of contrast, the Bitcoin blockchain has become a "dead network." Bitcoin has become a store of value, and nothing else, and that makes it boring for many investors.

In Bitcoin investing circles, them's fightin' words. As soon as Calacanis made the "dead network" comment, he received immediate pushback from the crypto community. Some even offered him $1 million bets based on his claim that Bitcoin would never reach another all-time high.

Bitcoin may no longer have as much upside potential as it once did, but it's impossible to ignore Bitcoin's impressive historical track record over the past 18 years. Bitcoin tends to move in four-year cycles of boom and bust, and each time, it has bounced back better than before.

In 2022, for example, Bitcoin collapsed in value by 64%. But it bounced back with triple-digit returns in 2023 and 2024, before hitting a new all-time high of $126,000 in 2025.

History may not repeat, but it certainly rhymes. The Bitcoin bottom is already in, and 2027 could be the year of the long-awaited Bitcoin comeback.

Dominic Basulto has positions in Bitcoin. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Bitcoin. The Motley Fool recommends Coinbase Global. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3109. The United Explorer Card Is Great if You Fly Basic Economy

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Basic economy tickets come with plenty of restrictions, but holding the right credit card is my top trick for avoiding some of them.

As airfares remain high, that basic economy fare might look more tempting than ever. Saving $100 on a round-trip domestic flight could mean more money for hotels, dining or other experiences at your destination.

I’ve flown my fair share of basic economy flights, which come with restrictions like being unable to select a seat, earning no award miles and boarding last. You also can’t make changes or cancel your itinerary without paying a hefty fee, and — specific to United Airlines — you can’t bring a full-size carry-on on board.

But as a United frequent flyer, I have a secret to share with you: Holding the United℠ Explorer Card (annual fee: $0 intro for the first year, then $150) lets you avoid several of those basic economy restrictions.

Right now, new United℠ Explorer Card applicants can receive this welcome offer: Earn 70,000 bonus miles after you spend $3,000 on purchases in the first 3 months your account is open. Plus, earn 10,000 bonus miles when you add an authorized user in the first 3 months your account is open.

Most major airlines no longer let you earn points or miles on basic economy fares. United is an exception, though — assuming you hold the right credit card.

Eligible cardholders, including those who have the United℠ Explorer Card, earn at least 3 miles per $1 spent on United basic economy tickets, excluding taxes and select fees. That’s half of what a standard economy ticket earns for cardholders (6 miles per $1), but it’s better than zero. If you have elite status, you’ll earn more.

Those miles are in addition to the 3 miles per $1 you earn on all United purchases when using your United℠ Explorer Card.

With the card, your miles can become more valuable. That’s because cardholders get at least 10% off award tickets that are operated by United or United Express. If you have United status, that discount gets bumped up to at least 15% off.

With the credit card, you have access to priority boarding in Group 2. That means you’ll be able to board earlier compared to the rest of the basic economy passengers in Group 6, making it more likely you’ll find space for your carry-on bag.

Now, you might be thinking: Wait a minute… Basic economy doesn’t allow you to bring a carry-on bag. Let me explain how that works.

United enforces its full-size carry-on restriction based on your boarding group. Group 6 passengers can't bring a full-size carry-on.

Because the credit card gets you Group 2 boarding, you essentially skip that restriction.

Premier members — who board in Group 2 or earlier — are also exempt from the full-size carry-on restriction when flying basic economy.

If you don’t want to deal with the hassle of a carry-on bag, you can opt to check it instead.

As long as you pay for your ticket using your United℠ Explorer Card, you’ll be able to check your first bag — and your companion’s first bag — for free when traveling on United. (If you’re booking an award ticket, make sure to use your card to pay for the taxes and fees.)

Keep in mind that if the first flight on your itinerary is operated by another airline, that airline’s baggage rules would apply and you would have to pay for your bag.

Of course, holding the right United credit card only gets you so far.

With basic economy, you’ll still need to pay a fee to change or cancel your ticket, and you won’t be able to select a seat for free.

Ultimately, whether basic economy is a good deal depends on a few factors.

If your plans might change, pay up for standard economy. Otherwise, the penalty to change or cancel will end up costing you more than you saved.

Another thing to consider is whether you’re willing to sit in a middle seat. You might be fine with it for a few hours, but what about a long-haul flight to Asia? Everyone’s threshold is different, so you’ll need to decide whether the middle-seat gamble is worth it.

If your plans are firm and you don’t mind a potential middle seat, booking a basic economy ticket can be a solid way to save money. Just book with a United card like, the United℠ Explorer Card, to avoid the biggest downsides.

Flat-rate travel rewards: Capital One Venture Rewards Credit Card
