#3108. Biotech stocks are surging. How investors can play the new drug innovation boom

Moderna gave investors a scare as its stock plunged on Thursday. But it's still one of the hottest biotechs this year. And it's hardly alone – the sector is on a roll and looks poised for more gains.

The iShares Biotechnology ETF (IBB) is up 24% through October 23, building on a 28% gain in 2025. Two dozen of the ETF's 233 holdings have more than doubled year-to-date. Moderna leads the pack with a 587% surge — even after an 18% decline following a leak of clinical data for its cancer vaccine (which was actually positive).

Analysts say a new biotech cycle is underway, fueled by clinical trial results, M&A activity, and promising technologies such as genomics, mRNA — and AI.

"We're in the midst of a powerful innovation cycle," said John Hall, portfolio manager of the T. Rowe Price Biotechnology ETF. "What's super exciting is we're seeing newer modalities start to make it to market." Companies "understand the disease biology better," he adds, and that could ultimately lead to more novel drugs making it to market.

Investors are excited about technologies like mRNA and genomics, along with a steady stream of new drug approvals. The FDA approved about a dozen novel biologic drugs in 2025, out of 46 approvals in total. Roughly 66 novel drugs are in the pipeline for FDA approval, including potential blockbusters like Moderna and Merck's cancer drug for melanoma, and a next-generation obesity drug from Eli Lilly.

Analysts say AI could also lead to more approvals as companies deploy the software to speed up drug development.

"Biotech is a phenomenal application for artificial intelligence and machine learning," says Piper Sandler analyst Ted Tenthoff. "You can set up exciting predictive models that, frankly, can do things that we've never been able to do in the space in terms of novel drug discovery."

Granted, the industry is recovering from a fallow period. The sector's last strong performance was during the Covid era when ultra-low interest rates encouraged investment in early-stage drug developers, and mRNA vaccines fueled a revenue surge for companies like Moderna, Pfizer and Biontech.

The sector then went cold as many early-stage companies fizzled. Demand for Covid vaccines evaporated, and surging interest rates put a chill in IPOs and M&A.

The risks haven't evaporated – this is still a sector whose products take years to develop, resulting in far more failures than successes. But analysts say the outlook is healthier now. While that's reflected in strong performance and richer valuations, there's still good reason to invest.

Recent clinical trial results are stoking excitement that novel drugs in the works could tackle some of the toughest problems in medicine.

Moderna and Merck's melanoma drug, for instance, uses mRNA to trigger an immune response in patients, targeting tumors that have been surgically removed and genetically analyzed. It's not a preventive vaccine, but it uses a similar immunology mechanism and showed a 42% reduction in cancer recurrence or death (used in conjunction with Merck's cancer drug Keytruda).

While the Phase 3 results disappointed Wall Street, oncologists say the outcomes were still highly positive. And they see the technology as a potential game changer, ushering in an era of personalized treatments for cancers. Moderna and Merck have said they aim to submit the drug for FDA approval and get it launched in 2027.

Inflammation treatment is another promising area. AstraZeneca and Amgen recently released positive Phase 3 results for another use for their drug Tezspire, an injectable approved for nasal polyps and asthma. In clinical trials, it showed that it successfully relieved swallowing symptoms and healed microscopic and visual tissue damage in patients with severe throat inflammation.

Roche, meanwhile, notched a win after the FDA approved its drug Enspryng, a therapy to treat rare inflammatory and autoimmune diseases. It became the first and only at-home injection for adults with thyroid eye disease.

Obesity drugs are also advancing beyond the first generation of GLP-1s. Eli Lilly's retatrutide, a "triple agonist" targeting three different hormone receptors, cleared two more clinical trials over the summer. The drug produced 28%-30% weight loss at the highest doses in the study, Lilly said. The company plans to submit the drug for FDA approval in the first quarter of 2027.

Much of the innovation isn't just happening in drug-making – it's also in areas like gene sequencing, mRNA, and high-tech instruments.

Illumina has more than doubled this year. The DNA and gene sequencing company is seeing accelerating demand for its NovaSeq X high-end sequencing platform. Pharmaceutical companies including Bristol-Myers Squibb, Merck and Kura are working with Illumina to develop precision oncology solutions.

Another big winner is 10x Genomics, up more than 300%. The biotech firm offers its lab instruments to help scientists research cancer, immunology and neuroscience. The company's Atera platform is helping produce data sets to train AI models, a process that helps accelerate personalized therapies.

On the AI front, companies are using the technology for more efficient drug discovery, says Shreefāl Mehta, president of Guidant BioTherapeutics. Using AI, "you can now bring a drug to clinical trials with less capital. More investment is also going into companies that are demonstrating that," he said.

Global AI in drug discovery will grow from a $20 billion market in 2025 to more than $160 billion by 2035, according to forecasts by Towards Health, a research and consulting firm.

One other catalyst for the sector could be M&A. Dealmaking for biopharma hit $134 billion in the first half of 2026, up from $112 billion for all of 2025, notes Jeet Mukherjee, biotech analyst at BTIG. That's a positive signal, indicating more drugs in development are showing clinical promise.

It also helps that Big Pharma has a "patent cliff" ahead. Products representing $180 billion of revenue will lose exclusivity by 2030, according to research firm RTW Institute. Companies with looming expiries "are looking for novel products and innovative products to put into their pipeline," Mehta says.

GLP-1 blockbusters have also left some companies flush with cash for acquisitions. Eli Lilly alone has completed 10 acquisition deals in 2026 worth $25 billion.

ETFs can capture the investing theme, though they aren't all alike. The iShares Biotech ETF is one of the lower cost funds. It tracks a market-cap weighted index of biotech stocks, led by companies like Gilead Sciences, Amgen, Vertex Pharmaceuticals, and Moderna.

The State Street SPDR S&P Biotech ETF holds stocks in equal weight, offering more exposure to small- and mid-cap names. Its top holdings are Moderna, Natera, Twist Bioscience and Halozyme Therapeutics.

Piper Sandler calls Illumina and 10x Genomics the "primary infrastructure plays" in the life science arena, saying their technologies are "driving the next generation of precision medicine."

Piper analyst David Westenberg has a buy rating and $305 target on Illumina, implying 14% gains from recent prices around $267. He has a hold rating on 10X with an $80 target, above recent prices around $70.

Twist Bioscience is another name to consider. The company makes customized synthetic DNA and sells DNA sequencing tools. Twist has partnered with Eli Lilly's AI and machine-learning platform and linked up with Anthropic in August to start testing its AI model, Claude, for protein development.

Twist isn't expected to turn an EPS profit until 2029, according to consensus forecasts. But revenue is expected to go from $572 million in 2027 to $833 million in 2029. Analysts have an average target of $168 on the stock, implying 21% returns from recent prices around $138.

In the small-cap space, Piper Sandler's Tenthoff likes Monte Rosa Therapeutics, a pioneer in protein degradation that selectively destroys disease-causing proteins.

The firm has partnered with Novartis to develop a drug for inflammatory conditions and autoimmune diseases. The companies announced a Phase 2 clinical study in September for an autoimmune disease treatment, which has been "triggering milestones to Monte Rosa," Tenthoff said.

Tenthoff has a buy rating and $37 target on the stock, which recently traded around $10.70.

Also in the small-cap arena, BTIG's Mukherjee recommends Faeth Therapeutics.

The biotech is developing a novel two-drug regimen to treat breast cancer and other tumors. The regimen "has the potential to address safety and dosing limitations of another recently approved drug for breast cancer," he says. And it could become "a mainstay of the future breast cancer treatment paradigm and other cancers."

Faeth is a clinical-stage company that has yet to bring a drug to market. Yet the stock is up 93% year-to-date on positive clinical trial results, trading around $18. Mukherjee has a $55 target on it.

Kymera Therapeutics is worth considering too. The company is developing an oral version of the blockbuster drug Dupixent (made and co-developed by Regeneron Pharmaceuticals and Sanofi). Dupixent is an injectable, however, and oral agents "are frequently preferred by patients," Mukherjee says. "The drug has the potential to not only take share from patients taking Dupixent for numerous immunological diseases but also expanding the market to those not taking systemic biologic drugs," he tells CNBC.

Kymera has said it doesn't expect a patent fight over Dupixent because its drug is distinct, not a biosimilar or biologic copy.

T. Rowe's Hall also likes Kymera for its potential "to unlock disease targets that have been classically considered un-druggable." The stock is the second largest holding in T. Rowe's Biotech ETF, accounting for 4.2% of the portfolio.

#3107. Tesla Stock Hasn't Closed at a Record in Nearly 11 Months. Here's How Its Past Record Droughts Ended.

The EV maker has been here six times before. What did shareholders get once each wait ended?

Tesla (TSLA +4.23%) last closed at a record on Dec. 16, 2025, at $489.88 a share. That was 327 days ago, or nearly 11 months. With Tesla shares trading near $346 as I write this, the stock would have to gain about 41% just to tie it.

Long gaps between records are nothing new for the EV maker, though. Since its IPO in June 2010, Tesla stock has gone 300 days or more without a record close six other times, based on daily split-adjusted closing prices.

Every one of those droughts eventually ended. But what happened next was very different. Two of them gave way to rallies of over 600%. The other four were followed by a new drought within months, and twice within days.

And the difference between the two groups probably boils down to whether Tesla's profits were turning higher.

The first two droughts came early in the electric-vehicle maker's life as a public company. The stock spent 474 days without a record from Nov. 24, 2010, to March 12, 2012, and then exactly 300 days from April 3, 2012, to Jan. 28, 2013. The next two lasted longer, at 942 days from Sept. 4, 2014, to April 3, 2017, and 821 days from Sept. 18, 2017, to Dec. 18, 2019. Most recently, shares went 1,133 days from Nov. 4, 2021, to Dec. 11, 2024, and then 364 days from Dec. 17, 2024, to last December's record.

That makes the current 327-day wait the second shortest of the seven. The middle of the six past droughts is around 650 days, or almost twice as long as this one has lasted.

Of course, the drops along the way were steep. In the 2021-to-2024 drought, the stock's lowest close was around 74% below its prior record.

And in every one of the six, shares closed at least 31% under the old high at some point. This time, the low so far was $298.32 on July 29, around 39% below the December record.

Two of them ended in huge runs. Once the 300-day gap closed on Jan. 28, 2013, Tesla stock kept setting records until Sept. 4, 2014, rising around 652% in that time.

And once the drought ended on Dec. 18, 2019, shares climbed about 1,464% to the November 2021 record that started the longest wait of all.

The other four runs were short. The March 2012 record led to just 22 days of new highs before the next drought began. The April 2017 record brought around five and a half months of records and a 29% climb.

The December 2024 run of records lasted six days. Last December's record ended one drought and began the current one the same day.

In other words, a new record on its own hasn't said much about what happens next. For shareholders, I'd say this matters more than the length of any drought.

What the two big runs share is a turn in profits.

In May 2013, Tesla said it had become profitable in the first quarter "for the first time in our ten year history," earning $11 million. The stock gained about 24% the day after the letter came out.

The 2019 run followed a similar swing. The company made $143 million in the third quarter of 2019. That was a sharp turn from losses of $702 million and $408 million earlier that year. Even more, its net income hit $721 million in 2020 -- after losing money in each of the four years before that.

But the four short runs had nothing like that behind them. The company lost $396 million in 2012 and almost $2 billion in 2017.

And its operating income (profit from the business before interest and taxes) was falling in both 2024 and 2025, down 20% and then another 38%.

That trend hasn't turned yet. In the first nine months of 2026, Tesla's operating income totaled about $2.1 billion, down about 30% from the same period in 2025.

What does this history suggest for anyone owning the stock now?

I don't think 11 months without a new high is a reason to sell on its own. After all, waits this long have been normal for the growth stock, and every one of them has ended.

But the next record might matter less than what's behind it. One that comes with operating income climbing again may look a lot more like 2013 or 2019. Without that, a new high might fade as fast as the last two did.

Daniel Sparks has clients with positions in Tesla. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Tesla. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3106. We Write About Saving Money; Here’s How We Trick Our Brains Into Doing It

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Even personal finance writers and editors need psychological hacks to save money. Here are the tips we use, and why they work.

While staring at the Container Store cart on my phone at 11 p.m., I did something for the first time that I’d read about a hundred times. Instead of spending $200 on more organizational boxes that I categorically did not need, I transferred that amount to my savings account.

Normally, my willpower is no match for the very real psychological phenomenon called present bias: Our brains light up more when we’re rewarding our current selves than our future selves.

Research has also shown that you imagine your future self almost like a stranger — it’s tougher to care about that stranger than about who you are right now. But that night, I saw near-future-me using that $200 on next summer’s vacation. I wanted the vacation more than the boxes. So I abandoned my cart.

I had managed to use a psychological savings trick: Find a goal that helps you envision your future self. With this epiphany in mind, I asked some of my fellow Nerds to share the savings habits they’ve put to work in their own lives. Perhaps reading these can give late-night you some saving inspiration.

Kimberly Palmer, senior writer and spokesperson: I let nonessential items sit in my online shopping cart for at least 24 hours before buying them. It definitely makes me realize I don't need a lot of them! I also informed my children of this rule, and it has helped cut down on their requests — or at least they know they have to be patient.

Karen Gaudette Brewer, head of content: Fellow let-it-sit-in-the-cart person here. I wait for notifications that something's at least 30% off before I make any moves. Ideally, 40% to 50% off.

Julie Myhre-Nunes, managing editor: When I browse online, I add an item to my shopping cart and wait 24 to 48 hours. After that, I either decide it’s a waste of money/an impulse purchase and delete it from my cart (or forget about the cart altogether), or I decide I want it and make the purchase — it’s the former 99% of the time.

Why it works: “Waiting 1 to 2 days allows me to take the emotion out of purchasing. I no longer succumb to marketing or sales, and instead make spending decisions based on what my family and I actually need or can use.” — Julie again, eloquently making the point for me.

Caitlin Constantine, managing editor: My one real shopping weakness is books, and I’ve found I can get a very close approximation of that feeling without spending an outrageous amount of money by going to the library, wandering around the stacks to see what books might look interesting, and checking out a bunch of them. Even if I don’t end up reading them, I’m still helping the library with circulation numbers, so I see it as a win-win.

Spencer Tierney, senior writer: Plus one to Caitlin Constantine’s “my one real shopping weakness is books.” If I want an older or smaller book, I'll try to find it at one of four libraries I belong to on Libby, the library e-book reader app. (The Libby waitlist for new books can take months, so I have to account for that.)

Why it works: There’s no secret here: It’s nice to get free stuff. You might be surprised what the library has to offer, like movies, magazine subscriptions, games, classes and theater tickets.

Pamela de la Fuente, managing editor: We always tell people to automate savings, and I do in my high-yield savings account, but Dasha Kennedy, the woman behind “The Broke Black Girl”, told Elizabeth Ayoola on the Smart Money podcast that you should invest, even a little bit every week or so, for future you. So I just set up a small recurring deposit to my robo-advisor.

Why it works: You only have to make this decision once for your future self, not over and over again. And if you do it early enough, you could avoid being part of the 41% of Americans who wish they had started investing earlier, according to a 2026 NerdWallet survey.

Athena Cocoves, managing editor: To cut down on food delivery, I started cooking when I feel like it instead of when I’m supposed to. I exercise free will and make soup at 10 p.m. It sounds more obvious than it feels, and it's made a big difference in my budget. And it'd make an even bigger difference if I wasn't also rewarding myself with fancy spices and vinegars, but a girl's gotta splurge somehow.

Ryan Brady, lead writer: My wife takes advantage of a T-Mobile freebie that gives us a discount for DoorDash deliveries. She uses it to buy groceries from Aldi instead of takeout. Aldi is far from us. Publix is right across the street, but more expensive. So this saves us money on the actual items and also helps us not impulse buy (as we often do when we're in-store shopping).

Why it works: You don’t need to call on willpower if you can avoid the situation that tempts you to spend money.

» Read more: Strategies to save on groceries that actually work

Kenley Young, managing editor: I use credit cards for most purchases to get those sweet, sweet rewards, but there's a lot in this article that I've also taken to heart.

I've started using library resources more, and it's saving me money on things like monthly subscriptions.

I've negotiated down several monthly bills, too: refinanced my car loan, got cheaper homeowners insurance, opted for a little less auto coverage in exchange for a lower premium, and haggled online with everyone from Xfinity and SiriusXM to my local newspaper until they all gave me a discount.

Plus, I've opted for some home improvement projects with the expectation that my utility bills would be lower. A plumber finally fixed our toilet, which has led to lower water bills, and I even installed solar panels.

Lastly, my analog skill (playing live music) has become a steady side hustle. I play eight to 10 gigs a month, where (on top of my baseline rate) I also get tips, free food and beer.

Why it works: Because you’re Kenley and you put in a lot of effort.

But you don’t need to be Kenley to find a savings habit that works for your personality, your financial situation and even your musical talents. You just need to experiment. Pick the strategy that speaks to you most, and test it out. Which one are you going to try first?

Headwinds in the smartphone industry are negatively impacting Qualcomm.

#3104. 3 Millionaire-Maker Quantum Computing Stocks

The quantum computing market is full of exciting investments, but not all of them will pan out.

Quantum computing is an emerging industry that could have several huge winners in it. There are countless early-stage quantum computing stocks that I think could provide massive returns, as this technology is set to provide massive economic value once viability is reached. While that may seem like a far-fetched technology, the reality is many in the industry believe 2029 could be the year when viable quantum computing shows up.

That's right around the corner, so what stocks have huge potential? Let's take a look.

IonQ (IONQ +23.42%) is one of the first stocks investors think of when they hear about quantum computing. It was the first quantum computing stock to go public, and it has performed exceptionally well as a company, even if the stock is currently well off its all-time highs.

IonQ currently has one of the most accurate quantum computing technologies, with a 99.99% two-qubit gate fidelity score. IonQ has achieved this thanks to its trapped-ion approach to quantum computing, which yields excellent accuracy but at the cost of processing speed.

Next year, IonQ has plans to release its 256-qubit system, which could drive huge growth for the company as clients adopt early-stage quantum computing technology. That could be the event that is needed to turn IonQ's stock around and could kick-start huge returns.

Quantinuum (QNT +25.99%) is one of the newer quantum computing stocks on the public market. However, it's quickly emerging as a top option. Quantinuum just published results of 99.9995% logical fidelity, which isn't the same measure as IonQ's, but is still a very impressive result.

Quantinuum also uses trapped-ion technology like IonQ, which gives it an advantage in the accuracy measure of quantum computing. It believes it can achieve fault-tolerant quantum computing by 2029, and if it could do that, it would achieve impressive shareholder returns along the way.

Rigetti Computing (RGTI +19.29%) is taking a different approach than these two. It's focusing on superconducting computing, which hasn't yielded the greatest accuracy results. However, superconducting computing can operate at far greater speeds than trapped-ion computing, which will be a major advantage if Rigetti can get its accuracy where it needs to be.

Rigetti's two-qubit fidelity for its largest 108-qubit system is 99.1% (comparable to IonQ's figure) or 99.9% for a single qubit (comparable to Quantinuum's figure). As Rigetti's qubit size decreases, its accuracy goes up, but those systems are so small that doing anything useful on them isn't really viable.

Rigetti's goal is to produce a 1,000-qubit system with 99.9% two-qubit gate fidelity over the next three years, but that will be significantly smaller and less accurate than anything IonQ or Quantinuum is producing. However, because of Rigetti's speed advantage, it may be a longer-term quantum computing play that could make a comeback over the next few years.

We'll see how it pans out, but it's still a viable investment option.

One thing investors need to keep in mind with this trio is that there is no guarantee any of them will reach profitability. These are very high-risk stocks that could provide a high reward, but there's no guarantee either. There are countless impressive competitors in this space, many of whom have massive cash flows to fund quantum computing operations. These three are all upstarts and must continue to get funding from outside sources until they have a commercially viable product and business in place.

If they can make it to that stage, then they will be huge winners and a successful investment. But the reality is there will be a lot of failure in this industry too. Investors need to choose the best of the best, and these three represent that.

Keithen Drury has positions in IonQ. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends IonQ. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3103. Wall Street's Biggest Bubble Looks Ready to Burst, and I Don't Mean AI or Quantum Computing

A $1.5 trillion powder keg can upend the stock market’s four-year rally.

Last month, Wall Street and investors celebrated the fourth anniversary of the current bull market. Both the benchmark S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.31%) and growth-fueled Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +1.09%) have more than doubled from their 2022 bear market lows, while the iconic Dow Jones Industrial Average (^DJI -0.46%) has rallied as much as 90%.

A confluence of factors has propelled Wall Street's major stock indexes to new heights, no two of which are larger than the evolution of artificial intelligence (AI) and the advent and early-stage proliferation of quantum computing. Both are trillion-dollar addressable opportunities that investors simply don't want to miss.

But following the stock market's parabolic climb, concerns are mounting about a possible AI and/or quantum computing bubble-bursting event. While history hasn't exactly been kind to next-big-thing technologies -- every game-changing technology has endured an early-innings bubble-bursting event since the mid-1990s -- there's an even more prominent bubble that looks ready to burst, based on what history tells us.

While history shows that the AI and quantum computing bubbles should eventually burst, the $1.5 trillion outstanding margin debt bubble may be the spark that upends the stock market's four-year rally.

"Margin" is money an investor borrows from their broker, with interest, to wager against (short-sell) or purchase securities. When margin is used to buy stocks or exchange-traded funds (ETFs), it represents a form of leverage.

Using margin can be risky. If an investor's wager moves in the desired direction, they can quickly amplify their gains. But if a stock or ETF moves in the opposite direction, losses can be quickly magnified. This dynamic makes outstanding margin debt a somewhat crude, yet intriguing measure of investors' willingness to take risks.

Total Margin Debt hits $1.5 Trillion, a new all-time high 🤯 👀 https://t.co/1IXqZGgrqs

According to FINRA, outstanding margin debt rocketed to an all-time high of $1.502 trillion in June. Although margin debt is expected to steadily increase over time in lockstep with the overall value of the stock market, bad things often happen when the amount investors borrow from their brokers rises dramatically over a short time frame.

Over roughly three decades, there have been four instances in which outstanding margin debt has climbed by at least 65% over a reasonably short timeline (12 to 19 months), including the present. The previous three occurrences all coincided with substantial bear markets:

History makes it crystal clear that outsize risk-taking is the ultimate red flag on Wall Street. While margin debt can't tell us when the music will stop or what catalysts could cause the stock market to sink, it clearly shows the unsustainability of excess risk-taking, which is doubly problematic in a historically expensive stock market.

From a historical standpoint, the short-term outlook for Wall Street's AI- and quantum computing-driven rally isn't pretty. If history rhymes once more, investors can expect a sharp bear market downturn in the presumed not-too-distant future.

While deep red arrows are known to tug on investors' heartstrings, significant pullbacks in Wall Street's major stock indexes can also represent generational buying opportunities for optimistic, long-term investors.

Despite most investors disliking stock market corrections and bear markets, these events are a natural and healthy part of the investing cycle. Given that downturns are commonly driven by emotions, there's not much that can be done from a monetary or fiscal policy perspective to prevent double-digit declines from occurring.

The silver lining for investors is that bull and bear market cycles aren't linear.

A May 2026 post on X (formerly Twitter) from Bespoke Investment Group examined every S&P 500 bull and bear market since the start of the Great Depression in September 1929. What researchers found was a mile-wide gap between bull and bear markets.

The current bull market that began on 10/12/22 is now the 9th longest in S&P 500 history, surpassing the 1,324-day bull that ended on 2/9/1966: https://t.co/4mGsS2t2ft

On one hand, the typical S&P 500 bear market found its trough after an average of 286 calendar days (roughly 9.5 months). By comparison, the average S&P 500 bull market persisted for 1,023 calendar days, or approximately 3.6 times as long as the average bear market.

More than a century of backward-looking S&P 500 total return data from Crestmont Research provides even more for patient investors to cheer about.

Analysts at Crestmont calculated the rolling 20-year total returns, including dividends, of the S&P 500 since 1900. This meant tracking its components in other major indexes prior to the S&P's official inception in 1923.

Crestmont's analysis yielded 107 rolling 20-year periods (1900-1919, 1901-1920, and so on, through 2006-2025), all of which produced a positive average annual total return. I repeat, 107 of 107 rolling 20-year periods generated a profit, including dividends.

Even if someone hypothetically invested at a stock market peak, or during a recession, depression, war, pandemic, etc., they would have always made money if they held an S&P 500-tracking index for 20 years.

Patience is a virtue and a path to potential riches on Wall Street. If the $1.5 trillion margin debt bubble bursts, consider it an opportunity to pounce.

#3101. Here's Why I'm Still Investing in the S&P 500, Even if a Bear Market Is Coming

Buying before a market decline feels awful. But waiting indefinitely carries its own risks.

The thought is inescapable: If I invest today, what if the market drops tomorrow? It's a question that can give everyone pause. After all, no one wants to see their newly invested money lose its value.

Yet the alternative creates an even more dangerous trap: Once you start waiting, what could possibly convince you it is ever safe to buy? That's why, when dealing with money you won't need for many years, it's better to build a plan based on uncertainty than to keep waiting for the perfect entry point.

War. Economic turmoil. Apocalyptic forecasts. The reality is that these things come and go. Sometimes the market falls as a result. Sometimes it doesn't. In reality, an informed person can always find what may seem like a reasonable excuse to postpone investing.

Worse, this anxiety often shows up no matter which way the market moves: Rising prices can feel expensive; falling prices feel like a warning sign. Nevertheless, studies have made it clear: Those who put money to work in the stock market have consistently outperformed those who have tried to time the market or remained in cash.

One way to clear the psychological hurdle of investing in a falling stock market is to recognize that lower prices can offer benefits. First, they mean you can buy more shares.

For example, imagine an investor who puts $200 to work in the stock market each month, buying an index exchange-traded fund (ETF) valued at $100 a share. That results in two shares per month. However, if the index ETF falls to $80 a share, the same $200 investment buys 2.5 shares per month.

Granted, the earlier-purchased shares have lost value, and buying more doesn't erase that loss. However, for those buying with the intention of holding for decades, the lower price can help them accumulate many more shares.

In the long run, that will result in more assets, which will in turn generate added returns. In other words, it can help power the compounding mechanism that drives buy-and-hold investing.

This is the essence of dollar-cost averaging. By regularly setting aside money for investment, a person can remove the emotional guesswork of investing and, if the market declines, lower the average price paid.

Another reason some people fear investing into a downturn is that they are putting the wrong money to work in the wrong way. That is, investment money should be reserved for long-term goals that won't be interrupted by short-term challenges. Emergencies, bills, and near-term purchases should come from a separate pool of money.

That way, investment money can fluctuate without affecting a person's ability to make ends meet. Moreover, by having a separate cash reserve, a person won't be tempted to sell investments during a downturn to raise cash.

Lastly, it's important to establish the right investing routine. If $200 per month is too much, lower the amount; don't give up on the routine. If finding the right stocks seems overwhelming, start with an index fund that tracks the S&P 500 -- many are incredibly cheap, and buying shares has never been easier. Also, an S&P 500 index fund is a great way for investors to stay diversified, because the index itself spreads money across many large American stocks.

Is a bear market coming? Perhaps. Does that make me nervous about investing? No. That's not to say that a bear market drop wouldn't hurt. It's just that long-term investing requires the fortitude to ride out dips, corrections, and even bear markets.

The best investors are often the ones who can see downturns for what they are: opportunities for long-term investors to accumulate more shares at a lower price.

Jake Lerch has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3098. Mortgage Rates Today, Friday, November 6: A Little Lower

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates dropped today, but inflation data could shake things up next week.

With the midterm elections behind us, mortgage rates are continuing their gradual slide this month. One factor is a sell-off in tech stocks, which can send investors toward the relative safety of bonds and help push mortgage rates lower.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage ticked down to 6.96% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is five basis points lower than yesterday and 11 basis points lower than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Meanwhile, a stronger-than-expected jobs report could slow that slide by reinforcing expectations for another Federal Reserve rate hike in December.

And a reminder: While the economy never sleeps, markets are closed on the weekends. The rates you see Friday are unlikely to change much (if at all) until Monday.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

As for what’s next: Tomorrow’s jobs report, showing October data, could potentially nudge mortgage rates in either direction. To understand the link between jobs data and mortgage rates, keep reading below the video.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

The recent slide in mortgage rates might reverse course if next week’s inflation data comes in hot.

This week, the Nerds are watching the October Consumer Price Index, which comes out on Tuesday, Nov. 10. CPI is one of two key measures of inflation, which the Federal Reserve aims to keep in check relative to the strength of the labor market. (The Fed looks more closely at the other data set, the Personal Consumption Expenditures Index, which comes out Nov. 25.)

Right now, the labor market is looking pretty darn healthy: The October jobs report, released today from the Bureau of Labor Statistics, showed U.S. employers added 165,000 jobs in October, far more than economists expected, and unemployment edged down to 4.1%.

Raising the federal funds rate is the Fed’s main tool for bringing inflation down, but rate hikes are only on the table when the labor market is strong enough to handle it. Right now, it’s looking fairly likely that the Fed will approve a rate hike when the committee meets Dec. 8-9. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates directly, but an anticipated rate hike can put upward pressure on mortgage rates, which respond to investors’ expectations for the economy and Fed policy.

Still, a jump in mortgage rates isn’t a sure thing. October’s inflation data will capture the effect of the Fed’s last rate hike, which came in the middle of September. If that worked as expected and inflation shows signs of slowing, mortgage rates will be less affected.

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.46% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

#3093. Apollo to buy nearly 10% stake in Abu Dhabi-backed alternative asset manager Mubadala Capital

Investing giant Apollo Global Management is finalizing a deal to buy nearly a 10% stake in Mubadala Capital, the alternative asset manager subsidiary of Abu Dhabi's sovereign wealth fund, Mubadala Investment Co., according to people familiar with the matter who spoke on condition of anonymity.

The deal is expected to be announced later Monday morning, they said. Financial terms were not immediately known.

The transaction is significant on multiple fronts. It further advances Apollo's ambitions in Abu Dhabi often called the "capital of capital." The firm has spent the past 15 years building key strategic relationships there, with both the country's investment arm and its core leadership.

Apollo already has a multibillion-dollar partnership with Mubadala Investment Co., first established in 2022 and extended two years ago. At the time of that extension, Apollo said the agreement would strengthen the firm's Capital Solutions platform by enabling the firms to jointly source and provide customized debt and equity financing to companies worldwide.

In 2023, the institutions formed a $2.5 billion joint venture designed specifically to co-invest in global private credit opportunities over a five-year horizon.

For Mubadala Capital specifically — the region's only large-scale asset manager, advising and administering more than $755 billion in assets — the deal further advances its growth strategy under the direction of CEO Hani Barhoush, and its relevance on the global investing stage. It also marks an inflection point for the company's core alternatives business, which has grown to more than $60 billion.

John Zito, co-president of Apollo's Asset Management arm, and Barhoush have worked closely together over the past several years and, according to sources, their close-knit relationship and the success of prior partnerships led to talks of deepening ties. So did the belief that scale in the asset management business matters more than ever.

"Capital as a moat is a big thing," said one person familiar with the deal.

Importantly, the deal also continues a notable shift in the relationship between American investment firms and the Middle East. While the region, and Abu Dhabi specifically, has long been a sought-after source of capital, this deal further reverses that dynamic, with Apollo making a significant direct investment in the country and its investment vision.

Mubadala Capital's international offices include London, New York, Rio de Janeiro and San Francisco.

Apollo's investment in Mubadala Capital is similar to deals the firm has done with other asset management platforms, including Diameter Capital, Thoreau, Motive Partners and others.

Spokespersons for both firms declined to comment.

#3084. Want to Become a Stock Market Millionaire? $250 a Month in This ETF Could Get You There.

This could be the anchor of a great stock portfolio.

There are many ways to become a millionaire through the stock market, and some are riskier than others. But if you have $10,000 to start and you can invest $250 monthly, you can get there in 30 years with minimized risk.

One of the lowest-risk ways to achieve millionaire status is by investing in an index fund that tracks the S&P 500. However, you'd need to invest a lot of money or wait a very long time.

Instead, or in addition, you could invest in an exchange-traded fund (ETF) that tracks a growth index, like the Vanguard S&P 500 Growth ETF (VOOG +0.91%). It's similar to the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO +0.30%), but it focuses on the growth companies in the index. Instead of 500 holdings, it has 152. That's a riskier setup, but it's still well diversified.

Over time, that means it may be more susceptible to declines when the market falls, but it can gain much more when the market does well. And over many years, since bull markets tend to be much longer than bear markets, the growth ETF has outperformed.

Since inception in September 2010, VOOG has returned an annualized gain of 16.4%, edging out VOO's 14.8%, and it's actually the highest annualized gain since the inception of any Vanguard fund outside of two that were created last year.

Over a longer period, the S&P 500's annualized gain is around 10.9%, so let's assume a 12% annualized gain for the growth ETF over the next 30 years. If you start with $10,000 and add $250 per month, which amounts to a $3,000 annual investment, you'll have more than $1 million in 30 years.

Jennifer Saibil has positions in Vanguard Admiral Funds-Vanguard S&P 500 Growth ETF and Vanguard S&P 500 ETF. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Vanguard S&P 500 ETF. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3075. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#3074. Where Will Lemonade Stock Be in 5 Years?

Growth is accelerating, but the stock hasn't followed.

Lemonade (LMND -4.65%) stock has performed poorly this year despite the company growing fast. It's down 36% this year, but it just announced its twlevth consecutive quarter with accelerating top-line growth.

There's only so long the market can remain pessimistic about this stock. At some point, smart investors will recognize the opportunity, and the price will start to climb again. Here's where it could be in five years from now.

Lemonade is an insurance technology company that has been using artificial intelligence (AI) and machine learning throughout its operations since its inception more than a decade ago. It created its business with a digital infrastructure that underpins everything it does, and management claims that the interconnected parts that communicate give it an edge over incumbents.

Today, all of the insurance companies are moving toward digital and AI, but they also have legacy systems in place are very difficult to simply replace or move into new technology.

The company is attracting loads of new customers who are looking for a better experience, such as digital onboarding and claims without having to speak to an agent, and there has been spectacular growth. In the third quarter, in-force premium (IFP), the average total amount of premiums, increased 34% year over year. Premium per customer increased 9%, and total customers increased 23% to more than 3.5 million.

Management noted that as AI agents start taking over tasks, having one find an insurance policy works in its favor. Customers may tend to consider brand names important, while AI agents will look for the best price and coverage. CEO Daniel Schrieber called it a "potentially transformative shift in insurance distribution."

The company is also still rolling out, providing a tremendous growth runway for the next few years. Its renters product is now live in every state, and it has launched 25 new product-geography combinations so far in 2026. Its Car product is now live in 14 states, reaching half of the U.S. population.

From a coverage perspective, as it reaches new regions, its growth prospects scale. Each region represents multiple new opportunities from different kinds of policies and bundles. That means even if it doesn't reach the second half of the population over the next five years, its top line could still double, or more. If IFP growth continues to accelerate and grow at a compound annual growth rate of 35%, for example, IFP would grow from $1.5 billion to $6.7 billion.

Lemonade still isn't profitable, but it's on track to reach positive net income soon.

For several years, its loss ratio was too high, keeping investors away. The loss ratio measures how much of a policy is paid out as claims, and if it's too high, the insurance company won't make money. It's now competitively low, which means the company's algorithms are working and its underwriting is effective. As of the third quarter, it has a low 12-month trailing loss ratio of 60%.

Adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) loss improved from $26 million last year to $20 million this year, and management maintains that it will be positive in the fourth quarter, with a full year of positive adjusted EBITDA in 2027.

At one point a few years ago, management said that it expected to have positive net income in 2027, but it has not recently renewed that guidance. However, it's likely to happen within the next few years.

In five years, Lemonade is likely to be operating a thriving, profitable business that could capture market share from the insurance powerhouses. For example, Lemonade's trailing 12-month revenue is less than a tenth of Progressive's (PGR -2.32%), but it's growing much faster. The stock should follow.

Jennifer Saibil has positions in Lemonade. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Lemonade. The Motley Fool recommends Progressive. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3070. 3 Reasons Why Ultra High Yield Realty Income Is a Buy After Its Latest Earnings Report

Realty Income's third-quarter 2026 earnings showed that rising rates aren't the end of the world for this giant net lease REIT.

Shares of Realty Income (O -0.77%) have been under pressure since the pandemic. Even after a recent uptick, they are down roughly 25% from their 2020 high. But the real estate investment trust (REIT) hasn't really changed much over that span. If anything, the company has become more attractive, with its third-quarter 2026 earnings underscoring that.

Indeed, despite rising interest rates, it was another fairly boring quarter, with a modest increase to the company's guidance and yet another dividend increase. Here's why dividend lovers should still view Realty Income as a foundational investment.

"Boring is good" is in quotes because those are the words of Realty Income's CFO, Jonathan Pong. They came up during a conversation about the potential impact of rising interest rates on the giant net-lease real estate investment trust (REIT). That comment basically summarized the entire ethos of Realty Income, which trademarked the nickname "The Monthly Dividend Company." You have to be reliable, to the point of being boring, to back up a nickname like that.

The third quarter of 2026 was another quarter in which Realty Income quietly went about its business, even as real estate markets faced headwinds. It's true that rising rates will increase the cost of capital, but that didn't stop Realty Income from finding a way to make $2.6 billion in profitable investments (with a 170-basis-point spread).

There are several reasons for this success. First, Realty Income is massive, with a globally diversified portfolio of over 15,500 properties. It sees almost every significant deal. Second, it has an investment-grade-rated balance sheet, which, along with its size, affords it advantageous access to capital markets. And, third, the REIT has also been expanding its sources of funding, from strategic property sales (capital recycling) to the creation of an institutional asset management business. There are more points that could be made here, but the big story is that Realty Income wasn't waiting for rates to rise before addressing the issue; it was already prepared. Which is exactly what you'd expect from a company that's bold enough to call itself "The Monthly Dividend Company."

Realty Income had a solid quarter, which is good news. But it also raised its full-year guidance, which basically tells the market that the REIT is operating just as well as it always has. The raise was modest, taking the adjusted funds from operations (FFO) guidance range from $4.44 to $4.45 per share up to, wait for it, $4.45 to $4.46. That's not a shocking change, but remember: "boring is good."

Also worth noting, the company maintained its occupancy outlook at 98.5%, which is very strong. Same-store rent growth remained at 1.1%-1.3%. And management reaffirmed the goal of hitting $10 billion in acquisitions, noting it was already at $7.9 billion, roughly 80% of the way there. The guidance raise was good news; the fact that Realty Income's business is progressing in line with expectations is probably even better news.

There's one more easy-to-overlook event from the quarter that dividend investors will find important: Realty Income increased its dividend. The hike was modest, with the dividend rising just 0.7% year over year. However, the dividend has been increased annually for 32 years and is generally hiked a little bit every quarter. The adjusted FFO payout ratio is a very reasonable 73%.

It probably shouldn't be a shock to anyone that "The Monthly Dividend Company" has a well-supported and growing dividend. But it is one more piece of the dividend story that this quarter's earnings highlight, and it shouldn't be ignored. Slow and steady is what Realty Income is going for. Sure, that's kind of boring, but as Jonathan Pong pointed out, boring is good.

Nothing that happened in the third quarter of 2026 changes Realty Income's long-term story. That's the big takeaway: this giant REIT was built from the ground up to survive difficult periods. With a 5.6% yield, it can provide a solid backbone to a diversified income portfolio, allowing you to add lower-yielding but higher-growth stocks to the mix. Realty Income is worth buying after its third-quarter earnings because its earnings proved, again, that this ultra-high-yield REIT isn't likely to surprise you (or keep you up at night worrying).

Reuben Gregg Brewer has positions in Realty Income. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Realty Income. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3061. Should You Buy VOO Before the Market Rebounds?

The sharp post-election pullback in the S&P 500 has investors reeling. It might be a good buying opportunity.

The stock market just gave investors something they haven't really seen since the start of the Iran war: a chance to buy at a discount.

The Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO +0.30%) has fallen roughly 6% from its October high through the close on Nov. 5 following a sharp post-election sell-off. For investors who thought stocks were getting too expensive, this could be a real opportunity.

But then there's the issue of whether investors are ready to take advantage of it. Are they going to view it as a chance to buy on sale given their long-term focus? Or are they going to panic and look at selling stocks instead of buying?

If you're a long-term investor, this could be a buying opportunity you haven't seen since April.

The midterm elections gave Democrats control of both chambers of Congress, raising some questions about the future of the artificial intelligence (AI) boom.

Will there be efforts to slow the level of innovation? Will there be potential restrictions on data center construction? Will it be more challenging to spend billions of dollars to expand AI infrastructure?

That's a legitimate short-term concern for Vanguard S&P 500 ETF investors. Mega-cap tech stocks have unquestionably driven the market's gains over the past few years. By some measures, their valuations leave little room for disappointment. The election may have simply been the catalyst to let investors realize those fears.

Corporate earnings growth hasn't yet given investors much reason to get out of stocks. According to FactSet, S&P 500 companies were on pace to deliver roughly 30% year-over-year earnings growth in Q3. That growth rate is important because it's currently helping offset several other potentially negative factors.

Inflation remains elevated. Oil prices are still above $90 per barrel. And long-term Treasury yields are putting pressure on balance sheets and equity valuations. Without strong earnings growth, the market would have a much harder time justifying its current prices.

For now, however, earnings are providing that support. The risk is that AI spending slows and earnings expectations follow. That'll be the most important factor over the next couple of quarters. But we have yet to see broad evidence that a slowdown is occurring.

If you're a long-term investor, I don't think recent events are a justification to alter your asset allocation.

The current pullback is large enough that it's a good opportunity to buy shares at a reasonable discount, especially considering that the macro backdrop hasn't really changed. There's fear about what could happen. But not much about what will change.

The market may or may not be at its short-term bottom here. But a 5% dip has been rare so far this year. And corrections have a recent history of reversing quickly. As long as corporate earnings continue growing, I think the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF remains a solid long-term buy.

David Dierking has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends FactSet Research Systems and Vanguard S&P 500 ETF. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3060. Breakfast News: The Buffett Moat Test

Does Amazon Web Services still have a moat? Test it against Buffett and Munger’s criteria with this week’s prompt.

Welcome to our Sunday Edition: Your weekly step toward better investing through AI.

Does Amazon Web Services still have a moat, and what would Warren Buffett and Charlie Munger make of it?

Amazon (AMZN -0.35%)’s AWS (Amazon Web Services) launched in 2006 and spent years as the clear leader in cloud computing. Artificial intelligence (AI) has made the field more crowded. Microsoft (MSFT -0.99%) sells access to OpenAI’s models through Azure, Alphabet (GOOG +0.78%) rents out its own TPU chips, and companies like CoreWeave (CRWV +6.28%) have built businesses renting Nvidia (NVDA +2.77%) GPUs by the hour. Meanwhile, all the big cloud providers are spending tens of billions of dollars a year on data centers.

For Amazon shareholders, that raises two questions. Are AWS’s advantages widening or narrowing? And is all that spending building a lasting edge, or is it simply the cost of staying in the race?

This week’s prompt looks at both. It starts with a standard industry analysis of the cloud market, then tests Amazon against the investing principles Warren Buffett and Charlie Munger laid out in decades of Berkshire Hathaway (BRKB -0.25%) shareholder letters and annual meetings. It doesn’t guess what either man would do. Munger died in 2023, and Buffett is no longer Berkshire’s CEO. It applies their written criteria, which are strict enough that few companies pass all of them.

If you own Amazon, or have been thinking about it, give the prompt below a try.

The cash flow question deserves the most attention. Buffett has written about his wariness of businesses that need heavy reinvestment just to hold their position. Whether AI data centers fit that description is still debated, and the prompt asks your assistant to back its answer with filings and figures.

AI Power Tip: Deep Research mode does noticeably better on Part 2, where the peer table needs fresh, segment-level figures from four different companies’ filings. Ask your assistant to show its sources for each cell.

This prompt is an educational tool, not a recommendation. The output is generated by your AI assistant, not by The Motley Fool, and may contain errors. Valuation figures in particular rest on assumptions your assistant chooses, so treat them as a way to see what the price implies, not as a target. Always verify key figures yourself.

You are an equity research analyst. Analyze Amazon’s competitive advantage, focusing on AWS as its hyperscale cloud business, with Amazon-wide context where it matters (e.g., retail scale, custom silicon, logistics, advertising cash flows). Your tone is inspired by The Motley Fool’s approachable tone and education focus but you are not The Motley Fool, you do not speak for it, your output is not Motley Fool research, and you do not provide financial advice.

PART 1: PORTER’S FIVE FORCES (hyperscale cloud market)

For each force, give a rating (Low / Medium / High), 2-3 evidence-backed points, and a one-line "so what" for Amazon:

- Bargaining power of suppliers (e.g., Nvidia/AMD, chips, power, data center land)

- Threat of substitutes (e.g., on-prem, private cloud, neoclouds like CoreWeave)

Identify Amazon’s top 3 hyperscaler competitors by latest reported cloud revenue (verify with the most recent quarter/fiscal year; likely Microsoft Azure, Google Cloud, and Alibaba Cloud, but confirm). Build a table comparing AWS to each on:

- Cloud revenue, YoY growth, and market share

- Operating margin (cloud segment where disclosed)

- Custom silicon (Trainium/Inferentia vs. Maia vs. TPU vs. others)

- AI model and platform strategy (e.g., Bedrock vs. Azure OpenAI vs. Vertex AI)

- Enterprise lock-in and ecosystem breadth

- Classify each of Amazon’s advantages as a durable moat or an eroding one, by type: scale economies, switching costs, network effects, cost position, or intangible assets.

- Name the biggest threat to AWS over the next 3-5 years.

- Give a bottom-line verdict in 3 sentences.

Evaluate Amazon through the documented investing principles of Warren Buffett and Charlie Munger (Berkshire shareholder letters, annual meetings, Poor Charlie’s Almanack). This is a framework, not a prediction of what either would actually do. Munger passed away in 2023 and Buffett is no longer Berkshire’s CEO, so frame it as "how their stated criteria would apply."

A. Four-filter test (Pass / Borderline / Fail, with a one-line justification each):

- Circle of competence: Is the business understandable and predictable over 10+ years?

- Durable moat: Does AWS earn high returns on capital that competitors can’t easily erode? Reference your Part 3 findings.

- Management: Capital allocation record, owner orientation, and candor.

B. Munger-style inversion: What could permanently impair this investment? Consider AI capex intensity (Buffett has historically disliked businesses that must reinvest heavily just to stay competitive), margin pressure from custom-silicon races, regulatory risk, and customer concentration in AI workloads. Does free cash flow, after the current capex surge, support the earnings story?

- Calculate owner earnings (net income + D&A − maintenance capex only). State your estimate of maintenance vs. growth capex and flag it as a judgment.

- Build a conservative DCF or owner-earnings-yield valuation with three scenarios (bear / base / bull), stating every key assumption (growth, margins, capex, discount rate, terminal value).

- (i) Estimated intrinsic value per share range

- (ii) A "would-buy-below" price using a 25-30% margin of safety on the base case

- (iii) The current share price (verify date) and the growth/margin expectations it implies (reverse DCF)

- (iv) Verdict: Below buy price / Fair value / Above fair value

D. Verdict in 3 sentences: Would a Buffett/Munger-style investor be interested, at what price, and what single fact would change the answer?

- Use the latest available data and cite sources with dates. Flag any figure that is estimated or not directly disclosed.

- Cite Buffett/Munger principles to specific, verifiable sources where possible. Do not fabricate quotes.

- Flag any statement about Berkshire’s actual Amazon holdings as needing verification against the latest 13F.

- Keep the total under 1,500 words, with the Part 2 table as the centerpiece.

- End with: "This is analysis, not investment advice."

You are inspired by an approachable, education-focused tone. You are not The Motley Fool, and you do not provide financial advice.

Clear disclosure: Every response must include this statement: "Educational only—this is not financial advice. Consider consulting a fiduciary for personal recommendations."

If the user changes their stated preferences or goals mid-conversation, acknowledge the change and adjust your response accordingly.

Never claim the result is a definitive measure of someone’s true risk tolerance.

Present any score or output as an educational framework based on the user’s answers.

Never provide personalized financial advice, buy/sell instructions, or specific securities.

If the user asks for personalized investment advice, restate the educational-only role and provide general educational ranges or concepts instead.

Look at where the four filters disagree. AWS could pass the moat test and fail on price, or look cheap on earnings while capital spending absorbs most of its free cash flow. The reverse DCF (discounted cash flow) in Part 4 is worth reading closely, too. It works backward from today’s share price to show what growth and margins investors are already assuming, which is easier to check than any single estimate of intrinsic value.

Rerun the prompt after each earnings report, since capital spending guidance, AWS growth, and segment margins change every quarter. You can also swap in Microsoft and Azure, or Alphabet and Google Cloud, to see how the other big providers do on the same tests.

Did your AI assistant’s read on Amazon’s moat match your own?

Discuss with friends and family, or become a member to hear what your fellow Fools are saying!

#3059. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."

#3056. Elon Musk Says Tesla Will End Cybertruck Production -- Here's Why

Car and Driver calls the Tesla Cybertruck "dead."

Tesla (TSLA +4.23%) CEO Elon Musk formally unveiled the Cybertruck in 2019. Actual production didn't begin until 2024. Next year, production will formally end, according to a new tweet from Musk.

Musk shared on social media that the electric vehicle (EV) maker will wind down "production of the Cybertruck at Gigafactory Texas around the middle of next year."

Why is the Cybertruck going extinct? Slow sales are largely to blame. According to estimates, Tesla is only selling a few thousand units per quarter. If the Cybertruck were a mass success like the Model 3 and Model Y, it likely would not be getting the axe.

Tesla CEO Elon Musk. Image source: The White House.

But there's another obvious reason that Tesla is winding down production for one of its least successful models ever.

Tesla's $1.4 trillion valuation isn't based on its success as an automaker. Instead, the market is pricing in plenty of growth for other opportunities, namely AI, humanoid robots, and self-driving cars. All of these initiatives will be capital-intensive to scale. Freeing up capital by ending a low-volume, low-potential model is a wise decision for Tesla as it pivots its priorities toward the future.

Just as importantly, Tesla will then be able to use the factory space to focus on Cybercab production. Musk said so directly, tweeting that ceasing Cybertruck production is designed specifically "to make room for increased Cybercab production."

Some experts believe that the robotaxi market will eventually be valued at $8 trillion to $10 trillion globally. Success in that market can move the needle for Tesla in a way Cybertruck demand never could.

Ryan Vanzo has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Tesla. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.
